vice-president of the commission. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i thank all those who have spoken. however, i feel obliged to repeat some of the information in more detail because some of those who have spoken have clearly shown that they do not know - i say this with the same frankness that you have shown me - what the commission is doing. i would like to reiterate - and i do not think there can be any misunderstanding - that we are talking about homophobia, which is a violation of human rights, and that is the only point on which i agree with mr agnoletto. homophobia is not a matter of defending certain categories of people, but is a fundamental problem of absolute rights, which can never be interpreted differently. i would like to make a few points, however. some people have asked - most recently mr bozkurt - whether or not monitoring of the protection of homosexuals and the fight against homophobia in the member states will be guaranteed. i can assure you, mr bozkurt, that this monitoring will not only be guaranteed, but has already been carried out! you should know the report drawn up a few weeks ago by a group of independent experts - which i have of course made available to parliament - which sets out for each country what behaviour is contrary to our common will to fight discrimination. that report has been formally transmitted to parliament and it is obvious that the reason why i, and not others, thought of taking such a country-by-country action was simply to put the facts at your disposal and at our disposal so that we can make our judgments. mr bozkurt, that monitoring will continue: all acts contrary to the spirit that animates us will be publicly reported. and governments too, who are called upon to take the first step and to remember what the national, and not just european, rules are on freedom of assembly, for example, can draw all the necessary conclusions from reading the relevant documents. transparent information is a priority. other speakers mentioned a promise by president barroso to adopt a cross-cutting directive - that is the term used - on strengthening and respecting rights in the context of non-discrimination. ladies and gentlemen, that too has been done! in 2005, the commission adopted a formal communication entitled 'non-discrimination and equal opportunities and rights for all' which sets out clearly what is expected in terms of ensuring greater protection of equal rights. in the communication to the european parliament we were obliged to remind everyone of what everyone knows, namely that when the commission makes a proposal based on article 13, the proposal - according to the statute, according to the treaties and not according to our interpretation - must be approved unanimously by the council. and today there is still no unanimity in the council, which i hope can be achieved. in any case, that 2005 communication states very clearly our intention to align the field of legal protection against all forms of discrimination, including discrimination based on sexual orientation, which is what some of you say we have not yet done, ladies and gentlemen. i now turn to another subject, the powers of the european commission. we believe that the european commission does not currently have the powers to deal with violations of a right which affects people deeply, as you rightly pointed out, mr cashman. but it is precisely to strengthen europe's powers that once again this commission and its speakers have proposed the establishment of a european agency for the protection of fundamental rights. if you accept that proposal and if there is an agreement in june, that agency, with the powers you propose - since it is for the european parliament to give an opinion on the matter - and perhaps with increased powers of interference - will be an instrument precisely designed to tackle discriminatory behaviour. it is a proposal which is already on the table, it only needs to be examined, and i have said and repeated that we will listen to parliament's requests to improve it. but this is only one of the tools available. then there are, of course, the ordinary instruments, such as article 226 of the treaty, the infringement procedures and also a wide-ranging case law of the court of justice, according to which, unfortunately, if actions are within the competence of the member states, the commission has no power to intervene. i hope that the agency will be set up soon, because that will perhaps be the first instrument to meet the need for such powers. mr bachelot, you speak of a right to diversity and i reply to you once again because we have already been active on this subject. you should know, i think, the program for an information campaign that is exactly called "for diversity". with this programme, which started at the end of 2003 and is still ongoing, we set out to collect positive information to explain that diversity is a value for europe. all the major european associations defending the rights of homosexuals participate in this programme. i believe that you are aware of all these activities and we intend to continue to work in this area, so i would like to dismiss the allegations that the commission has done nothing. we intend to continue our work on this and we intend to do so in a spirit of mutual frankness. i thought i would give you facts, not just ideas, about things that are already on the ground and that will improve our determined fight against homophobia.
the junilist believes that the eu is a union of values which should operate in accordance with the principle of equal value and rights for all people. this principle is fundamental to all our activities and is also enshrined in the un universal declaration of human rights. eu member states have ratified a number of legally binding international agreements based on this principle of equal treatment and non-discrimination. the right not to be subject to unfair discrimination on grounds of sexual orientation is thus a fundamental human right. in the debate, this right is sometimes seen as a negotiable privilege. it is important that we always bear in mind the un declarations on human rights when we consider legislative proposals in this house. only in recent years have eu member states begun to seriously address the issue of homophobia. according to swedish statistics, the general citizen is at risk of 4-6% exposure to so-called unprovoked violence. among lesbian and bisexual women, the corresponding figures are between 15 and 24%. among gay and bisexual men, between 28-36% have experienced unprovoked violence. the fact that sexual orientation determines the degree to which a person is at risk of being subjected to violence is of course unacceptable. it is important that we as politicians make it clear that all people have equal rights and obligations. if we do not do this, we are questioning the un declarations and fundamental human rights.
the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs has tabled an opinion. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. commissioner frattini, you are here. it is good that parliament is debating today the issue of trafficking in women and children. when the libe committee, of which i was rapporteur, adopted my opinion, it was by a very large majority in favour of a strong rejection of the abhorrent trafficking in human beings, but also in favour of seeing human trafficking as part of prostitution in the eu. what was important for libe was to set a target of halving trade within ten years and to make a great effort to give us parliamentarians better decision-making information, statistics and, of course, cooperation between the police, the prosecutor and the judiciary. we believe, i emphasize believe, that up to half of the victims of human trafficking may be children and minors. it was therefore important for libe to point out that 18 years must be made an age limit that cannot be broken. it is also natural for me that women and children who have been victims of trafficking should be given the right to stay or help to return. i also hope that in the future there will be cooperation with the tourist industry. countries and municipalities with large groups of tourists must make it clear that they do not accept trafficking in women and children. a natural continuation of the decision on the tourist industry which we are going to take here in strasbourg is what the social democratic women of the european union are protesting against, namely the rumors that thousands and thousands of women will be imported into prostitution for the football world cup this summer. i am happy to say that in sweden both our eu minister and the minister for gender equality, together with the president of the football association, have distanced themselves from this. i hope that all the speakers here today can give their support to this protest against trafficking in women. sports and sex trafficking and prostitution are not compatible. the committee on women's rights and gender equality wanted to go further than the committee on gender equality in its ambition to make prostitution in the eu visible. i intend to return to this question at a later date, for example when we are dealing with the communication from commissioner frattini. human trafficking cannot be reported off as christa prets believes. it requires courageous politicians, including in the eu, to take responsibility.
madam president, there are always three sides to the trafficking of human beings: the victims, the organised crime networks and the demand side. the customers, or ‘clients’, are predominantly males who feel that they have a right to buy women, or rather, girls. should we grant them this right without limits? a recent comprehensive study commissioned by the committee on women’s rights and gender equality and conducted by the italian-based transcrime institute, shows that this restrictive view on the demand side has a decisive impact on the number of victims of trafficking. i am pleased to say that finland is about to adopt a law similar to the one in force in sweden, criminalising the buyers of sex. the prets report which we are now discussing is weak in that respect. i know it will take some time before a majority of member states have criminalised the buying of sex, but is it not time that we criminalise the buying of slaves and criminalise the men who buy the victims of trafficking? i will end this speech in the same way i have promised to every time i speak in this house: ceterum censeo prostitutionem esse dilendam – ‘i therefore maintain that prostitution must be abolished’.
mr president, i would like to thank you. peter mandelson, after the wto meeting you said that the outcome must be a good compromise because most people are dissatisfied. that is a very clever statement, and if it were a question of budgetary negotiations, a zero-sum game in which concessions actually cost money, you would be right. now the situation is just the opposite. in trade negotiations, you gain as much from the tariffs you remove yourself as from those you force others to remove. it's not a zero-sum game. even if the meeting didn't turn out to be the breakdown that many feared, i think we should be honest and say that it's a failure. the problems were deferred to the future and brought closer to a breakdown, and in important parts of the industrial and services sections i even think that the meeting produced minor deteriorations of the text. i see two reactions that are worrying. on the other hand, there is a reluctance to engage in multilateral negotiations, a desire to focus on regional and bilateral agreements instead. that would be a big mistake. they will never produce sufficient results to liberalize world trade in significant areas. it is an easy way of avoiding the dismantling of one's own protectionism. on the one hand, i hear the attitude that "we did our part in hong kong, it is the fault of others that we are standing still". i think that attitude is a recipe for failure. it is equally good to realize that success in negotiations is not based on the ability to point out the protectionism of others, it is only to see the speck in your brother's eye without noticing the beam in your own. instead, success is driven by the realization of the costs of its own protectionism. for europe, the hong kong meeting should be a wake-up call, not just to the obvious - that our agricultural protectionism is costing us far more important liberalisations of industrial goods and services. above all, we must join the united states, japan and others in recognising that the costs of our agricultural protectionism to our consumers and taxpayers are reason enough to abolish it. "free trade, one of the greatest blessings a government can bestow upon its people, is unpopular in almost all countries", said thomas babington as early as 1824. the wto meeting in hong kong shows that the same could have been said today. we all, as consumers and workers, are paying the price for the shortcomings of trade liberalisation around the world.
mr president, i would like to thank you. stavros dimas, you have the most important task of all commissioners. if you fail, the commission fails. you are the guide for josé manuel barroso. it's time we realize that we're facing a climate chaos. you can't negotiate with mother earth. we are guests in her house and we must follow her rules. that is why we must reduce emissions quickly. we have to impose carbon taxes on products from countries that do not comply with kyoto. it would create pressure for better policies in the us and australia. countries that don't take responsibility for the climate should not be allowed to parasitize our planet. the current economic and trade policies are failing and creating climate chaos and destruction instead of happiness and prosperity. it's urgent to settle this. we need trade rules that follow the rules of the earth, not short-term lobbyists. you can't sue against the climate chaos. we need carbon taxes on imports, air taxes, better regulations on car emissions, better transport policy and better trade policy. then we can take care of our land first.
mr president, i would like to thank you. there are several good reasons for rejecting the port directive. firstly, this is a matter for each member state to decide in accordance with the eu's subsidiarity principle. it is up to the member states to decide on this type of issue. our basic view is that swedish rules should apply on swedish territory. secondly, the process which has led to the return of the ports directive to the agenda is undemocratic. the european parliament rejected an identical proposal from the commission less than a year ago. thirdly, the operators concerned by this directive are largely opposed to it. there is now a well-functioning competition between europe's ports. the port directive distorts competition by allowing the shipping companies' own staff to take over the loading and unloading of cargo. who really wants this directive? the main proponent is the commission, which wants to push through this legislation at all costs. the swedish junilista will say no to this legislative proposal.
mr president, i would like to thank you. it is a mystery to me that the european commission has put forward this proposal again. we hear the great resistance it is facing and commissioner jacques barrot now has an opportunity to show that the eu is keeping pace with the citizens by withdrawing this proposal. i think there are three arguments for doing that. first of all, we do not need the directive. it works well in many ports in europe - i know this from my own country, sweden, among others. the ports are developing and there are many flexible solutions. what we need instead is a directive on competition between ports, something which we in the socialist group have also pointed out. this commission proposal is a european central bank. it is not the liberalisation of ports that is being encouraged. it is a european central regulation which we do not need because it does not lead to the development of ports, but which on the contrary can hamper their development. the european commission has therefore unfortunately not put forward this proposal out of concern for ports and transport. these central rules make port operations more complex. the development of ports and transport systems will be more difficult, as ports are key transport nodes and combined terminals that must function well for the entire transport system to do so. let regional and local actors develop the ports, that is the best. finally, this port directive would be bad for the employees of ports. it is the port workers who would pay the price for the central regulation proposed by the commission. it is likely that many jobs would be lost and a considerable amount of activity would be transferred to the recruitment industry, with the result that conditions and security for employees would be worse. it is for many good reasons that i am therefore proposing that parliament reject this proposal.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the fundamental question in this debate is whether competition and new services are good in this area as in all other areas. it is about whether things are worse for consumers, workers and businesses if you have competition. i think that the commission should be able to take a more positive approach to the problem of the environment, and that is why we are voting against the report. we can see from the european societies that where competition and new business have been opened up, this has led to the development of services, the development of activities, lower prices and better service to the consumer. we have seen it in telecommunications, in aviation, in area after area, and we have heard the same warning argument before these deregulations have been implemented. i remember when telecommunications deregulation was once discussed, how it was seriously claimed that telephony would not be possible and that those working in that industry would lose their jobs. in fact, more jobs have been created. in the port area, which is so crucial for trade and development within the union, it is self-evident that the dynamics of increased market entry and competition both between and within ports will lead to the development of services. it is crucial not only for the better functioning of port operations as such but also for the better functioning of shipping, transport and trade. it is about the whole european goods flow, but also about the opportunities for new regions to emerge new ports and new structures in our economy. if we want competition, we will also see new developments and change. if you say that everything is perfect as it is today, that our ports all over europe are working just as they should, then nobody has to be afraid of competition. if we get new businesses and new opportunities, things will happen. that is why the fundamental question is whether we want to see a more dynamic flow of goods in europe. i want to.
in writing. the report aims to strengthen citizenship of the union by decoupling it from national citizenship and by requiring harmonised rules for the granting of national citizenship. on the contrary, we believe that citizenship is one of the cornerstones of the sovereignty of the nation states, and that each member state must be free to decide on it independently. there may be arguments for reforming the rules on the acquisition of citizenship after a long period of permanent residence in the country, the right to vote and to stand for election in municipal, regional and national elections. however, the question of citizenship in the member states is exclusively a matter for the countries concerned in accordance with their own legislation. it is for each member state to determine the conditions for the acquisition and loss of nationality and the effects of nationality. the european union has no competence in this respect. citizenship of the union is thus subordinate to national citizenship and is thus to be defined by national laws on citizenship. the report also contains a number of articles on the introduction of a european tax, the uniform european party lists for european parliament elections, the importance of an eu constitution for strengthening european cohesion and common starting points for the integration of the european dimension in the curricula of all schools.
in writing. it is a human right to be able to participate actively in the democratic process and it is something we are working hard to make real for everyone. but the eu's use of this as a lever to strengthen its power ambitions to take control of the democratic structures of its members is not justifiable.
in writing. i voted against the alde group on paragraph 46 concerning criminalizing the clients who deliberately exploit persons in a situation of coercion. whereas the alde group line was to vote no to this i believe that people that deliberately buy women/girls who are forced into prostitution should be seen as committing a crime similar to the crime of buying stolen goods - i.e. fencing. and i think even the member states that today are against criminalizing the buying of sex could introduce this as a criminal offence.
in writing. we have voted in favour of the report on trafficking in women and children. we believe that the security of citizens is best protected by joint action against crossborder crime, in particular crime against the fundamental freedoms and rights of persons and against human dignity. the moderate delegation also wishes to emphasise that, in order to give legal effect to the demands made and thus highlight the importance of the issue, the issue of trafficking should be dealt with in the european parliament's committee on legal affairs and not in the committee on women's rights. we are opposed to proposals in the report which concern the areas of competence of the member states, such as the organisation of the police.
in writing. the own-initiative report deals with important issues and the junilista supports many of the basic ideas which permeate the report. sexual exploitation and trafficking in human beings must be combated. furthermore, the serious crime that often follows from cross-border trafficking in human beings cannot be ignored. it is therefore of the utmost importance to facilitate cooperation and the exchange of information within the eu. the junilista group, however, believes that each member state must be allowed to choose its own method and approach, including in these matters. the junilista therefore chooses to vote against the own-initiative report.
in writing. combating trafficking in human beings and the demand for these services, i.e. sex workers, are interrelated, but criminalising this type of crime at eu level could lead to penalties that are not adapted to the internal logic of national systems and could also lead to the criminalisation of sex workers being banned.
mr president, the recent crisis between russia and ukraine over gas supply has shown that it is more important than ever to have a european energy market that is less dependent on imports. this will provide not only enormous opportunities for the european energy industry, including renewables, but also a window of opportunity for tackling climate change, boosting growth and creating new jobs. in order to achieve this we want more transparency, better regulation, a renovated and functioning grid, a european energy strategy, debate on self-sufficiency and solidarity between member states, and rules that enable consumers to decide on their own consumption in a workable way. we therefore welcome the green paper as well as the presidency’s programme. there is much to be done, but i think we can do it.
i thank the commissioner for his reply and i am pleased that the commission will cooperate with the employment committee as we now deal with the issue in an own-initiative report introduced on the posting directive. it has become clear - not least from the messages sent by the commission about the various signals in the vaxholm case - that the posting directive is not at all as clear as one might wish. the idea is that posted workers should be able to work under conditions that are broadly similar to those of the country in which they are posted. i ask the question: is this directive adapted to all labour market models in the eu or only to those labour market models which have legislation and universally recognised collective agreements? do you think it is in keeping with the scandinavian model of non-universal collective agreements, where each company is negotiated with? or is a change needed that also includes the labour market model?
member of the commission. the commission’s interpretation of the ruling of the ecj on the marks & spencer case is fully in compliance with the ecj’s ruling. the problem in that case was that while united kingdom law permits loss relief inside the united kingdom market, the same is not permitted in the case of other companies or branches of marks & spencer in different member states – one in germany and one in belgium. the ecj ruling, in compliance with the philosophy of the internal market, was in favour of marks & spencer and the commission fully supported that. the commission wants to study carefully the budgetary consequences of such rulings and try to take the initiative to have better coordination within the tax policy of the member states: if not harmonisation, at least coordination is needed in order to avoid such problems. to conclude, i want to refer to the first question concerning the common consolidated corporate tax base, which would solve the problem. the difficulty is that, as i have already said, in the best case, if everything goes smoothly and well, we will come up with the legislative proposal no earlier than 2008, so we still have two years in which to provide some temporary solution.
member of the commission. the sector study referred to by the honourable member was commissioned by the commission in 2004 from wik consult in order to assist ongoing work on the gradual opening-up to competition of the european postal market. the views expressed in the study are those of the consultant. the study is merely one of several elements that the commission is considering in looking at various options and their possible consequences. in addition to the regular consultation with stakeholders and notably an online public consultation currently under way, the study actually serves as a platform to engage in a transparent and open debate on the conditions for enabling the further phasing-in of competition in the postal market. it is the understanding of the commission that the approach taken by the consultancy on the issue of the uniform tariff and the frequency of delivery was an attempt to give more flexibility to the public postal operator in order to sustain a viable and efficient universal service in the long term. the current postal directive 97/67/ec, as amended by directive 2002/39/ec, does not impose a uniform tariff but requires a minimum frequency of delivery of five days a week. the commission can assure the honourable member that on all aspects of the postal reform in the european union, including both the issues mentioned, it will try its best to strike the right balance between the necessary degree of harmonisation at eu level and those conditions to be fixed at member state level, which would reflect the very different characteristics of each national postal market.

member of the commission. the point raised by the honourable member will form part of our thinking in whatever proposals are put forward by the end of 2006. and, yes, i am very conscious of the importance of a universal service and particularly the importance of such a service to rural parts of europe.
member of the commission. article 23 of the proposal for a directive on services in the internal market does, as the honourable member says in her question, codify existing rights to patient mobility. the case law of the court of justice has established that, subject to certain conditions, patients have the right to have the cost of healthcare received in other member states assumed by the social security system in their own member state. patients are, without prior authorisation, entitled to get reimbursement for non-hospital care received in other member states. for hospital care, prior authorisation may be required before a patient can go abroad to receive health care. authorisations must be granted when the necessary care cannot be given in the patient’s member state within a medically justifiable time limit. before the proposal for a services directive was tabled, the commission carried out a study on the implementation of the rights of patients in the member states. the conclusion was that ‘european citizens are encountering unjustified or disproportionate obstacles when they apply for reimbursement, from their member state of residence, of costs for non-hospital treatment incurred in another member state, or for authorisation for assumption of the costs in the case of hospital treatment’. for an individual citizen, the right to receive treatment in other member states and to have the cost covered by his or her own social security system is probably one of the most important benefits of the internal market. these are the reasons for the commission to propose the inclusion of health services in the scope of the services directive and for proposing a specific provision on the assumption of health care costs incurred in other member states. the commission has also been actively involved in the work of the high-level group on cross-border healthcare and patient mobility with a view to establishing how best the framework for guaranteeing and promoting patient rights in the single market can be developed. this work has shown the clear benefits of cooperation between member states on health and healthcare related issues, of which the reimbursement of costs is just one aspect. a number of member states are concerned about the effects cross-border care and patient mobility may have on their national health budgets. in the absence of a clear legal framework, we will continue to be confronted with developing ecj case law. pending the vote in this house on the first reading of the proposed services directive, it would be inappropriate to speculate on the position on certain articles, but i can confirm that where member states impose disproportionate or unjustified restrictions, the commission will act, including by launching infringement procedures.
member of the commission. as i said in my reply, one of the reasons for the inclusion of health services in the scope of the services directive was to deal with some of the issues raised by the honourable member. however, as the honourable member will be aware, the debate on the services directive in the committee on the internal market and consumer protection and in other committees has thrown up a number of other issues, and the internal market committee voted in a particular direction. at next month’s part-session, the first reading of the services directive will come before the house and amendments will be debated. therefore, there are many conflicting viewpoints among parliamentarians on this issue and there are conflicting viewpoints among member state governments on the scope of the services directive to include healthcare costs. as i have said on this issue and on any others relating to the services directive, i remain open to hearing the concerns and opinions of all stakeholders.

for the gue/ngl group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. the united left group supports all the amendments proposed by the committee on the environment to this regulation on the application of the aarhus convention by the eu. two points are of particular importance to us. firstly, it is essential that the decision be comprehensive so that projects financed by the european union are also covered by the regulatory framework. the european investment bank must also be subject to the rules. over the years, the eib has been widely criticised for its approach to environmental issues, for its lack of environmental consideration and for its extreme closeness. secondly, we want environmental organisations to have a real right to go to court when environmental rules are not being respected. we are therefore opposed to any restriction of this right, such as that proposed by the rapporteur in amendments nos 28 and 30. in practice, this would mean that many of the most important national environmental movements would be excluded from the possibility of going to court, and that is unacceptable to us.
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. there is a very tangible and concrete background to this directive which we are now going to decide on. these are the major disasters caused by the collapse of mining dams a few years ago, particularly in romania and spain. these were environmental disasters that had enormous consequences for the surrounding environment, and also poisoned the water systems. in addition to this, we have a constant environmental problem, with waste from existing and old plants leaching heavy metals and other toxic substances into our environment. this calls for a specific directive to regulate waste from the mining and other extractive industries. when we look at the proposal that the commission once put forward a few years ago and at what we are going to decide on tomorrow, there are significant differences. parliament has certainly contributed to making this directive better and stronger. a number of parliament's amendments were adopted at first reading and more are being added to this reading. i would like to highlight some specific points in this report which are particularly important. firstly, the creation of dust safety rules to prevent such disasters in the future. the high concentration of cyanide used in romania is also prohibited by this directive. there will be clear rules on permits and how they are to be issued, and it will be possible to see which environmental problems can be addressed. in addition, an absolute obligation to respect the water directive is implemented, which means that the water status must not be degraded. in addition, there are rules to restore the site where the mining takes place. the time of leaving behind waste and destroyed sites shall end with this directive. to this end, a financial guarantee is introduced, to which companies must commit funds to restore the site where they have mined. as regards the new member states, especially romania but also bulgaria, we have reached a declaration from these countries during the conciliation process in which they undertake not to seek exemptions but to respect all the rules of the directive. this is important, not least because romania has significant problems in this area. as regards historical waste, member states are required to carry out an inventory, which means that they must look for historical waste which causes environmental problems. overall, the environmental situation in the union will improve significantly. the arguments against this have been mainly economic, that it costs companies. but it's important to realize that environmental costs, restoring the environment, are part of the cost of production and they should be. if they are not, then the unserious players benefit. this directive is in favour of the mining industry which is serious and takes responsibility for its environmental policy. this directive will also promote the development of new environmental technologies that will enable the recycling of historical waste, and this offers an opportunity for european industry in this field. last but not least, and most importantly, it will benefit the environment and the health of those living in these areas. but of course this directive is not perfect, directives are very rarely perfect. there are some things i'd like to see even stronger. the rules for non-hazardous waste are weaker than they should be. the reason for this is that the council made deteriorations at first reading. i did not bring the decision i wanted from this parliament to negotiate these deteriorations away. as regards waste facilities, the definition is too weak and too generous to industry, which risks creating certain loopholes. as regards historical waste, there are no clear requirements for action against it. however, it is important to note that other union environmental legislation, in particular the water framework directive, imposes an absolute obligation to address cases where historical waste is seen to lead to a deterioration in the status of water. this means that the directive as a whole is still good. we can be satisfied with the work we have accomplished during these years. finally, i would also like to thank those with whom i have worked, the shadow rapporteurs of the various groups with whom i have had a good cooperation. i would like to thank the european commission officials who have been excellent partners this time and the presidency for the very good conciliation. it has been easy to work with them and to find concrete solutions to the various problems we faced.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the commission is not prepared to accept the amendments. "it takes a whole new way of thinking to solve the problems we have created with the old way of thinking", said albert einstein, whom we have discussed before. i therefore have a few specific questions. what concrete action does the presidency intend to take in favour of smes? you say you're going to create jobs through energy policy. can you explain how? will you support voluntary or mandatory targets for alternative energy and biomass? that is also a very important question. as you know, it's easier to fight for your principles than to live up to them. you talk a lot about the environment and consumer power. i would therefore like to ask you, with regard to the chemicals directive: will you fight for the replacement of the most dangerous chemicals and improve the council agreement reached in december? will you add the right of the consumer to know what dangerous chemicals are in the product? finally, i would like to thank you for a very good meeting in vienna last week and wish you good luck with the presidency.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would say to the president of the council and the president of the commission: let us stop talking about the lisbon process as a process in itself. let us instead talk about what we have done and what we are going to do to achieve its requirements and its objectives, namely to achieve european competitiveness. otherwise all references to the lisbon process will become an alibi for what is not being done. let's talk about what we're actually gonna do. implement the services directive. this is the most concrete thing we can do to strengthen europe's competitiveness. make sure that it is based on respect for the diversity of europe, taking advantage of the competitiveness of each country. it is not only the most important measure to strengthen european competitiveness, it is also the most important thing we can do to achieve the reunification of europe and to ensure that all european countries can grow and meet across borders. ensure that the long-term budget in the negotiations with parliament is characterised by an additional priority for research and development. it is one of the individual actions we can take that will make the greatest contribution to europe's becoming a leading knowledge society. to ensure that europe can act with unity and strength in its policy towards iran, which is today a country pursuing a policy which is not acceptable to the international community or in accordance with the requirements that must be imposed on each country. a country which threatens to annihilate another country, which covertly develops nuclear fuel technology and which supports terrorism must be met by a coherent and common european policy in alliance with our international partners. these are essentially european data which show why european cooperation is needed. the council and the commission have a major responsibility to ensure that policy is marked by concrete action to implement this.
in writing. we are seeking to reach an agreement with the council on a new interinstitutional agreement on budgetary discipline and improvement of the budgetary procedure. the negotiations are based on the european parliament's resolution of 8 june 2005 on the political challenges and budgetary means in an enlarged union 2007-13. we therefore support the european parliament's resolution on the european council's common position of 15 and 16 december 2005. however, we cannot support the idea of an improved european dimension in the eu's agricultural policy. we look forward to the review of eu expenditure and revenue in 2008 and hope that it will lead to a more modern structure of the eu budget and reduced expenditure on the cap.
in writing. we are not satisfied with the agreement reached in the council on 16 december. we want to reduce expenditure on agricultural policy and the structural funds and to increase the resources for research and development and for the common foreign and security policy. parliament has a say in the financial perspective and has announced that it will push its own budget line very hard. we do not support the expansionary budget line which parliament is advocating, but we do support other proposals such as increasing the appropriations for research and development. in view of this, we abstained in the final vote on the resolution and did not vote against it because we can support parts of parliament's proposal.
in writing. i have today chosen to abstain when the european parliament voted on its resolution on the financial perspective. in the resolution, the european parliament rejects the council's agreement. i am very disappointed, to be sure, by the council's agreement, which means that my priority areas will receive less resources than the commission has proposed. this applies to areas such as research, innovation, measures to combat crossborder crime, the structural funds to help smes and environmental measures. but the council has been taking a disgraceful long time and i do not want to delay this process any further. it is important that we secure the funding of projects and programmes for the years 2007-2013 so that they do not have to be cancelled because of uncertainty and lack of co-financing. this is important not least for the new ten member states. that is why i have decided not to stop the financial perspective that has now been agreed.
in writing. the resolution rejects the council's agreement on the financial perspective. however, i think it is unwise for the european parliament to reject the proposal and have therefore chosen to abstain. the road to council agreement has been long and difficult and it would have been problematic if the council had not finally succeeded in reaching agreement on the matter. the european parliament certainly has a say in this matter and has the right to reject the council's proposal, but it would be unfortunate if the long-term budget did not come into being because of power games between the institutions. parliament must now take up its responsibilities and, together with the council, do its utmost to arrive at a long-term budget. i think it is good that the council agreed on the financial perspective and that the level of expenditure was not too high, but at the same time i am critical of much of the content of the budget. for example, the budget has the wrong profile, with too much money being spent on agriculture and regional aid. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competition' in the context of the directive on the protection of consumers. that is why i do not support parliament's position. a firm prioritisation of eu resources is crucial to bringing about the changes europe needs.
in writing. of course, the eu should not have a single bathing water quality directive. we have voted in favour of the report because parliament has listened to our criticisms and is reducing the regulation we have now. the report also strongly rejects the proposals for further regulation which were put forward when parliament last dealt with the matter. it should be noted that the previous proposal clearly showed the difficulty of detailed rules which would apply in completely different circumstances. in countries with long coasts, large water areas, short bathing seasons, low water temperatures and low population density, requirements should be different from those in countries with densely populated beaches where many people travel to bathe in warm water for long periods of time. we moderates are glad that our criticism has been heard.
in writing. i have today voted in favour of the conciliation committee's joint text for a european parliament and council directive on bathing water quality management. my point of departure is that this is a matter of subsidiarity, that is to say, it should be dealt with at local or regional level in this case. bathing water is a matter of concern for many swedish municipalities, which have a huge number of bathing sites. it is therefore important that the directive does not burden municipalities with more bureaucracy. the reason i have said yes, after all, is that this directive is a great improvement on the 1976 bathing water directive which is still in force. it is a simplification and an improvement in health and the environment. a narrower but more coherent eu needs to consider whether issues such as this should not be brought back to the member states to be dealt with at national or local/regional level.
in writing. this report deals with an issue which should not be on the eu agenda. of course, it is not possible to compare the conditions and conditions of bathing in the mediterranean sea with those of thousands of lakes in countries such as sweden and finland. the bathing water directive is an example of the eu's rarely applying either the principle of subsidiarity or the principle of proportionality in practice. this is a regrettable fact. the proposal resulting from the conciliation procedure has certain advantages over the current directive. for example, the number of criteria to be applied by the member states is reduced. at the same time, new absurd demands are being made, such as the introduction of eu-harmonised signs at bathing sites in the eu. we are confident that the member states can deal with bathing water quality issues independently. we are therefore opposed in principle to the bathing water directive and have voted against the compromise reached by the conciliation committee.
in writing. i have voted in favour of the conciliation committee's joint text for a european parliament and council directive on bathing water quality management. in principle, i do not think that bathing water quality is an issue that should be dealt with at eu level, but by member states at national or local level. the reason for my vote in favour is that the proposal contains significant simplifications of an earlier 1976 directive on bathing water quality. voting in favour of this proposal is part of the fight to simplify eu legislation. at the same time, of course, it also means changes in the right direction in terms of health and the environment. to make the eu more effective and proactive in the major crossborder issues, it is necessary to delegate certain policies to the member states. this is one of those questions, it must be closer to the citizens.
in writing. we believe that the aarhus convention is a valuable legal instrument to guarantee public access to environmental information and participation in decision-making processes. the purpose of the convention is therefore positive. we have voted against amendment no 25. in principle, we believe that the action should be brought before a national court applying the law in force.
in writing. the ports directive is an example of redundant eu legislation. we voted against the report and we are in favour of rejecting the commission's proposal. we chose to support the amendments which seek to limit the scope of the directive. we therefore voted in favour of proposals to exclude piloting services and self-handling from the scope of the directive. we are critical of the ports directive for several reasons: the ports directive takes neither the subsidiarity nor the proportionality principle into account. political decisions on loading, unloading and self-handling shall be taken at national level; the proposal for a directive means that staff performing the same job can belong to different trade union organisations. this creates disruption and disrupts the order and peace of the industry; the harbour directive will hardly promote competition. the ports are companies that compete with each other. the ports directive is an attempt to force them to accept that other companies can take over parts of their business against their will. it's not reasonable. nobody would think of forcing volvo to accept subcontractors they don't want. competition between port operators is positive and leads to more efficient solutions. if a large company in another industry is not efficient and customer friendly, it will be outcompeted. this is also the case in the companies that make up europe's ports.
in writing. we support the development of afghanistan towards peace, stability and democracy after the fall of the taliban regime. but at the same time we are concerned about the eu's increasingly strong military role in the country. afghanistan is not part of the eu's immediate neighbourhood, and therefore it is not the eu's task to place troops in the country. instead, it is the task of individual countries/defence organisations under a un mandate. the world organization should be better equipped to take military action in trouble spots around the world. in view of the above, we abstain from voting on the final resolution, but we support the proposals on the protection of human rights.
in writing. global climate change is a major problem that has good reason to be addressed at international level. the commission is also proposing that the eu should be able to take on board the new eu-wide framework for the protection of the environment and the environment. this type of call can be made by the respective national government independently. we also believe that the member states should be able to take a national position in the context of negotiations on international climate issues. however, we assume that all parties will comply with the agreements reached. we are critical of the proposal to establish a communication strategy at eu level to inform citizens about climate issues. eu cooperation is based on trust between member states. we are convinced that the member states are capable of taking the necessary measures to ensure that the public has access to appropriate information on climate issues. the position expressed in this explanation of vote is our position of principle on this matter. the explanation of vote is thus relevant to future cases of a similar nature.
in writing. this report addresses a number of important issues in the field of sustainable development. the junilist believes that there are good reasons for eu cooperation on cross-border environmental issues. the report does, however, address a number of key policy areas which should be outside the eu's remit. examples include: what investments are to be made in public transport and road network expansion in the member states; debt cancellations to developing countries; environmental taxes at eu level; whether the environment should be a subject of primary education in member states' schools; taxation of labour in the member states; employment and social inclusion we are strongly critical of the fact that the eu institutions have a greedy appetite for increased influence. we have therefore voted against the whole, but we are in favour of individual paragraphs, especially those dealing with genuinely transnational environmental problems.

mr president, i would like to thank you. this is a very good and important report on one of the most important issues for the future of the european union. what relations should we have with our neighbouring countries? how can we strengthen cooperation on issues where we must cooperate, such as the fight against terrorism, environmental problems, security and energy? how can we help to strengthen the rule of law and democracy in the countries around us? i believe that we should not be defining the borders of europe at the moment. the countries of europe that can, will and meet all the criteria should be given a clear membership perspective. we have seen what this has meant for the transformation of our new member states. eu membership has worked for them like a whip and a carrot and it has been very, very important in the transformation there. this means a great deal for the democratization and reform process in the balkans and in turkey. that is why i believe that we should give ukraine a membership perspective. of course, the eu cannot be just any size, and then neighbourhood policy can be an option, but then it must be an attractive alternative to membership. the message from parliament is clear on this point. we need to further define and define what we are going to do with the policy. what are our priorities? can we solve the institutional issues that will arise with these countries? there is a need for common values and common policies. furthermore, cooperation agreements must be tailored to each individual need. as has been pointed out, countries look very different. the focus must be on the democratic issues. the transition fund or the democracy fund proposed by charles tannock is a very good idea. i would like to thank mr tannock again for a very good report.
mr president, i would like to thank you. commissioner, what is it? first of all, i would like to say that the report by charles tannock on the european neighbourhood policy is a document which i fully support and i can only congratulate him on the excellent work he has done. his report shows that he is well acquainted with the history of europe and the conditions that prevail today. only those who know the past can also answer the challenges of tomorrow. however, the conditions for his work have not always been easy. the european neighbourhood policy now covers both european countries eligible to apply for membership and to become members of the eu, and non-european countries that cannot become members of the eu. in my personal opinion, the neighbourhood policy should only apply to countries neighbouring europe. european countries seeking membership should not be seen as neighbours but as potential eu members and should see a clear eu perspective. moreover, the debate on these issues has been fluctuating considerably in recent times. when we talk about the european neighbourhood policy, we are talking about a long-term perspective. a short-term perspective is often lacking. does this mean that opinion both inside and outside parliament has changed, or do we lack political courage? for example, a year ago the opinion on ukraine was that ukraine had a very clear eu perspective and was without any doubt part of the european family. we all remember the many orange scarves that were seen in this plenary in support of the democratization process in ukraine. if the question of the status of the negotiations for ukraine's membership had been raised then, i am quite convinced that there would not have been so many who would have opposed this. today the situation is different. we are still telling ukraine that we want it to be a member, but we are stressing that it will take time and that there should be no rush. the situation in belarus, to mention another state, is dictatorial and far from satisfactory, and their road to eventual eu membership is long. nevertheless, i believe that, alongside calls for democratization, we should send clear signals that belarus, when it becomes democratic, will also have a place in the european family. what i am looking for in neighbourhood policy and in the european debate in general is vision and optimism. without optimism and a clear determination about what we want to achieve and what europe we want tomorrow, we will not be able to meet today's challenges.
in writing. small businesses are crucial for job creation in the member states and thus for the future development of europe. however, this is not a cross-border issue, but on the contrary it must be up to each member state how it wants to shape its small business policy. institutional competition, where member states can find good solutions independently without being locked into rigid eu regulations, is crucial for the development of small businesses. that is why we believe that the eu is not the solution to creating a small business-friendly environment. the report notes that obstacles to the development of smes include access to credit, complex tax systems and administrative procedures for accessing structural and development funds. the member states should do more to simplify for small businesses and there are concerns about the wide disparities in entrepreneurship training and the encouragement of entrepreneurship in schools. it is also argued that the charter should have the force of law and be binding, as this would increase the involvement of the member states. the idea that a european charter and annual reports would be the solution is just another example of the eu's lack of reality anchoring. the junilista has therefore voted against the report.
in writing. we support a thorough reform of the sugar sector in the eu. it is not reasonable to keep a non-competitive market alive by means of extensive protectionism and substantial financial support. the european parliament is trying to block the commission's reform proposals. the european parliament wants the reform to be implemented over a longer period of time, price reductions to be limited, restructuring aid to be increased and specific support to be granted to production in areas with 'structural climatic conditions'. the consequence is that the necessary structural adjustments are being postponed. overall, the commission's reform proposals are more far-reaching. we have therefore voted against the european parliament's proposal.
in writing. we have voted in favour of the report on the evaluation of the debate on european union. we think the report is good in general, but we would like to stress that it is not the task of the european institutions to convince citizens of what changes are necessary or good. opinion-forming and political debate shall be based on the involvement of actors in democratic society, individuals, political parties and other voluntary associations.
in writing. i voted for paragraph 27, but i do not agree with the second sentence of the paragraph. the public need not be reassured, they can think for themselves and decide what they think of the constitution.
in writing. the junilist generally supports the eu's neighbourhood policy as it aims to strengthen democracy and develop trade and prosperity in countries in the union's neighbourhood. that is why we shall vote in favour of the report at the final vote. we believe that the instruments of the neighbourhood policy must be flexible and cost-effective. there must be clear requirements for the follow-up of the results. neighbourhood policy should be limited to issues of cooperation on trade and democracy and human rights. it should not be used, as it is today, to extend the eu's sphere of interest through the common foreign and security policy.
in writing. we voted for amendment no 19. we interpret the concept of 'guaranteed minimum income' in the above-mentioned amendment as meaning a guaranteed decent standard of living, as we are in favour of a guaranteed decent standard of living but against the introduction of state-relegated minimum wages.

a writer. president. - the debate is closed. mr president of the council! a little overshadowed by the dramatic events in the middle east, in havana there is a bearded dictator who has been haunting the world for decades. he rules over a country where human rights are violated, where the people live in abject poverty, and where dissenters are imprisoned and harassed. after china, cuba is the world's largest prison for journalists. in the spring of 2003, a wave of oppression swept the island. leaders of the democracy movement were arrested, and after farcical trials, 75 of them were sentenced to long prison terms. last year a large group of young people were arrested as a preventive measure, in case they might cause trouble. that's how much fear there is on this island. when the european parliament awarded its sakharov prize to the 'damas de blanco', the wives and daughters of the imprisoned dissidents, just before christmas, representatives of the movement were not allowed to leave the island to travel to strasbourg. cuba is a terrible dictatorship and the above facts are well known. over the years, the situation has worsened. the situation for political prisoners is very serious. my friend héctor palacios, who was sentenced to 25 years in prison, is very sick and the doctors fear for his life. he's not getting help for his high blood pressure and his heart complications. adolfo fernandez seinz, to give another example, a journalist sentenced to 15 years, is also in poor health and has lost 20 kilos since his imprisonment. outside prison, dissidents are also harassed, such as sakharov prize laureate oswaldo josé payá sardiñas and blind human rights activist juan carlos gonzalez leiva, who has been under house arrest since april 2004. it is, of poor state of health. when the council of ministers decided a year ago to change the 'common position' it had held for many years and instead to start talks with the regime, it thought it saw some kind of opening and possibilities in cuba. a majority in this house criticised that policy, and a year later it has to be said that it has had no effect. it was a wrong decision and it should be reconsidered. what does the council intend to do now to support the dissidents in cuba? how can we support the democracy movement and the cuban people in a good way? we must increase the pressure on fidel castro and at the same time find a strategic way to identify the positive democratic forces that exist. why is it so hard to support the democratic forces in cuba when it's going well in belarus? there's still a kind of castro romance that's embarrassing to me. i would very much like to have answers from the council to these questions.
rapporteur. – mr president, the issue of men’s violence against women is not a small issue on the fringe of society. it is everywhere and it affects the whole of society directly and indirectly. i am grateful for what i perceive to be a growing understanding of the scope of this problem. i want to thank my fellow meps in the committee on women’s rights and equal opportunities for the valuable help and support i received during this work. i am also pleased that commissioner franco frattini has shown a strong and honest commitment. we now have a serious opportunity to recognise all women who have experienced violence but not justice. the cornerstone of my report is that we need correct figures. at eu level there are no reliable studies. however, three national prevalence studies in finland in 1999, sweden in 2001 and germany in 2004, indicate that previous assessments have vastly underestimated the extent of the problem. using the united nations’ definition of violence against women, these studies show that 40 to 50% of women in these three countries have, at some point in their lives, been subjected to violence by a man. i should like to emphasise that: between 40 and 50% of all women between the age of 16 and 67. the corresponding figure for the whole of the eu would be incredibly high, at between 80 and 100 million women. obviously effective measures must be based on correct facts and figures and the need for similar studies in other member states is urgent. the magnitude of the problem in itself indicates that all member states are repeatedly violating the basic rules of civil liberties and the rule of law on a scale that we have yet to understand. you may think this an exaggeration. it is not. i would like to invoke a classic liberal. more than 300 years ago, the british philosopher, john locke, introduced a fundamental idea: a government should be regarded as a tool for the defence of fundamental rights, the most fundamental civil right being the right to life and physical safety. this is the moral, normative basis for the state and its reason for being. citizens agree to obey the laws, but this presupposes that the state fulfils its basic commitment to protect the lives, freedom and property of its citizens. any state which fails in this task has broken the contract. when it comes to violence against women, the failure is measurable in all member states of the union. all violence is a challenge to our civilisation and to the rule of law. but men’s violence against women is a specific challenge to the contract of which all common policy is an expression. there is a systematic difference in the way crimes of violence are treated, depending on the gender of the victim. violence against men typically happens in public, perpetrated by another man, often a stranger. violence against women typically happens in private, perpetrated by a man known to the woman, very often a man with whom the woman has or has had a relationship. the most crucial difference, politically, is that private violence against women is not prosecuted as seriously as public violence against men. the issue of men’s violence against women is not a small issue on the fringe of society, there is also a history. violence in the street from a stranger, primarily affecting men, has been a criminal offence in europe for centuries, but it is quite recent that violence occurring in the home was made a criminal offence. well into the latter part of the 20th century, europe had many laws which excused domestic violence by men against women. this legacy lives on in our union. we can see it in case law, attitudes and perceptions about the less serious nature of private violence. the issue of men’s violence against women is not a small issue on the fringe of society. men’s violence against women has also been a way of keeping men, in general, in power. when the un general assembly in 1993 adopted the declaration on the elimination of violence against women, it was the first time a un document had placed men’s violence against women in a gender-power perspective. it explicitly links violence to the superior position of men. what we need to do now is to act. first get the figures right and then proceed to the proper solutions. the issue of men’s violence against women is not a small issue on the fringe of society, it affects us all. i demand zero tolerance of men’s violence against women.
for the pse group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i should like to take this opportunity to thank the rapporteur for giving us the opportunity to discuss this extremely important issue in the european union this evening. violence by men against women is a serious socio-political problem throughout the world, in addition to being a criminal act and a violation of human rights. we social democrats believe that violence by men against women is linked to the unequal power relations between men and women, and the problem will increase unless the member states strongly and clearly oppose this through legislation. men's violence against women naturally has great social and human consequences. behind every battered woman, i often say, is a number of children who, in addition to suffering great physical and mental suffering, believe that this is the way it should be, which in the long run may lead them to adopt the same behavior when they become adults. we must break this vicious circle. often we forget the children in these contexts too. it is important that children who see their mothers beaten are also considered victims and have the right to a fair trial. it is also demonstrated that violence against women is an important factor in the lives of women and girls who are victims of trafficking and prostitution. i would also like to point out that we should combat the attitude that prostitution is to be equated with professional practice. this kind of violence and gross human abuse is absolutely unacceptable. all forms of violence against women honour-related violence, genital mutilation, etc. and prostitution and human trafficking are linked. the eu must lead by example and take the lead in combating these manifestations of unequal distribution of power and discrimination between the sexes. it is also important to work on prevention through research, information and the exchange of knowledge between member states. often, the focus is naturally on the victims, but active prevention strategies targeting the perpetrator and those at risk of becoming perpetrators are also required. finally, i would like to mention briefly one of my own amendments, which concerns alcohol in relation to violence. alcohol is often involved in acts of violence and it is important that no reference to intoxication be accepted as an extenuating circumstance in cases of male violence against women. however, the triggering effect of alcohol on men's violence against women should definitely be taken into account when reviewing strategies to reduce alcohol-related harm in society. (applause from the left)
i am pleased that the report emphasizes the principle of subsidiarity in the authorization procedure. i also welcome the fact that the european parliament has stressed that the commission should pay particular attention to the consequences of the liberalisation of the postal market for rural dwellers (paragraph 9). however, i do not have any clear wording to make it clear that we cannot accept a deterioration in the service provided to the people living in isolated areas of europe. the commission has not taken a position on restrictions on the frequency of mail distribution (see oral question h-1135/05). the european parliament is not clear on this issue either. the future availability of postal services to people living in rural areas depends on the price of services being comparable to the level in the rest of the country. the european parliament does not take a position on this issue in the report, nor has the commission. i am also critical of the european parliament's criticism of national vat on the postal market (paragraph 18) and of the proposal that the european commission should investigate the way in which the pension data of public postal operators are handled (paragraph 17). these are issues which must be dealt with at national level. i shall vote against this report.
for the verts/ale group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. it is true that i do not often agree with the commission on liberalisation projects, but this is a sensible form of liberalisation. i see that jacques toubon has done some serious work and has carried out an 'impact assessment', but that is not enough reason to regulate at eu level. if a country wants to regulate bread sizes and pasta sizes, they can do so, but not at eu level. it may be a little ridiculous when the eu is doing this. what consumers expect is protection against dangerous additives and a review of the additives we use. a clear label large enough to be read, and in your own language, so that you understand the ingredients. the rules on misleading marketing cover the problems that consumers might encounter due to confusing pack sizes. it is the kind of legislation that has a real 'impact' on consumer needs. i agree with the commission on alcohol, but that is another matter. it is a question of controlling consumption in a different way, and the existing rules proposed by the commission are appropriate. there are reasons for regulation. i think it was a well-balanced proposal that the commission made, not to increase eu legislation.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to thank the commissioner for his report. i would like to welcome the commission's proposal for the deregulation of packaging. i hope that the further discussion will lead to a move towards the commission's line, so that it is the consumer and not the politicians who make the choice. the rapporteur, jacques toubon, has shown a willingness to accept my amendment which saves swedish milk packaging, so that the milk for the coffee at work can be kept. there is still some room for manoeuvre in the liberalization of butter and pasta, and in general less detailed regulation. it would be bad for competition and for consumers if we were to undermine the interests of smes and give market shares to large multinationals through policy measures. we cannot accept the report in that form, as we come from a small country. we've had enough of debating cucumbers and strawberries. let us not have a debate on milk-snatching and let us negotiate further towards greater freedom for the consumer. there is a long way to go before this matter is satisfactorily concluded.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the amendments by jacques toubon to the commission's proposal mean that many countries are setting up new rules at eu level for a market that is already functioning. we swedish social democrats believe that the rapporteur's amendment does not bring any benefits to consumers, on the contrary. if the rapporteur's line were to be heard, it would entail considerable costs for many european companies to convert, and this without there being even a need or necessarily a desire on the part of the groups which it claims to represent. the swedish consumer agency, for example, which is actively working with several groups and associations on packaging issues, has never heard any requests for fixed packaging sizes, according to our contacts with them. moreover, the eu institutions would not live up to their ambition to improve the quality of legislation by avoiding detailed regulation. we swedish social democrats have nothing against putting high demands on companies to achieve better consumer protection, quite the contrary. in this case, however, as i said, it is a question of unnecessary regulation and we shall therefore vote against it.
mr president, i would like to thank you. thank you helmuth markov for a job well done. what we need are safer and more secure roads in europe. today, 43,000 people die on our roads every year, and we know that the times when large vehicles are involved in accidents have disastrous consequences. we must do everything we can to make the roads safer and safer. the decision we are about to take is a genuinely european one. rider and professional drivers often work in several european countries. their daily work is to move goods and passengers across our continent. you're competing on a european level. we must also have fair competition and good control possibilities at european level. we must also have better conditions for professional drivers, conditions which are safer and more secure in their work. we have the opportunity to decide that today. we're not getting everything, but we're getting somewhere. there is also the introduction of digital tachographs, and i think that is a very important issue. today, we have travel writers who can be manipulated, and who are manipulated. it is important that we have safe tachographs which contribute to safer and safer traffic for all of us, but also for professional drivers. i therefore propose that we vote 'yes' to this conciliation proposal.
