on behalf of the alde group. – madam president, we are at a crossroads in the fight against trafficking in human beings. last week on 8 march, and again now, commissioner frattini announced that an extensive study will be launched this year on how laws on prostitution affect the situation of trafficking in the different member states. that is indeed good news. in september last year, the committee on women’s rights and gender equality presented a similar, smaller, study on the issue. it was the first of its kind. that study gave us a strong indication that criminalising the demand for trafficking has a very strong impact and that it substantially lowers the number of girls and young women being trafficked. legalisation, on the other hand, has the opposite effect. it increases the number of victims of trafficking. the figures also show clearly that not only does the legalisation of prostitution increase the legal part of the business, it also increases illegal prostitution. with the forthcoming study by the commission we will strengthen the factual basis for measures against trafficking, especially criminalising the demand. this is no longer just an issue of different opinions; it is an issue of facts. i warmly welcome commissioner frattini’s work on the issue. do we really want to fight trafficking? there is a way. are we brave enough?
for the pse group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i thank mr papadimoulis for the extensive work he has done on this report. we live in a time when disasters seem to be becoming more common. a few years ago, iran was struck by a terrible earthquake. i remember it very clearly because i could see on tv every day that they had no help in digging up the living people under the ruins. just over a year ago, southeast asia was hit by a flood disaster that also hit my own country hard. there are stories from this disaster of how some of the eu member states saved their own citizens but left other people sitting next to them to their fate. it is clear, however, that the rescue operations would have worked better if eu cooperation had worked better. it is also true that aid could also have worked better in cases where eu member states have been affected by major floods, forest fires, etc. moreover, as climate change increases, these types of disasters are likely to become more common. that is why this instrument is extremely important. we social democrats have stressed that this instrument must work both within and outside the eu. in part because of the fact that our own citizens are often outside the union, but above all out of solidarity with other people. we also think it is very important that the eu should also cooperate with the un, which is the natural organisation. we have also tabled our own amendment on the budget for this instrument, which we hope everyone can support, because it states clearly that the budget is dependent on the negotiations on the budgetary plan. we also support the rapporteur on the legal basis and on extending the instrument to include disasters affecting public health. if we have to give priority to the budgetary work that is to follow, then the most important thing for us social democrats will be direct action and its preparation both within and outside the eu.
for the verts/ale group. i would like to thank mr matsakis for his exemplary work as rapporteur. the parcom agreement of 1990 already agreed that the chlor-alkali industry would phase out mercury by 2010. now we must get an export ban in place before then, otherwise it will export huge quantities of mercury. the largest deposits in europe are right there. it is important to have an export ban before then. we talk about safe landfills, but the second largest use today is 10 centimeters from our brains. i got my first amalgam-free filling 21 years ago. it's still holding. it's amazingly good quality. only 0.05 percent of dental fillings in children and adolescents in sweden today contain amalgam, while the corresponding figure for adults is two percent, and it is steadily decreasing. the swedish chemicals agency has shown that the environment and health are the main reasons why we have to get rid of this, and there are good alternatives. the amalgam fillings account for the vast majority of waste water discharges. you see here that three quarters come from amalgam and that the large part comes from dental clinics. thank you again, matsakis, for a great job.
for the pse group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i should like to begin by thanking the rapporteur for his open approach to this report, which has been successful in one committee. he has done that in a most excellent way. i also share the rapporteur's view that restructuring is not something new. if i look at my own hometown, i can see that the big jobs that existed when i was a child are no longer there - it's completely new jobs that have grown up. as a result, society has changed, and the workplace is more high-quality today than it was when i was growing up. this development will continue. the new thing is that it is happening so much faster today in the globalised world, which is why we need to have restructuring as an instrument. it can be seen as a threat or an opportunity, but since we have chosen the path of saying that we will not compete with india and china, with their low wages and working conditions, but will maintain a high level of wages and good social conditions, restructuring must be an instrument of the lisbon process. but the question is how we will carry out the restructuring. first, we must have foresight. there are trends that we need to be able to see. we must also act in time, because if you act late, it is sometimes too late. then the factories will have to shut down, there will be no other options. we must act on the change in time. secondly, it must be done with participation. the social partners and employees must be involved in the whole process, so that the process can be prepared for restructuring in terms of skills development and other measures. thirdly, experience must be shared. i visited the dublin institute last weekend. there is extensive work and information on successful restructuring processes. i can say, for example, that already today 75 per cent of the employees in västervik, where electrolux closed down last year, have been given new jobs. the company, the social partners and the local community have worked together to create new jobs. in creating a new global fund, let us not forget the old instruments: the social fund, skills development and the responsibilities at national and regional level. i am a supporter of the globalisation fund if it is used to empower individuals and jobs and not to preserve old structures. but we should also use the old instruments, such as the social fund, to strengthen skills development.
for the gue/ngl group. i would like to thank my colleagues on the committee on women's rights, in particular the greens and sartori, for their dedicated work on this issue. i fully support it and so does the whole group. we recognise that an institute does not in itself increase equality, but that, if properly designed, it is a hugely important tool for successful equality work. i would like to point out two proposals which would further strengthen the work of the institute. the first concerns the possibility of an effective evaluation of the impact of decisions at all levels on women and men, and the second concerns the composition of the management board: six members from the council, six from the commission and three additional members from a workers' organisation, one from an employers' organisation and one from a voluntary organisation. i think that the latter three should also have the right to vote and that the ngo should be a women's organisation.
junilista believes that gender issues are important and that equal treatment of women and men is a matter of course in all contexts. the new eu institute for gender equality will analyse gender equality in the union, based on comparative data from each member state. the natural question is what added value this authority will bring. will it have a concrete and significant function, or is it mainly a way for the eu institutions to show their ability to act? will the authority contribute to the promotion of gender equality, or is it mainly a form of alibi, a way of showing that the union cares about gender equality issues? the arguments presented in the report are not convincing. the authority's tasks may be carried out within the framework of existing structures. the eu already has the central statistical office 'eurostat'. it can produce comparative statistics on gender equality in the eu countries. there are also national authorities dealing with gender issues. these authorities can cooperate across national borders. the commission is composed of 18 men and 7 women. about 30% of meps are women. the eu institutions should start by implementing gender equality within their own structures and thus set an example, before new centralised eu authorities are established. moreover, cooperation can take place in more decentralised forms, without the eu growing by creating new authorities for each pressing issue.
in writing. the report proposes the creation of a new european institute for gender equality. the basic position of the junilistas is that gender equality issues have a very high priority. equal treatment for women and men is an indispensable requirement that all eu countries must fulfil. this does not mean, however, that the eu should set up another bureaucracy for this purpose. sweden has come a long way in its gender equality work and junilistan is convinced that this work is best done at national level. a european-level gender equality institution means more bureaucracy and less efficiency. in order to engage citizens of both sexes in the fight for equality, it must be driven from below in the member states, not from above by employed eurocrats. the need for comparative statistics in this area can be met by eurostat. we have therefore decided to vote against the report as a whole.
in writing. the eu has too many authorities and agencies. i shall nevertheless vote in favour of the establishment of this institution. gender equality is a disaster, not least symbolized by 83% of men in top positions, untimely parental leave rules, a total lack of gender education and miserable legislation. it may be worth several million to try to correct this a little bit with an institute that would complement the already existing one on discrimination against minorities. then it may be reasonable to have an institution on discrimination against the majority, which is women.
in writing. there are good reasons for member state governments to give priority to measures that reduce the risk of natural disasters. we are, however, questioning the prominent role that the european parliament wants the eu to play in this context. parliament called for the eu to play an active role in preventing disasters such as severe drought in spain and portugal or fires in southern european countries, for the eu to intervene in disasters outside the union through rescue services, etc., and for the budget for the rapid response and emergency mechanisms to be increased by eur 105 million (compared to the commission proposal) for the period 2007-13. we believe that it is primarily the responsibility of the member states to respond to natural disasters. many forms of natural disasters (such as droughts and fires) are recurrent and predictable. it should be fully possible for member states to carry out independently the investments which minimise the risk of such disasters occurring. in addition, the un already has an operational system to help countries affected by disasters and serious accidents. member states could well support this system, rather than building a parallel structure and thus risking unnecessary duplication of work. we have voted against this report on the basis of the above arguments.
in writing. the eu countries should build structures to help each other in the event of forest fires and other natural disasters; this is a typical area where international coordination is good and necessary. parliament also amends the legal basis, which increases the scope for citizens' influence. i therefore vote in favour, even though i lost the vote on the inclusion of terrorist attacks. i believe that these are of a very different nature and can be protected against in a very different way, and therefore should not be covered by the fund. this is a question of operations of a different nature and only the purely civilian parts, such as helping with the clean-up after an attack, should be covered. terrorism mainly affects countries with a colonial foreign policy which should be changed, but instead they want to spread that policy and its consequences to all eu countries. however, it is likely that only a very small part of it will be used for these purposes and it is therefore still an acceptable proposal.
in writing. we swedish social democrats support the hutchinson report in principle, but our view on relocations is as follows. we believe that eu funds should not be used to move production, because in practice this means moving around unemployment and pitting workers in different regions against each other. however, we do not consider that all relocations are generally wrong. the restructuring of industries must be possible in order to develop them. the increase in the level of knowledge and skills in a region or country may require relocations in order to develop the region.
in writing. an increasingly globalised world is creating new conditions. it creates new problems to deal with and it creates new opportunities to take advantage of. the european parliament is voting today on alain hutchinson's own-initiative report on relocation in a regional development perspective. we have voted against the report because we think it is a wrong approach to the problem of relocation of businesses. we agree that it is not justified for eu funds, including from the structural funds, to finance improved conditions of competition for european companies which shortly afterwards decide to move their operations outside the eu. however, neither the state nor the eu authorities should intervene in the way companies conduct their business and what requirements apply to prevent companies from making rational decisions to survive. we don't create full employment through increased government regulation, we create it by facilitating more businesses and new investment in the private market. we cannot therefore vote for further restrictions on the mobility of companies within the internal market, as proposed in this report. in order to overcome the problems and the displacement caused by a globalised market, we therefore need a different starting point from this new trend based on economic patriotism.
in writing. this report, which is outside the legislative procedure, deals with an important issue. however, we would have liked to have formulated the problem differently. we believe that we cannot interfere in the relocation of companies to third countries outside the eu. as a rule, market considerations should determine localisation at global level. in this context, eu countries can make efforts to compete in terms of, for example, offering knowledge, skills and stability. as regards relocations of businesses within the union, we must tackle the problem of individual member states offering discriminatory tax breaks and state aid as a complement to eu structural aid. the commission notes that the commission did not provide any information on the profitability of the company in the period considered. we see this as a major problem in the eu's internal market. the rapporteur also mentions in his explanatory statement that a relocation monitoring centre could be set up within the eu. instead of a new supervisory body, the commission must be responsible for monitoring closures due to restructuring and discriminatory tax systems. we therefore vote against the report. in principle, we consider the subject to be important, but we would have liked to see a resolution with a different approach to this important issue.
in writing. junilista supports the proposal to take measures to reduce and eventually phase out mercury emissions. this is a transnational environmental issue where a common and coordinated approach is warranted. we have therefore voted in favour of this report. we do, however, have some comments on certain points in the european parliament's proposal. for example, we believe that the member states are fully capable of carrying out information campaigns on the health risks of exposure to mercury on their own. we also believe that it is primarily the responsibility of the member states to deal with the social consequences of mercury mine closures, rather than the eu.
commissioner, what is it? the union's history of anti-dumping is appalling. well-organized special interests are allowed time and again to seek through customs small profits that come at great cost to consumers. when the commission introduced duties on television sets, consumers had to pay two crowns for every crown earned by the industry. in the case of the duties on pakistani bed linen, each crown for the producers cost our consumers three crowns. in the case of norwegian salmon, the situation was even worse: each norwegian krone of profit for the salmon farmers cost the consumers as much as 70 kroner. the commission consistently fails to take adequate account of consumers and thus the community interest. now they are about to make the same mistake again with shoes from china and vietnam but this time we know in advance the price of the policy. the danish government carried out a study which showed that the costs to consumers in the union are eight times greater than the profits to producers, and in total the union loses over two and a half billion kroner. for sweden, the figure is even more frightening. every swedish krona earned by swedish producers costs swedish consumers 44 krona. in total, swedish consumers are expected to pay almost 60 million more for their shoes. the only country in the union where there is an estimated wine store is slovakia. the estimated salary is $300,000. it will be cheaper if we here in the european parliament scrape together the money, so that the citizens will not have to pay the cost. honestly, i don't think this is a mistake. the commission has nevertheless deliberately chosen to compare with expensive shoes from brazil, measuring the increase in imports before the quota was abolished with that after the quota was abolished. i am seriously concerned about the growing protectionism i am facing in the union and i hope that this is the last time the commission will give in to protectionist demands at the expense of the citizens.

mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to make some general reflections on the whole of the lisbon agenda. in my view, it is based on a misunderstanding of how economies and, for that matter, human civilizations develop through history. we have had a moment in history when europe became the most dynamic knowledge-based region in the world. it was from the late 1700s and the 1800s. it started with changes in britain, legislation on economic matters, the abolition of the scrapyard, and so on. the advances were enormous and rapid, and they spread rapidly to large parts of the world. steam engines, spinning jennys, etc., were also used in other countries. that's how development happens. the idea that there are now exceptionally clever people who know in detail what measures should be taken in european countries to achieve a favourable development towards a knowledge-based dynamic region is completely wrong. countries are looking for solutions. they look at each other eagerly, imitating good solutions. that's how development happens. keeping on like this is something that's going to take our minds off the track. we should be dealing with real issues in the european union.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i shall concentrate for this brief moment on an amendment by the socialist group in which we welcome the proposal by the heads of government of six countries to establish a european pact for equality between women and men. we propose action in three areas. firstly, we want to reduce the gender imbalance in the labour market, secondly, to facilitate the reconciliation of work and parenting and thirdly, to introduce a gender perspective with follow-up in all policy areas. the aim of this pact is not to create a new process but to strengthen the processes already in place, such as the lisbon process, in order to achieve the goals of sustainable growth, full employment and social justice. the goal of childcare and the possibility of combining work and parenting is particularly important.
mr president, i would like to thank you. when we discuss the lisbon process, we can see that it is succeeding more or less well in different countries. countries that have implemented reforms and changes are better able to cope with both globalisation and competitiveness. those who have not implemented reforms are doing worse. but what is remarkable is how little has actually happened at the common european level, how little we have increased the scope for expansion for new companies, new products, new services and new markets. the commission's overall task is first and foremost to combat the new protectionism we see emerging among governments and politicians in europe. the new protectionism is directed at the new member states and the outside world where the large future markets are located, but it is also directed at and growing between the old member states. the overall task of managing the lisbon process is to combat this protectionism. it is contrary to the treaty and to all that european integration is. europe has great potential. where we have carried out reforms, we have succeeded. take the telecommunications market, for example. that's where we're most successful. what the commission must do is to make clear the importance of safeguarding the free trade that has built europe's prosperity. the commission is also proposing to strengthen the role of the european parliament in the fight against fraud and corruption. it's the results that count, not the goals. then we can give europe a new future.

in writing. we are strongly critical of the eu's destructive fisheries agreements with third countries. we therefore regret that the eu is opting to conclude fisheries agreements with new countries. recurrent reports have highlighted the negative impact of the fisheries agreements on the coastal population of the contracting countries. the agreements lead to overexploitation of fishing waters, which affects the local population. however, the commission and an overwhelming majority of the european parliament have consistently chosen to turn a blind eye to this criticism. the fisheries agreement in question may also have negative environmental consequences, according to one of parliament's opinions. while the eu advocates increased aid, citizens' taxpayers' money is used to finance fisheries agreements that impede development. this is neither a consistent nor credible policy. we believe that the fisheries agreements should be dismantled and that they should be abolished in the long term. the cost of the agreement should be borne by the member states of the union whose vessels fish in third country waters. these member states may then decide independently whether they will finance this expenditure by charging fees to their fishing vessels.
in writing. we, the moderates, have voted in favour of the report in question, but we regret that the protection of fundamental rights is not sufficiently highlighted at eu level and believe that the jurisdiction of the court should include the fundamental rights of individuals also in matters of internal security.
in writing. the own-initiative report on the evaluation of the european arrest warrant against adelaine hazan will help us to further strengthen the legal certainty for our citizens. the report points to improvements that have been made. i would, however, like to emphasize the problems which still constitute an obstacle to the guarantee of legal certainty and which are mentioned in the report. it is of the utmost importance that translation, interpretation and legal assistance be guaranteed to persons arrested under the european arrest warrant. it is the responsibility of each member state to remedy this important problem which today involves human rights violations.
in writing. this report shows a clear commitment to enhanced cooperation in the field of criminal justice. the council was asked to prohibit member states from re-introducing a systematic double criminality check and to integrate the arrest warrant into the first pillar. the report highlights major problems in the implementation of the arrest warrant. member states have clearly indicated their wish to preserve parts of the traditional extradition system. some countries have refused to execute arrest warrants for their own citizens, citing fundamental rights or discrimination. others have retained or reintroduced double criminality controls. the junilist considers that this is a clear sign that the member states are safeguarding their sovereignty in the area of criminal justice. impeachment or serving a sentence are some of the most far-reaching measures that can be taken against an individual. in this case, legal certainty must take precedence over the simplification and efficiency that the report claims the arrest warrant generates. the junilista rejects the idea of extended supranationality and considers it to be a national issue. we have therefore voted against the report.
in writing. we support the resolution because we believe it is important to focus on measures to reduce the number of victims of the sex slave trade. however, we believe that the resolution should cover all forms of prostitution. the word "forced prostitution" can be interpreted as if there is an opposite, so-called "voluntary" prostitution. we believe that all prostitution is forced - no woman chooses to prostitute herself voluntarily. she is forced to do so for various reasons, such as poverty and unemployment, but above all there is a clear link between the choice to become a prostitute and previous physical, psychological and/or sexual abuse.
in writing. in the report on restructuring and employment, the rapporteur, jean louis cottigny, adopts the same sad attitude as in the report on relocation in a regional development perspective, which was voted on yesterday. yesterday we voted against this economic protectionism and today we are voting against it. once again, it is our firm view that it is hardly the state or the eu that should tell companies how to restructure. but we should not hide our heads in the sand and pretend that certain restructuring and relocation of businesses do not affect people and social conditions in the area concerned. we support the call for a stronger dialogue between the social partners on these issues, but the negative effects can be addressed in other ways than by preventing the private sector from developing. instead, we should focus our energy on improving the conditions for more long-term jobs to be created through more enterprises.
in writing. the eu has long been taking policy action to address restructuring in various sectors. this report contains some positive proposals for changes to these measures, such as more effective control of the resources disbursed from the community funds and the non-use of the funds for relocations within the union. the basic junilist position is that the consequences of relocations and restructuring are national issues. we do not believe that the eu should take action to hold companies to account. this type of important issue is to be decided in the member states. parliament called for: the establishment of criteria for the conditions under which restructuring may be carried out (to save jobs, improve competitiveness and not for purely profit-making purposes, etc.), the creation of a special fund for 'adjustment to growth', the union's responsibility for the 'hidden effects' of restructuring, such as the consequences for workers' health, psychological problems and increased mortality among those who are dismissed, the participation of employees in companies, since they become the actors in decisions on restructuring, views on the fact that member states prematurely retire workers as a consequence of restructuring. regardless of political opinion on the above issues, this is a national issue. we have therefore voted against this report.
in writing. the report is generally good and highlights many of the problems of a short-term speculative economy. that is why i am voting for it. the report does, however, make a positive reference to the fund which the commission intends to set up. this fund would involve direct payments to individuals and would be the embryo of the eu taking over social policy, which would be an unfortunate development. if this fund is introduced, however, it is good that private funds are involved and finance a reasonable part of it. i am voting against new eu directives on labour law in restructuring as this would undermine the swedish model of collective agreements between the social partners.
in writing. this report contains a long list of calls on the member states to take action to tackle the relative poverty in their respective member states. there are of course good reasons for member states to cooperate on this type of issue, for example by voluntarily exchanging experience and best practice. however, social inclusion and poverty are issues to be dealt with at national level or through voluntary cooperation between member state governments. it is difficult to see what added value or specific competence the european parliament brings by acting as an opinion machine on this and similar issues. the report proposes that: member states should increase lifelong learning opportunities (paragraph 11), member states should provide access to good quality childcare at low cost (paragraph 24), member states should reform their pension systems to ensure maximum social equity (paragraph 44), member states should not increase the overall taxation of labour through reforms of their public pension systems (paragraph 45). the junilista advocates that important issues such as the above should be decided through a broad national debate. member states shall then decide on the appropriate legislation and relevant measures, either independently or in voluntary cooperation with appropriate actors, in the ordinary democratic procedure. we have therefore voted against this report.
in writing. in the final vote we voted in favour of the 2007 budgetary guidelines, despite our serious objections on two points. we are against the development of a special statute for members' assistants. the assistants are thus at risk of living in conditions which are quite different from those of the citizens in the constituencies in which they are to work. there is also a significant risk that the assistant will become a special lifelong career. we also oppose the establishment of a european centre in brussels to carry out information policy on the eu.
in writing. we welcome the introduction of more environmentally friendly fishing methods. however, we are critical of the proposal that the eu should introduce a support and compensation system for professional fishermen who are negatively affected by the introduction of more environmentally friendly fishing methods. the report does not mention the amount of compensation or the budget line from which this compensation is to be paid. we support the involvement of fishermen and their representative organisations in the design of measures to protect the marine environment and to restore fish stocks (amendment no 1). we are opposed to the proposal for community financing of the proposed compensatory measures for fishermen (amendment no 2). we are critical of additional budgetary expenditure within the eu and have chosen to vote against this report as a whole.
in writing. in this resolution, parliament addresses the areas where action is required by the national parliaments to achieve the agreed objectives of increased growth and employment in europe. the lisbon strategy is based on the member states implementing what has been agreed. the lisbon strategy must not become an argument for constantly demanding increased appropriations from the eu budget. the junilista group instead believes that the lisbon strategy must be included in the budget of each member state. the resolution also calls for the eu budget to be increased to meet the objectives of the lisbon strategy. for that reason, we choose to vote against the resolution. the resolution contains many positive proposals, but it is based on the assumption that the eu should have a budgetary plan, which we do not support. the responsibility for implementing the lisbon strategy lies with the member states, which is why it is important that their membership fee to the eu is not increased but that they have a margin to take action themselves on what is needed within the lisbon strategy. we therefore voted against the motion for a resolution tabled by the ppe-de, pse and alde groups.
mr president, i would like to thank you. commissioner, madam president of the council! it's great that you're keeping a high profile on the balkans. we all have an interest in the stabilization and democratization of that region. the eu really has the potential to play an important role. i wish you both good luck in bringing your colleagues in the other member states to the same high level of ambition. i want you to know that you have the liberal group with you. the various initiatives to get countries to cooperate with each other and with us are very good. it is also good that it has finally been decided to build on cefta, which already exists and actually works, instead of creating something new as was discussed earlier. i think it's a very wise decision. at the same time, it is also about different countries with different traditions, different histories and different levels of development. it is therefore important that we maintain the message that all these countries are welcome to join the european community if they so wish, and that they will then be treated on their own merits. there is a concern today in some quarters that we want to bring the countries back together and that they will be treated as a collective. i think that concern is overblown, but it's very important to be very, very clear on that point, that each country is treated on its own merits. as for the death of slobodan milosevic, he was a despicable dictator, responsible for the deaths of hundreds of thousands of people and for much of the tragedy that has unfolded. i also regret that the trial could not be completed and i think it was obvious that it was a rather pathetic man who ended his days. we must still be crystal clear that radovan karadzic and ratko mladic are to be released, and that this should be done now. there's no room for compromise there.
for the alde group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. enlargement is the eu's greatest success in european cooperation. the unification of east and west in may 2004 marked the end of europe's division and demonstrated the power of the dream of a united europe based on democracy, open trade and respect for the rule of law. the eu membership was the carrot that helped the strong reform forces in the former communist dictatorships, the prospect of membership also has a huge significance for the negotiations and reforms in turkey and the balkans. the eu has a tremendous source of so-called soft power here, which contributes to a more stable and democratic europe. we in my group believe that it is important to keep the door open for further enlargement, as the treaty says. of course, the criteria must be applied, and the eu's capacity to receive is important. but we need to change internally and not blame enlargement, but take that discussion. i am aware that there is a debate in many countries and a concern that the eu is growing too fast and how it should go. that conversation must be conducted in a humble and forthright manner, but we must also have the courage to stand up for and highlight the benefits of enlargement. in this context, the debate on economic protectionism is very worrying. we have a responsibility to our neighbours. the promises to the balkans and turkey must be kept. they set the pace, and we do what we can to speed it up. we must also remain open to other countries such as ukraine and perhaps also, one day, belarus, even multilateral agreements, let us first - as commissioner rehn said - take the opportunity to individualise and concretise the neighbourhood strategy for those members. now, to bring up new concepts that we haven't really discussed and that we don't know what they mean doesn't feel very fruitful at the moment. mr president, i would like to thank you. thank you to both trakatellis and kyprianou. you've done a good job of it. but we find ourselves in an absurd situation when we spend five times as much money on tobacco subsidies as on health promotion. five times more money spent destroying health than helping. what i think is good is that this report focuses on prevention. the resources are so limited that they are only enough to share good examples, disseminate information and cooperate. but it is at the national level that the big money is and where the big work will be done. the good thing about the amendments of the european parliament, and i would like to stress that they are important, is amendment no 53. and because the money is so limited, it cannot go to lobbying organizations, or covert lobbying organizations, for the pharmaceutical industry. it is good to check carefully that this is not the case. amendments nos 92 and 144, which deal with equality, have not been mentioned so far. i think that's an important aspect that we have to consider. too much of the money goes to men's health care and too little goes to women's health care. but the great things that europe can do here are in health and trade. the public health articles of the treaty hardly apply to trade policy. where is the health dimension of this policy in relation to alcohol? the same goes for chemicals and pesticides - that is where the big action has to be taken. commissioner, you can make a first contribution to that overall vision by not approving the eight new pesticides that are in the pipeline. they are bioresistant, endocrine disrupting and carcinogenic class 2. yes, that's a long list of perfect reasons to ban a chemical. you can take that chance now.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i have had several comments on the commission's health plan and i have had some quarrels, but i think it has turned out really well and i would like to thank both mr kyprianou and mr trakatellis for that. health first is a matter for the member states has been self-evident to me. i am glad that the majority of the house agrees with me. what we do at eu level should bring clear added value for patients. there are three areas in which i have been involved and which i would like to highlight today. first of all, the starting point of this report is and must always be that the individual's own commitment to his own health is of the utmost importance. that is why we politicians, in health care and in public health work, must always regard citizens, even if they happen to be sick, as adults with rights and obligations. with the right to control their own lives and their own health care. we must never forget that our own commitment to our own health is always the best medicine. it is therefore regrettable that the social democrats want to delete the wording which refers to the commitment to one's own health. as meps, we must also facilitate mobility within and between countries, so that everyone can seek the treatment and care they most believe in. today, free movement in the eu is least for those who need it most, i.e. patients. for them, the borders of europe become little berlin walls that stand in the way of their access to treatment. for them, freedom of movement can be a matter of life and death. we should not believe that the most important elements of the good society can be regulated and organized by a planned economy, which has so clearly proved to be the economic model most destructive of creativity and household life. we need more choice and mobility. we must also work to improve access to health information. to us as decision-makers, to the performers and, not least, to the users. we need to be able to compare outcomes, not just costs, as we do today. this is necessary both to learn from each other and to enable users to benefit from the mobility and freedom of choice granted to them by the court of justice. it is for the benefit of europe's patients. up until about world war ii, you were a victim when you came into contact with medical care. you were usually healthier before the doctor was called than after. with the development of treatments and medicines, we have become patients in the health care system. but i am convinced that in the near future we will be consumers of health care, and we must help create this shift in perspective. victims yesterday, patients today, health care consumers tomorrow. it would be a wonderful development that i think would make us healthier, but also freer.
in writing. the report recommends that the eu establish a separate community programme on health for the period 2007-13. the budget for this programme is proposed by the european parliament to be eur 1 200 million (the commission proposes eur 969 million). the junilists are of the firm opinion that health matters are essentially a matter for the member states. several of the objectives mentioned by the rapporteur (such as combating ill health caused by tobacco, alcohol and poor diet) can be pursued by the member states independently. the principle of subsidiarity is thus not sufficiently taken into account. of course, international cooperation is necessary in connection with, for example, viral epidemics and complex medical issues. however, international cooperation should mainly take place through multilateral agreements and within the framework of the activities already carried out by the world health organisation (who). we reject the allocation of additional funds for this purpose and have chosen to vote against this report on the basis of the above reasoning.
in writing. i think that designations of origin for agricultural products and foodstuffs are often good because they strengthen and develop local production and trade as opposed to the dominance of the wto brand giants on the global market, but i am opposed to the eu deciding on mandatory labelling for these goods; this should continue to be voluntary.
in writing. geographical indications and designations of origin are a source of wealth for europe. sharing our culinary heritage is enriching. at the same time, however, the disadvantages of these designations must be borne in mind. we do not want appellations of origin to be used for protectionist purposes. for a couple of centuries, european immigrants have been making food and drink in their new home countries, bringing with them traditions from their countries of origin. the european union must have a tolerance within the world trade organisation for such traditions and for the fact that trade names and designations of origin may sometimes collide. the two reports by the european parliament on this subject are merely supplementary proposals in accordance with the consultation procedure. we believe that they have little to add, although some of the proposals are better to than against, and that this subject should be discussed in the council of ministers. we also strongly reject the idea that an eu authority could be given the task of controlling geographical indications and designations of origin. we believe that we must trust the authorities of the member states in this matter. we have therefore voted against both these reports.
in writing. we swedish social democrats want to see an open and united europe that welcomes new members on the basis of the copenhagen criteria. we therefore regret that the report places too much emphasis on the eu's own absorption capacity, as this may give the impression that the european parliament is questioning the eu's ability to include more members. the eu's own absorptive capacity ultimately depends on the union's willingness to include more members. we are also concerned about any tendency to exclude countries from membership on the basis of, for example, their economic situation.
in writing. this own-initiative report addresses the important issue of continued enlargement of the eu. the junilists are in principle in favour of further enlargement of the eu, provided that the prospective member states share the fundamental values which form the basis of the union's values. this includes, in particular, respect for the rule of law, human rights and democracy. as to other laws not covered by the above, nations are sovereign. the rapporteur also proposes a very substantial increase in the budget, equivalent to about sek 25 billion, which we are opposed to. we have therefore voted against the report.
in writing. on 1 may 2004, the eu was enlarged by 10 new member states from eastern and central europe. countries that were once behind the iron curtain have, with the help of the eu as a carrot and stick, developed into democracies with market economies. this is a historic event. we must now fulfil our promises of continued enlargement to countries such as romania, bulgaria and croatia. but we must also keep the door open for new applications. those who meet the requirements will be admitted to membership. today we are voting on a draft strategy for further enlargement. it proposes that the eu should define its geographical boundaries. i will vote against that. the borders cannot be locked. the establishment of frontiers for europe would, for example, make the ukrainian people, who are now weighing between democracy and dictatorship, feel that we are throwing the door in their faces. it would be a historic setback.
for the alde group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. because we had so much to do in the morning, this debate has been postponed a little and it is unfortunate that the rapporteur and many others who have worked on this issue cannot be here, but that is the way it is. i was suddenly given a lot of speaking time by my group and turned down the last five minutes, even though i was offered to sing a song. but i won't. the single market has created millions of jobs and increased prosperity. it has erased boundaries and opened doors. we can live, travel, study and work - whatever we want. consumers have access to a host of new products. we must therefore go further and also create a single consumer market. this means a common regulatory framework and a high level of consumer protection throughout the eu. as cross-border trade increases, so does trade at the individual level. then you have to know what the product is that i'm buying, what i do if there's something wrong with the product, how i can appeal, what the regulations are. how do i know this product really does what it claims to do? what are the possibilities for service, warranty and dispute resolution? this must be guaranteed if we are to regain the confidence of the citizens, because this is an everyday issue that concerns the conditions of the everyday consumer and this is what we need to work on at eu level. it is therefore very good that the commission has put forward this proposal for a long-term multiannual programme for consumer protection. the liberal group, however, does not agree with the commission on the need to combine consumer protection with public health. we share the rapporteur's view that they should be kept separate. these are two separate policy areas with different approaches, although there are, of course, points of contact. you should keep the two separate. we do not want any special authority or agency in this respect either. other important issues which we are very pleased to have included are the view that consumer policy needs to be integrated into all policies, that it is important to cooperate with the consumer organisations that are around us in our member states and that those states which have not yet made progress in the field of consumer protection can receive extra support and attention. i am very glad that we have been able to agree on this and that there is a broad consensus among the groups on this. i would like to thank mrs thyssen - i hope she reads the minutes - for the foresight and cooperation she has shown. we are ambitious in consumer issues, both in terms of cooperation and evaluation and information. so, of course, there must be a proper budget. i hope that there will also be good cooperation in future with regard to the consumer programmes which the commission will present later this autumn. in fact, perhaps the opposite should have been done, i.e. first the major political guidelines and then this programme, but that is the way it is now. we stand behind this and will support the report by a large majority - or indeed, i think unanimously - when it is voted on at the next partsession.

mr president, i would like to thank you. there are two things that are important to define in the common european task in the field of energy policy, and which i think we must clarify. the first is that one of the obvious tasks for us together is to ensure that the internal market functions. there are many obstacles here today. it is all about ensuring that businesses can grow together across borders, and ensuring that the internal market can function, both legally and technically. we need common networks because it is a way of achieving many of the objectives we share. we'll have more competition and lower prices through joint networks. if we are to have an opportunity for renewable fuels to grow, we need a larger market where they can also be marketed and developed on a commercial basis. the reduction of vulnerability is achieved through common grids, and the possibility of simultaneous access to safe and cheap electricity. the second common task in the field of energy policy is, of course, that of foreign and trade policy. i think that it is crucial, once again, for solidarity between countries that we have the common networks. we can talk about solidarity and solidarity clauses all we want, but the fact is that it is only with common networks, such as those of countries like estonia, latvia and lithuania, or any other country that can be subject to political blackmail, that we will be able to achieve real solidarity. then it becomes a reality, not just a political goal. in foreign and trade policy, any cooperation with russia must be based on clear and transparent terms of trade and distribution. the union must support a policy which leads to a commitment by russia to deliver to each individual member state under safe conditions. that is why we have a good energy policy, and that is why we have solidarity, and that is why we have a better europe.
mr president, i would like to thank you. despite the success of 10 european parties, the democratic legitimacy of this parliament, among others, has been diminished in elections. the report says that there is a gap between the citizens and the eu institutions, which is true, but the solution to this is not financial support for european parties, but democracy should be strengthened, inter alia, by returning power to the national parliaments. it is also said that a union close to the citizens is a prerequisite for the next step in european integration to be supported by the citizens. so, to convince the citizens to accept the rejected constitution, contributions will be allocated to the european parties. all democratic forces can of course form parties, but to think that by distributing large financial contributions one is going to get the citizens to accept the draft constitution is not only trying to buy acceptance, it is also very doubtful democratically.
the member states are facing significant demographic challenges. however, the conditions and problems differ from one member state to another. for this reason, as well as for democratic reasons, national solutions are needed. it is unreasonable for the european parliament to put forward detailed policy recommendations which are expected to be acceptable to all member states. the report in question contains a long list of calls for action by member states in such pressing areas as social security, taxation, working time and immigration. the paths to be taken by each member state in important policy areas must be decided by national democratic processes and not imposed from above. political and social progress is made by countries experimenting and trying out different solutions that can be compared. then you learn from each other. it is through such processes that european culture has grown and, in effect, conquered the world. european solutions and ways of thinking have been successful precisely because they have emerged through institutional competition between different countries rather than being decided centrally. the report under discussion is another example of the european parliament's slow but steady attempt to gain increasing influence over national policy issues. i deplore this process and criticize the near-inexistence of opposition in the european parliament to this undemocratic development.

in writing. the current situation in the democratic republic of congo is very worrying. the country has long been plagued by great instability, which has led to brutal violence against civilians, famine and a very serious situation throughout the great lakes region. we are, however, positive about the fact that elections are now being held. we in junilist strongly oppose the use of the mission in the democratic republic of congo to further strengthen the eu's common foreign and security policy. the situation in the democratic republic of congo must be resolved, but not by the contribution of a joint eu force. it is up to each member state, at the request of the un, to decide whether to provide troops or not. we have therefore voted against the resolution.

in writing. we are opposed to the subsidy system which is now being set up for european political parties. parties must be built from the bottom up by the parties in the member states. if these member parties do not find it justified to increase their funding for their european party, it may be questioned whether there is really a need for european political parties to exist. we consider it reprehensible to start parties at eu level that are heavily subsidised by the eu taxpayers. we therefore vote against this report.
in writing. we voted in favour of the report on demographic challenges and solidarity between generations. we voted against the report that higher taxes to fund social security are a less sustainable solution in the long term. furthermore, we interpret an increase in the retirement age as an increase in the actual retirement age, not the statutory one. since the text was not sufficiently clear on this point, we could not support it. we also do not support the european parliament calling on the member states and companies to break the link between old age and higher pay.
in writing. the moderates have today chosen to abstain in the vote on the demographic challenges and intergenerational loneliness. although the report makes many relevant proposals, we cannot support it. the reason is that the report deals with matters which fall within the competence of the member states.
in writing. we voted for the report on the promotion of non-food crops because we think it is good when farmers find alternative crops to traditional ones when these are no longer viable. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. we do not, however, believe that a switch to energy crops should be allowed to slow down the dismantling of the cap. it is important that these new products are developed and grow in a free market without subsidies. temporary aid should, in so far as it exists, be aimed at infrastructure measures to start up operations, not at subsidised production.
in writing. the report is merely an opinion of the european parliament outside the legislative procedure. it is based on the further extension of the existing common agricultural policy to energy crops. there is much to add to the debate on energy crops. we have voted against the report.
mr president, i would like to thank you. when we discuss competition policy, there is one point to emphasise, and that is that european welfare, european businesses and european jobs have been built by competition. the opportunity to start new businesses, the opportunity to challenge old established businesses - that is what has created european welfare and european prosperity. it is therefore legitimate and important to guard competition policy against all the forces that want to promote protectionism in various ways. there are a few points in this context, and this is also clear from the report we are discussing, in which the commission has a particularly important role. the first is to discuss competition issues from the perspective of the single market as a whole. it is a step we must take, because if we are to have global champions, we must also ensure that europe, across borders, can have viable large companies that can also be actors on the global stage. it calls for a competition policy which moves forward the positions of the old competition policy. i therefore believe that the commission must act with vigour and unwavering loyalty in upholding the treaty. by that i mean taking strong action against those parts of the internal market where member states do not live up to the standards that are set. this is the case in telecommunications, where we have seen that the liberalisation of competition has brought prosperity and where it is important that this will apply in all sectors of the economy. it is about energy issues, and i welcome the fact that the commission, as i have understood from the media, will act to enforce a single market and to fight the protectionism we see today in the case of various mergers. the last point is about what jonathan evans also mentioned, namely to ensure that we have a transatlantic competition policy which is reciprocal and which gives strength and power to the whole transatlantic economy. i would also like to emphasize that competition policy and competition are the most powerful forces behind european integration today. it's what weaves economies together and it's what gives us jobs. (applause) i would like to thank you.
mr president, i would like to thank you. first, a warm thank you to michael cashman for his great cooperation over the years, for the increased openness. the european parliament has always been the driving force, but i must also admit in the name of truth that the commission and the council have also recently contributed to the improvement in quality which is actually quite enormous and which we have seen over the last five years. we now have an open legislation in the eu which in some respects goes much further than in most national parliaments, such as my own. the aim of this review is to achieve uniform rules for all three institutions. in the long term, i hope that this will be reflected in the national bodies. the regulatory framework must be designed to be accessible to the majority of people, both in the eu and in the member states. parliament adopted this transparency initiative to increase democratic scrutiny and clarify legislative ambiguities. the minutes of council meetings must be made public when they are enacted, which does not necessarily mean that all coreper meetings are public. as in the conciliation process, a certain degree of negotiating power should be guaranteed. however, transparency should of course be applied when the decision on the legislation itself is actually taken. to give the public access to legal opinions in general would be to reduce the political space for action. alternatively, the quality of the opinions is reduced. however, in certain cases, a member of parliament involved in a case should be allowed to have access to the legal opinions after having given a written undertaking to observe the confidentiality of all other participants in the case. strong and clear data protection is a prerequisite for increased transparency. the rights of openness and data protection complement and reinforce each other. information given in confidence must be respected, and we should not have retroactivity in this area either. i am convinced that when the commission is finished, we will have a reasonable and balanced proposal. i have full confidence in margot wallström. citizens' access to documents must be further developed and the council must show respect for citizens and the democratic process. there's a lot of work to be done there.
