mr president, i would like to thank you. i thank the rapporteurs for their good work and i like johannes voggenhuber's proposal to refuse to be complicit in an undemocratic decision-making process. where's the council? do they have trouble staying awake this late in the day? it's because they're locking themselves in their rooms -- if they open up their rooms, they're getting enough oxygen to last them through the night, so they can participate in these debates as well. why don't they open the doors? politicians like every chance they get to be on tv, don't they? you wonder why the council is passing up that opportunity. you wonder what they are doing, and the whole population of europe wonders. there's an easy way to fix this: open the doors and see what happens. i trust that margot wallström will lead the eu into the 21st century and wake the council from the slumber of the 20th century. i feel it is important to remind the council that if they now open their meetings, it must not be an hour's meeting and a four-hour working lunch, so that openness becomes a chimera. we trust that the council will also eat quickly and decide long and openly.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to begin by thanking the rapporteur, mrs magda kósáné kovács, for her very good work. we in parliament have supported the idea that the guidelines should be long-term, three-year cycles, and that they should be integrated. given this longterm perspective and this integration, it is extremely important that we review the national reform plans. it is important that the commission does so, but it is also important that parliament is involved in the examination and follow-up of the national reform plans. commissioner almunia said that the economic outlook is better now. that is true, but the employment situation in europe is anything but bright. it is very possible that it can be improved, but let me take three areas that are extremely difficult. firstly, there is the issue of youth unemployment. youth unemployment is extremely important, because if young people go directly from education to unemployment and long-term unemployment, it is extremely difficult to get them back into the labour market later on. i do not believe in the french model or, as some other parties say, that by reducing the security of that group or of any other particular group, employment is stimulated. the employment of managers is stimulated by an active economic and labour market policy. as regards the older workforce, we face the dilemma of leaving the labour market far too early in the eu today. we need more skills development for the older workforce, but at the same time we need to strengthen health and safety at work. finally, equality. equality must permeate everything. i welcome the gender equality pact which is now, or will be, part of the lisbon process. especially important is the development of child care and other facilities that enable men and women to work together and have a functioning family life. these are the aspects i would like you to take up.
mr president, i would like to thank you. two reflections first. the economies in europe that are most open to globalisation are the ones that are doing the best today. that's the one reflection. the second is that the countries with the most flexible labour markets also have the best job creation. it is against this background that i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr josé manuel garcía-margallo y marfil, for the work he has done. because the changes that are proposed there, the policy orientation that is proposed, are the policies that can bring more jobs and more prosperity. the crucial question is whether this will lead to change, whether policy will change at european level and at member state level. a swedish politician once said that "if things are allowed to continue as they have, then things will go on as they have". we have more than 20 million unemployed today. it is an economic issue, but it is also a very social issue. there is no issue more important to social security than the growth of new jobs. the commission has a number of tasks to which you must give attention. firstly, it must enforce the directives that open up more competition, and secondly, it must act to open up new business, and that puts the whole of competition policy in focus. new entrepreneurship is not just the emergence of small businesses, but also the possibility of establishing new crossborder companies in europe that can be 'global champions'. the commission must take its responsibility in this respect, but also the member states in the reforms leading to new enterprises. finally, we must ensure that there are more flexible labour markets in europe. if we do not, we will be shutting millions of people out of the labour market and social security. those in favour of social security and economic development are in favour of more flexible labour markets. i would like to say this to our friends on the socialist side: it is reforms that bring security.
mr president, i would like to thank you. may 1, 2004, is a day i will always remember. that day, i was standing with dr. otto von habsburg on the border between slovenia and austria, and i participated in a solemn ceremony. it was a celebration of the reunification of a city that had been divided into two parts, one of which belonged to austria and the other to slovenia, following the post-war partition, and slovenia's accession to the eu. the symbolism was clear. and standing there, i felt strongly that the people did not feel that they were entering into something new, but that they were rejoining something that they had always been a part of. and that's that. the ten new members of the eu are the ten old countries of europe. it's just that communism had kidnapped them for 50 years and now they're finally reunited with us. in sweden, the social democrats had painted horror scenarios for this reunification, about how our country would be invaded by foreign workers who would dump our wages and take advantage of our social benefits. i and my party fought in the swedish parliament to prevent our country from introducing transitional rules. we wanted the new countries, which had suffered so long under communism, to enjoy the freedoms of eu membership. we refused to accept the propaganda of the social democrats and we showed what real solidarity is. we won the battle; sweden never introduced any transitional rules. the evaluations now made show unequivocally that sweden has not been subjected to the social dumping that the social democrats were frightened of, but that free movement has only had positive effects on sweden's economy. the same experience has been shared by the other countries which failed to introduce transitional rules. and i must say that it is with great disappointment that i have taken note of the decision of several countries to extend their transitional rules. to do so when the examples cited were absent is not reasonable. the only right thing to do is to abandon transitional rules, and i hope that in the future europe will be moved more by reason than by fear. (applause) i would like to thank you.
in writing. we have voted in favour of the amendments concerning european coordinators, as we believe that a voluntary and temporary coordination function could serve a purpose, especially in cross-border projects.
in writing. the junilists believe that eu member states should cooperate on cross-border issues where cooperation brings added value. transeuropean energy networks are such an issue and that is why we also voted in favour of the report as a whole when it was discussed earlier in parliament. however, several of the amendments we voted on today are unnecessarily bureaucratic and the additional cost and administrative burden they would entail is not commensurate with the potential benefits. we have therefore voted against these amendments.
in writing. in principle, i am opposed to this type of report, where parliament repeats, in matters of routine, positions already known and public goodwill in a policy area. it does not contribute to greater confidence in parliament and it provides a legitimate basis for those who want ever greater eu intervention also in policies which should be national and competitive, in this case employment policy. i have, however, voted in favour for one important reason: the free movement of labour. the decisions authorising discriminatory transitional rules against workers in the new member states are a flagrant departure from the principle of free movement of labour and a shameful act towards the new member states of the european union, which have joined the union with great enthusiasm. the experience of countries with weak or no transitional rules has shown conclusively that there is no need for them and that the 'invasion' threatened by some political leaders has not taken place. rather, there is a need to attract more people who want to work, rather than to prevent those who do. the need to make this clear and to express clearly parliament's opposition to any extension of the possibility is for me a sufficient reason to support the report.
in writing. we believe that the council and coreper must be public when legislation is being enacted. it is an important democratic principle for the functioning of the electorate's accountability to its elected representatives. unfortunately, the draft report contains several points which support the adoption of the draft constitutional treaty rejected by the french and dutch referendums. we have tried to get these parts removed from the report by calling for separate votes on these very points. however, regardless of the outcome of these votes, we want to vote for the report as a whole, as reforms to the transparency of the work of the council and coreper are a priority issue and we want to put pressure on the council to change its rules of procedure. but we strongly oppose the way in which the majority of the european parliament is involving the debate on the future of the constitutional treaty in this important issue of transparency.
in writing. this own-initiative report highlights the eu's double standards in international trade policy. there is much talk about the need for emerging economies to open up their markets to european companies. the rapporteur also said that eu agriculture had a 'multifunctional character' which 'must be respected'. we believe that free trade will lead to a better world in the long run. however, account must be taken of the development of individual countries. if trade is to have a poverty reducing effect, even at the beginning of the development process, the eu must change its trade policy by abolishing agricultural subsidies and allowing poor countries to compete on fair terms. since the report contains mostly negative texts, we voted against it in today's vote.
madam president, i would like to thank you. i believe that this debate cannot be reduced to a debate on the quality of legislation. we also need to discuss the quantity, the amount of eu legislation. in fact, the volume of eu legislation has grown dramatically in recent years, and the eu is now legislating in a whole range of new areas. at the same time, it is extremely rare for the eu to return decision-making powers to member states or to repeal legislation. the combination of a very large amount of legislation and a lack of quality results in poor visibility and makes it difficult to implement legislation in practice. as far as quality is concerned, much can be done today, and there are proposals in the reports. outdated legislation can be removed, simplified and consolidated. all this is good and well, but we also need a different approach to the legislative process. we need less detailed management, more target management and more freedom for the member states to choose how to implement the targets they set. as far as impact assessments are concerned, they are good, but it is not always so easy to measure what you want to measure. it is often easier to measure simple economic factors than environmental factors, for example. we had that discussion in connection with the debate on reach, the chemicals policy. it was easy to measure the costs of the company, but it was hard to measure the enormous positive effects on public health or human suffering, because you get fewer diseases. that makes one a little cautious in this area. if we really want to simplify, we have to reduce the number of eu laws, and the area that is most over-regulated is agricultural policy. here most of it could be brought back to the member state level and thousands of laws could disappear. some speak of the eu constitution, but the fact is that it would have made these problems worse by increasing the eu's power, making it easier for the eu to usurp new legislative powers and to legislate in areas where it is not really allowed to go. so we have the dutch and french voters to thank today too.
for the gue/ngl group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. our group did not vote for this compromise in the conciliation committee, and we will not vote for it in the plenary tomorrow. we are not satisfied with the content and we are certainly not satisfied with the legal basis. this is not a criticism of the rapporteur. we believe that avril doyle has done her best in a difficult situation and tried to go as far as possible in asserting the right to maintain and introduce better environmental standards, but given the very weak position she gained from the second reading in parliament, it was not possible to reach the only reasonable one, namely to take article 175 on the environment as the only legal basis. for us, the only acceptable thing is that environmental legislation should be environmental legislation. we see two problems with this compromise. the first problem is that we believe that in some parts of it more could have been done; it could be done more quickly to eliminate these very dangerous greenhouse gases. but the most serious of all is that there is now an active risk of hindering countries who want to tighten their national legislation in this area. it is contrary to the spirit of the kyoto protocol and will be a potential obstacle to countries wanting to combat climate change, and as we all know climate change is the most serious environmental threat we face. as far as denmark and austria are concerned, they will have a guarantee until 2012, then the guarantee will be over, then we do not know what will happen, then there is the risk that they will be actively forced to reduce the legislation they already have. in other countries, reforms are effectively halted unless the commission decides otherwise. it's totally unacceptable that it's like this. this also means that countries within the eu will have difficulty in developing new technologies - exactly what this document says they should do - to go ahead and ban dangerous gases and force alternatives that new technologies can produce. the council and the commission are blocking the development of new technologies. we tend to agree in our debates on the seriousness of climate issues, and we tend to agree when we see the scientific reports saying that the situation is more serious than we thought a few years ago. then comes this statement from the commission showing that they have learned nothing from climate issues. what you're saying is that the market is still more important than the environment. the right of companies to sell dangerous gases is more important than the ability of politicians to combat the greenhouse effect and climate change. it is remarkable that in 2006 one can say something like that. (applause from the left)
mr president, i would like to thank you. i also want to thank avril doyle, because she's a fighter in this congregation. the council and the commission have defended old outdated technologies in the internal market instead of defending the climate. history will judge such actions harshly. an example: why don't we ban fluorescent greenhouse gases from refrigerators, for consumers? 90 percent of the market is already free of fluorinated greenhouse gases, it's very easy to decide to just ban it. allowing sulfur hexafluoride, which is twenty-three thousand times more damaging to the climate than carbon dioxide? the only way to have an alternative is, of course, to set a time limit. then comes the research that gives you the right options. in fact, there are already some options today. i wonder, then, if there is a typo in the commission's speech, if you would not be happy to see countries introducing stricter legislation to achieve the kyoto targets? i am assuming that there must be a clerical error in the speech, because the commission cannot possibly support such an old-fashioned and outdated policy.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the european establishment has a repugnant habit of using acts of terrorism, natural disasters, political crises and economic disruptions for its political ends. the legitimate anger, anxiety and political commitment of the citizens is being exploited to promote the fight for a european superstate. that is why we have often had to listen in this house to endless lines of speeches based on the terrorist attacks in madrid and london, the tsunami disaster in the indian ocean or the dangers of war in the middle east. the speakers, including the president himself, have often shamelessly used these tragic and fateful events to further a hidden agenda. the fight for a european superstate. now europe's energy supply is in crisis, it is the same thing. the twenty-five ladies and gentlemen of the european council have no idea what energy technology and policy will look like in ten years' time. they should therefore not set percentages for biofuels and renewable energy. the countries themselves should seek solutions and experiment in a free energy market. the eu's task is to maintain a free energy market and coordinate infrastructure investment.
in writing. the moderates have today decided to abstain in the vote on the report citizens for europe. the report makes some good suggestions, such as the importance of raising awareness of the victims of nazism and communism. it is also good that the eu institutions provide information on their activities and on the rights and obligations of european citizenship. we are, however, critical of the expansion of the eu's financial allocation for opinion-forming activities on european identity. a european identity is created by people meeting and working together, not by public institutions forming opinions.
in writing. we are very opposed to some of the amendments tabled by the committee on culture and education. the report is based primarily on a viewpoint which rejects the low turnout in the 2004 european parliament elections and the rejection of the constitutional treaty in french and dutch referendums. the citizens for europe programme has nothing to contribute to changing the political situation. we oppose an increase in the budget for this programme beyond the eur 235 000 000 allocated for the years 2007 to 2013, as advocated in amendment no 36. the sum already mentioned is too generous. working with local amateur sports associations is a show of contempt for the citizens. we also note the importance of the peoples of europe cherishing their memories of dictatorships and tragedies in their history. but it has to be done at national level. this is not something for the eurocrats in brussels to concern themselves with. we have therefore voted against this report.
in writing. i am in favour of the report in that it calls for the citizens of the eu to be more aware and informed about the eu and the possibilities they have to influence it. i also support the rapporteur's view that the programme should be available to all citizens and not just to well-educated and privileged groups. i am critical of the writings that refer to the new constitution and the aspiration for a common european citizenship.
in writing. we are in favour of the basic idea of the european capital of culture event. but at the same time we see the event swell in terms of the eu's commitments and the organisation around it. the idea of a european capital of culture is a good one. it is even so good that it can be financed in a variety of other ways, including local sponsorship. financial resources from the european union should not be necessary for the survival of this project. we therefore vote against this report as such, but we are not opposed to the idea of action itself.
in writing. this decision does not entail any new costs but gives the new and enlarging countries the opportunity to choose cultural capitals. cultural cooperation is what can give citizens knowledge and respect for each other and is positive. the commission's powers are limited and the countries and cities are responsible for the design. this is a positive development, in contrast to the imposition of eu legislation which restricts the democratic possibilities of citizens to participate actively. that is why i voted for it, despite some euro-nationalist flourishes.
in writing. we are committed to a well-functioning internal market and fully support the free movement of workers within the eu. this is one of the fundamental freedoms of the ec treaty. the free movement of workers contributes to the creation of more jobs and to the competitiveness of the union. we are proud that sweden is one of the three countries which have not introduced any transitional rules. we regret that the heads of state or government decided to introduce transitional provisions in the accession agreement with the 10 new member states. at the same time, we respect the choice of individual member states to act on the basis of the possibilities offered by the accession agreement. in its report, the european parliament called on the member states to abolish the transitional provisions in force. we believe that this question should be decided by the member states at national level. we therefore oppose the european parliament's opinion-making in order to influence the member states to act in a certain direction on this important issue. we also do not believe that the eu should carry out information campaigns on the free movement of workers. we have full confidence that the respective national authorities can handle this task satisfactorily. we also question the proposal to introduce a system of 'systematic monitoring of the migration of workers within the eu' and to allocate 'the necessary resources' for this purpose (paragraph 11). we have voted against this report.
for the gue/ngl group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. our party group believes that the elections in belarus were undemocratic. the opposition was prevented by a variety of methods from acting reasonably. the media is controlled by the lukashenka regime. this is made clear in our motion for a resolution. in this context, it is important that the outside world has close contacts with democratic forces in belarus, such as popular movements and free trade unions. the belarusian people cannot be isolated. despite lukashenko's anti-democratic methods, there is still significant popular support for him. this is partly due to the relative social equality and security of the country. but social security, which is a good thing, can never justify restrictions on democratic freedoms and rights.
for the ind/dem group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. international solidarity and the ability to help people in developing countries is very dear to my heart. i believe that aid is an important tool in this fight for a better world. the eu's aid policy is itself very bad. but it is also opposed by other policy areas. firstly, the eu has an agricultural policy which gives absurd subsidies to relatively rich farmers. this, together with the eu's duties on agricultural products from poor countries, is causing agricultural policy to actively oppose aid. secondly, the eu's trade policy is depriving poor countries of the opportunity to enter our market with their competitive products. a recent and illustrative example of this type of harmful protectionism is the eu's recently imposed footwear duties against, among others, vietnam. thirdly and lastly, i believe that development cooperation should be carried out at member state level. of course, coordination of action is always a key issue in aid to make it as effective as possible. however, this coordination should take place at un level, where donors from all over the world, not just from the eu, work together to get effective aid that de facto leads to poverty reduction. this would really lead to the improvement of the world's poor countries, which should be the main objective of aid.
in writing. we note that countries with strong animal welfare legislation to ensure good conditions for animals while reducing the risk of spread of infection and thus also reducing the risk of adverse effects on human health have not been affected by the decline in sales of chicken meat. we therefore want to stress the need to strengthen animal welfare and food safety in the current situation in order to give food producers the right to earn the trust of consumers. the eu should therefore act to achieve this. however, in the emergency situation, we do not want to risk breeders concealing infection or otherwise increasing the risk of spread of infection for fear of financial loss. that is why, in the present situation, we support the possibility of financial compensation for farmers who are affected.
in writing. we are voting against the amendments tabled by the european parliament. we believe that it is too early to assess the damage to the poultrymeat industry in the event of a bird pandemic. it is typical of this parliament that whenever something unexpected happens, it calls for financial support for the storage of goods, financial compensation for food destruction and the financing of information campaigns to regain consumer confidence. we see no reason to meet these requirements in this case. how far should the planned economy be allowed to go in the food industry? consumers must always eat and if they cannot eat poultry, other food industries benefit instead. we believe that the market is very well able to adapt to the current situation. the poultry sector has less incentive to take preventive measures if it receives compensation promises in advance.
in writing. the junilist is opposed to a common european asylum and refugee policy. we therefore do not consider this a matter for the european parliament. we believe that member states should be free to determine their own immigration and asylum policies, as long as they are in line with international law. human rights must not be violated. we are in favour of a humane refugee policy and we oppose the placing of asylum seekers in detention facilities. we vote against the resolution as a whole.
in writing. the delegation of the left party in the gue/ngl strongly condemns the lukashenko regime and its violations of democratic and human rights. we believe that the presidential elections in belarus violated several fundamental principles of democratic and fair elections, and we therefore vote in favour of the resolution. this does not mean that we support every single wording in the resolution, but it is crucial for us to protest against the crimes against democratic and human rights in belarus.
in writing. the global fight against disease and poverty is a matter of great urgency and requires a major international commitment. however, we do not think that this issue should be dealt with within the framework of eu cooperation. we believe that the fight against ill health should be carried out by the individual member states of the union and within the framework of the world health organisation. the junilista group wants limited eu cooperation in this area and would like to see aid policy re-nationalised. we are also opposed to the union having influence and extending its competence in matters already dealt with by other international organisations. we therefore vote against this resolution.
in writing. the report addresses a very central issue in eu aid, its inefficiency. the junilists' position is that aid should not be provided by the eu, but by each member state. the eu's assistance is, as the rapporteur points out, ineffective. while relatively large sums are being spent on pursuing an eu-led development policy, new trade barriers are being created by the eu, making it impossible for the world's poorest countries to enter the eu market with their products. in addition, the eu's common agricultural and trade policy is very damaging, since developing countries are unable to compete with their agricultural products because of tariffs and subsidies. if the report had been on aid at member state level, we would of course have voted in favour of it, because inefficiency and corruption are two major obstacles to good and effective development cooperation. however, this is now a matter of eu-led aid, which is why we have voted against the report as a whole.
a writer. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. since i was first elected, we've been discussing the situation in sudan. what's going on in there? imagine that one of your friends is killed and what a tragedy and misery that is. imagine that 180,000 of our friends have died in the conflict today. imagine a single friend whose house is burned down and forced to leave his home. how does that make you feel? imagine those tears multiplied by 2 million in darfur today. imagine a single relative being raped, and it being repeated tens of thousands of times. that is the scale of the humanitarian disaster that is afflicting sudan. what does the government do? resolution after resolution, we demand that they disarm the janjaweed guerrillas, and nothing happens. in resolution after resolution, we demand that they work with the international community to help their own people, but very little is done. the situation is not improved by refusing to allow un envoy jan egeland to go to darfur when he deems it appropriate. the commission is already providing eur 160 million in aid, and my own country is providing sek 330 million. if this money is to do any good, it requires the cooperation of the regime, not that they oppose our efforts. i think we should push through an arms embargo regardless of what russia and china say, and that all of us countries that stand for an arms embargo should be helped and help prevent the arms embargo from being broken. moreover, imprisoning women who resist rape is an absurd situation, and these four young women should of course be released immediately.
for the gue/ngl group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. it has been 20 years since the chernobyl nuclear disaster. still, 20 years later, we can see the terrible consequences. we can see them in the form of disease and disability for thousands of people. we can see them as giant patches of land that are uninhabitable, and that will be for many generations to come. even in my little corner of europe, in northern sweden, the fallout from the radioactive fallout had consequences for many years through the poisoning of nature that it brought. the chernobyl accident is the worst nuclear accident we have ever had, but we must not forget that it is not the only one. remember harrisburg! it was a good day. remember all the other accidents that have occurred in the history of the nuclear industry! nuclear power is not safe. over the past 20 years, very few new nuclear reactors have been built in europe. that means we have a lot of old reactors that are still running at full capacity. old reactors are dangerous reactors. it is time to seriously plan for the decommissioning of these older nuclear power reactors. the chernobyl disaster is a reminder of the dangers of nuclear power. we should add that the waste problem is not yet solved. we are producing waste today, and we still do not know how to dispose of it. we'll leave it to future generations. nuclear power has also proved to be a very valuable source of energy. it involves not only high production costs but also very high costs for the disposal of decommissioned reactors and the waste generated. last but not least: nuclear power is a finite resource. we're going to run out of fuel. we are now facing a gigantic energy transition. it is absolutely necessary to end dependence on oil and fossil fuels, not primarily because of the price of oil but because of the greenhouse effect. it would be a fatal mistake to replace dependence on oil with a new, even greater dependence on nuclear power. instead, we have a unique opportunity to build sustainable, renewable 'green alternatives'. it also requires that we dramatically reduce our dependence on energy. this is the conviction of the majority of the european left. but the eu must contribute to all this. this means that more of the eu's research money should go to renewable energy sources and less to nuclear research. that would require the euratom treaty, which has been obsolete for many years, to be dismantled as it stands today. only when we have eliminated nuclear power will the risk of new chernobyl disasters disappear. (applause) i would like to thank you.

been left a little bit out of the loop, which is sad. the development in latin america is very exciting very contradictory with economic growth, democratic stabilisation, reconciliation commissions and a development of welfare. chile has its first female president. at the same time, a very large group of people still live below the poverty line. inequality is high, human rights are violated in many countries, and populists have taken over in venezuela and bolivia. in cuba, for 47 years fidel castro has continued to imprison the opposition, harass dissidents and deny his people the enjoyment of fundamental rights such as freedom of speech, association, press and free elections. this parliament has discussed cuba on several occasions. my group is very critical of the council's softening of its attitude towards the castro regime and of the lack of support for the democratic opposition, whose representatives have twice been awarded the sakharov prize by the european parliament. in may, an eu-latin america summit will be held in vienna. we hope, of course, that it will be a great success. it is important to really enter into the strategic partnership that the european parliament has the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. the political legitimacy of the european union is low and waning. this lack of confidence is unfortunately well deserved. the eu is not demonstrating a convincing determination to crack down on abuse of power, irregularities and fraud in its institutions. the willingness to actually claim responsibility is weak. instinct is to put the lid on. whistle-blowers lose their jobs instead of getting credit. scandals and fraud are not investigated. scandals and frauds that are investigated are not prosecuted. discharge is granted, even though we don't know if everything went right. a majority in this house seems to want to hide unpleasant truths about the eu from the citizens so as not to make the organisation even more unpopular. this is a profoundly undemocratic approach. our job is to bring the truth and hold citizens accountable, not to hide the truth from them. as rapporteur on the committee on budgetary control, i had to prepare the discharge for the 'other institutions', seven in all. i have not found any reason to question the ombudsman or the data protection supervisor in these cases. four of the other five are open to criticism, but not of such a serious nature as to call into question the granting of the discharge. this applies to the council, the court of justice, the court of auditors and the european economic and social committee. as regards the committee of the regions, however, i think that further studies are needed before we can decide on the issue of discharge. in 2004, the european economic and social committee had problems with irregularities in the payment of travel expenses to its members. we now hope that this is a problem of the past. there's been a problem with belliard i and ii. i expect a full account in the court's forthcoming report. as far as the council is concerned, as usual, much of the risk of administrative activities lies in the construction projects in which the council is involved. i would like to emphasize the advantage of renting buildings rather than running such gigantic construction projects ourselves. the council would also welcome a clearer definition of what constitutes administrative expenditure and what constitutes operational expenditure. the court's internal controls were far from satisfactory in 2004. its internal auditor also acted as head of the verification unit performing ex ante controls, which is simply unacceptable. moreover, since his appointment in 2003, the internal auditor has not completed any of his audits. in this area, parliament expects vigorous action and improvements. the court's ongoing violent large-scale construction projects can only cause concern. a thorough and complete report to the committee on budgetary control on the progress of this project is therefore a necessity. the fact that the court does not currently require judges to account for their financial interests is not acceptable. the court must, whether or not there is currently a legal requirement, draw up its own binding rules in this area. parliament also called on the commission to take the necessary steps to ensure that the rules on the free movement of goods are respected. court of auditors the dominant position of a few large audit firms in luxembourg in the audit of the court's accounts is a matter of concern, while parliament understands that the court has difficulty avoiding this. an open, fair and understandable tender procedure is therefore of the utmost importance. the court has problems with its organisational structure which need to be changed. this must be addressed in the evaluation under way for the review of the organisation, preferably in line with the reform proposal drawn up by the british house of lords. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'commercial vehicle' to cover vehicles with a maximum mass of less than 300 kg. i come now to our black sheep, the committee of the regions. the committee of the regions has the most observations from the court of auditors of the 'other institutions' audited. in practice, in 2004, the internal audit was carried out by only one internal auditor, who was also temporarily employed. an internal auditor who is dependent on constantly renewing employment contracts must have great difficulty maintaining his independence from management. it is therefore inexcusable that the committee of the regions did not have a functioning internal audit in 2004, despite the strong criticism previously voiced by parliament in respect of 2003. we have still not received any explanation as to why the internal auditor who was in office until may 2004 was not in a position to produce any audit reports. the committee of the regions is also investigating by olaf the transfer of staff remuneration by applying a correction coefficient. transfers have been granted without being based on evidence of a right to such transfers. it is obvious that this should have been discovered by the committee of the regions as early as 2004 and that these payments should have been stopped. however, management has stated that there has been no fraud. in this case, too, there has been no clear indication that any action has been taken to recover incorrect transfers. the situation is aggravated by the fact that these payments are intended for persons holding key positions in the administration. the committee of the regions has been marked by a number of irregularities in recent years, which indicate that the promised cultural change within the organisation has not taken place. as things stand, olaf will be presenting its final report in mid-may. it would be irresponsible to grant a discharge before we know the outcome of this investigation. the committee of the regions' reorganisation has in several cases involved the promotion of persons involved in suspected irregularities, which is also illegal. the court's audit of the belliard i and ii buildings is still ongoing. the montoyer project should also be examined. so far, no explanation has been given as to why the former head of infrastructure seems to have withdrawn his declaration as subdelegate authorising officer for the 2004 financial year. we must also clarify why i, as rapporteur, was not informed of this and how the date for the theoretical reception was set. perhaps the damages awarded to the construction company would have been higher? in addition, the committee's internal auditor was denied access to the documents he had requested in this regard. there are strong reasons to wait for the court of auditors' ongoing investigation into this before we grant discharge. i would also like to take this opportunity to inform the house of the strange way in which the committee of the regions has been dealt with in the committee on budgetary control. i had received information from various sources indicating that there was much more to criticize in the committee of the regions' economic shenanigans than the committee itself had informed me. i therefore got the committee on budgetary control to agree that we should hold a hearing behind closed doors with olaf investigators and the committee of the regions' internal auditors for 2004. it was postponed until tuesday morning, 21 march 2006. when the committee began its sitting at 3 p.m. the day before and was to approve the agenda as the first item, there was suddenly a majority demanding that the hearing be adjourned the following morning. a majority of the members of the committee on budgetary control therefore refused to take part in information which i, as rapporteur, considered vital to taking a position on the question of discharge. i haven't been told why. i would argue that the protection of the eu's financial interests requires the committee to take all relevant information into account in such matters. the majority of the committee refused to see the information and then voted for us to propose to parliament that the discharge be granted now, even though i, as rapporteur, argue that we should await the outcome of the ongoing investigations by olaf and the court of auditors. i understand that the members of the committee on budgetary control have now had the opportunity to see documents showing various irregularities. if parliament does grant discharge, then you cannot say afterwards that you knew nothing. compare that with what is happening now with the discharge for the president in respect of the rents in strasbourg. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competent authority' to cover all the activities of the european union's institutions. the parallel with the committee of the regions is perfect. the only difference is that the strasbourg issue has been in the media. does it require newspaper articles for meps to react? should it not be obvious that members will decide to vote against a discharge for the committee of the regions until all the ambiguities have been cleared up? i therefore appeal to all members to think carefully about what they are voting on tomorrow. as we have not yet been able to clarify all the ambiguities, i would urge you to vote against the discharge to be granted to the committee of the regions for the implementation of the general budget of the european union for the financial year 2004. if there is a vote on the report, i would urge you to vote in favour of the amendments which i have tabled with bart staes to establish the facts of the case. we must do so if we are to take accountability seriously. the demand for accountability is at the heart of the democratic process.
for the ind/dem group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would like to begin by thanking the officials who have helped me with my work on the discharge. it has been an extraordinarily large, admirable effort. moreover, as i said, the work has been carried out against a certain backdrop. there is now evidence that strasbourg has been charging parliament an incorrect rent for a number of years. i would stress that before discharge can be granted, strasbourg must therefore give parliament access to all documents relating to the buildings concerned. if not, parliament must take the matter to olaf. in addition, parliament cannot of course sign a purchase contract until this matter has been clarified. if there had been a clear will to investigate and hold to account within the eu institutions, if the committee on budgetary control had done its job and acted as the audit body it should have, this would not have to happen. there must be a clear will to get to the bottom of irregularities constantly, not just when the media have found out about them. i would also add that the committee on budgetary control's examination of the markus ferber report 'washed' away the critical writings on the voluntary pension scheme for members of the european parliament. this system has no legal basis and no rules on what to do with the fund's deficit. it is unreasonable to put the cost of pension fund deficits on the shoulders of european taxpayers. it is also noteworthy that the members' contributions to the scheme are paid from parliament's general expenditure reimbursement scheme. it must be the case that those who are members pay their dues from their personal income. the system should be abolished.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i too would like to say a few words about the report on the discharge for our own parliament, the european parliament. it is a magnificent scandal that we have before us with the rents and strasbourg. i am of course of the opinion that discharge cannot be granted in these circumstances. it is not enough to postpone this, but we must also have complete clarity before the matter can be completed. however, this is not the only reason for denying our own institution its discharge. in fact, the report on the discharge has been 'washed' and much of the serious criticism of the pension system has been lifted. i believe that this criticism is also sufficient grounds for denying the european parliament its discharge. not only is our pension system not legally based and not paid for by our salaries, but it is poorly regulated, runs huge deficits paid for by taxpayers and has not taken account of the criticisms of the court of auditors. this question too will have to be further investigated in future work.
mr president, after listening to the rapporteur, the commissioner and our shadow rapporteur, i must admit that tonight i feel a little bit ‘netherlandish’. it might be the late hour, but i very much support what they have said here tonight. i would like to underline one thing that i think is important when we are discussing the policy for supporting innovation: there is no better precondition and no better basis for innovation than competition. it is the broad and strong policy of competition that is the best precondition for innovation. it is better to provide opportunities for thousands of entrepreneurs and innovators to work on an equal basis with each other than to support one at the cost of the other. it is very important to remember that, and everyone has pointed that out. the most important thing for the commission to do is to uphold competition and to be strong in defending small companies and small entrepreneurs. by doing so, we will achieve a climate of an innovative society in our part of the world. i would like to underline that the policy of state aid must aim for less, but better, targeted aid. support for innovations must not be an alibi or an excuse for increased state aid, because we must appreciate that innovations are a normal process of entrepreneurship, and we cannot have state aid designed to support normal entrepreneurship. state aid for innovations should be provided for those innovations and processes that otherwise would not take place in normal marketing or in normal business. it must be temporary, and it must be aimed at the clusters that are open to everyone, not excluding anyone: then we can have a good policy for innovations.
madam president, i would like to thank you. commissioner, i have to say that i am very pleased. first of all, i would like to thank gary titley for the interesting and excellent work he has done and for the interesting discussions we have had in the committee. road safety is important, and this is a road safety issue. we have a constant disaster on our european roads. every year, 42,000 people die on the roads in accidents that happen every day. that's why we have to take every precaution. one possible measure to save lives on the roads is the ecall. we know that the citizens want this. the citizens want to avoid disasters and save lives. we also know that the citizens want help to come quickly. we have seen this in other disasters besides road accidents, such as natural disasters both in europe and elsewhere in the world. rescue and help will come quickly. we can help with that with the ecall system. as early as 2001, the european parliament pointed out in a road safety resolution that we believe that eu road safety policy must also include measures for rescue, care and rehabilitation, and guidelines for better medical assistance to accident victims. this can be achieved by the ecall system. the commission has listened to what parliament said as far back as 2001. the commission now wants to introduce e112 throughout the eu, which will help road safety. this will lead to the saving of lives and the speeding up of treatment for seriously injured people in accordance with the principle of the 'golden hour', i.e. if you are treated within one hour, the chances of survival are much higher and the damage is less severe. the member states' involvement is essential for the industry to move forward. we know that there are major european car manufacturers developing the technical systems in cars. however, these systems must have a counterpart in the infrastructure, so that there is someone who receives the call. so there is a need, because the automotive industry is already developing this technology. amendment no 3 is important to move the development of ecall forward and to make the member states realise the importance of this. if the european parliament chooses to support this amendment, we will have another opportunity to put pressure on those member states which are lagging behind. equipping cars with intelligent aids must be an eu strategy for road safety work, as cars are produced for the whole european market. we know that if we have more intelligent systems, we can also introduce, for example, alcohol locks which would be very good at preventing alcohol consumption in connection with driving. finally, i would like to call for the report and this amendment to be approved.
