importance in view of the elections and the new governments, both in israel and among the palestinians. i would like to make a brief comment on iran. tomorrow there will be a meeting in vienna at the level of foreign ministers. the eu-3, javier solana, china, russia and the usa will take part in it. the meeting is to finalise an offer to iran that will allow iran to use nuclear energy exclusively for peaceful purposes. at the same time, there must be absolute certainty that nuclear energy will not be used or developed by iran for any other purpose. we have heard with interest today that my american colleague, condoleezza rice, spoke of the possibility - and i quote - of 'new and positive relations between the united states and iran'. we consider this an important signal and hope that we will be able to build a new cooperative relationship with iran with active american involvement. the direct dialogue between washington and tehran would certainly be of central importance in this context. stability and security, peace and prosperity are also the overriding common objectives of transatlantic cooperation with regard to iraq, afghanistan, haiti, sudan and the democratic republic of congo. in the area of justice and home affairs, the austrian presidency succeeded in involving the usa in the implementation of the external strategy. a number of important challenges arising from various aspects of cross-cutting policy issues continue to merit our particular attention, such as international humanitarian law, the fight against terrorism and cooperation in crisis management. there are issues on which we have clear differences of opinion, and i do not want to leave that unmentioned. the commission has already made a number of proposals. guantanamo - we were just discussing this - is one of those points. however, it is important to mention in this context that the dialogue of international law experts, which we have launched under our chairmanship, aims to reach joint positions that correspond to our common foundation of values. another controversial issue that we keep putting on the agendas of formal eu-us meetings is the visa waiver program or the international criminal court. we agree and work hand in hand on many multilateral issues, but the international criminal court is one of the areas where we differ. we also criticise the attempts of the united states to conclude agreements with third countries under article 98 of the rome statute, which run counter to the objective of universal application of the jurisdiction of the international criminal court. but let me also mention a positive aspect in this context. that is the circumstance that the us has allowed the prosecution of war crimes in sudan. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament. the commission has already taken steps to ensure that the community's financial instruments are used effectively and that the community's financial instruments are used effectively. we are each other's most important trading partners. two thirds of direct investment in the european union comes from the usa, and the level of mutual investment amounts to eur 1 500 billion. the two sides trade about one billion euros a day. the european union and the united states have a special responsibility in the world in terms of our joint share of world production. it is therefore essential that we cooperate closely on economic issues as well, and that we work together to gradually reduce trade barriers. i am sure that the european commission will take a more detailed look at this. since last year's summit, we have made great progress in implementing the jointly agreed work programme, such as the first meeting of the regulatory cooperation forum, which focuses on reducing or removing trade barriers, such as those caused by different safety regulations for cars or other goods. we are also working on a common strategy for the protection of intellectual property rights. we also welcome the proposals and recommendations of the european parliament as expressed in the reports by mr brok and mr mann. the reports also show us very clearly how much we are all pulling together. these reports also make it clear that you are as concerned about the improvement of transatlantic relations as the council and the commission. in this context, i would also like to mention the recent meeting of the transatlantic legislators' dialogue in vienna, which is an important component of our relations. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the subject of the transatlantic partnership. we do not think the time is right for that. the presidency therefore recommends that, for the time being, further concrete cooperation be built upon. the new transatlantic agenda of 1995 remains the current and valid framework for our relations. we have received clear signals from the united states that they are more reserved about further formalization of relations. we have therefore preferred to concentrate on content and concrete results and are now committed to making better use of existing structures. the regular formal meetings at senior civil servant level are well complemented by numerous other contacts and consultations in all areas of common interest. they have made the political dialogue more strategic and at the same time more substantial. we are already working closely together in many of the areas mentioned in the european parliament's reports. so a community of action exists. the next summit in june 2006 in vienna is currently being prepared by the council and the commission. our aim for the summit is to maintain the recent dynamic in transatlantic relations. we also want to give new impetus to further cooperation until the next summit, with a particular concern to make the european people better aware of the intensity, importance and usefulness of the transatlantic partnership. we have therefore decided to concentrate on a summit declaration as concise, forward-looking and substantive as possible, supplemented by a progress report on the political and economic aspects. we will also be setting out our priorities for the future at the summit. these include enhanced cooperation in the promotion of peace, democracy and human rights worldwide, a new dialogue on energy security, the conclusion of an air transport agreement and a focus on the enforcement of intellectual property rights in third countries. europe and the united states are inextricably linked not only by history but also by shared values. we can only face global challenges together. our many common economic, political and security interests far outweigh the differences that may and must exist between partners. we are, after all, indispensable partners with a shared global responsibility.
member of the commission. madam president, let me start with my perception of eu-us relations from the external relations perspective. commissioner mandelson will then complete the picture on the economic and trade relations. i shall focus, therefore, more on mr brok’s report. as this excellent report makes clear, the context of eu-us relations has changed enormously over the course of the last year. following president bush’s visit to brussels and the 2005 summit, the tensions we experienced in 2003 and 2004 have been replaced by a new spirit of constructive engagement. we came out of the 2005 summit with eight declarations on a wide range of issues which guided our collaboration. the declaration on democracy promotion shows how far our cooperation on foreign policy has come. even if there are differences from time to time – as we have just seen and the president-in-office of the council has just mentioned – we are now taking the initiative and being more proactive in our approach, rather than fire-fighting. our activity to spread democratic principles, respect for human rights and the rule of law – which mr brok highlights in his motion for a resolution – can only go so far. without the full commitment of the country concerned, democracy cannot be sustained. nevertheless, when that commitment is there, our coordinated action makes a difference, for instance – as has already been mentioned – in ukraine, where we supported the reform process; in belarus, where our common stance against the lack of reform sent a clear message to the regime; and in the middle east peace process, where coordination is essential and where it works quite well. i personally have been very closely involved in coordinating our position with the united states. the motion for a resolution mentions the need to better coordinate assistance. i am particularly pleased that the quartet took up the eu’s proposal for a temporary international mechanism to channel contributions to the basic health and social needs of the palestinian people. we are working on that basis and i hope that we will be ready by the end of the month. before handing over to mr mandelson, let me mention four issues which are highlighted in the resolution. the first is energy. both the eu and the us are faced with record high oil prices and increased dependence on foreign supplies of fossil fuels. according to current trends, the european union will have to import 70% of its energy in 2030 compared to 50% today. the us is facing a similar problem. we therefore need to take a holistic approach in tackling this problem, including involving our major partners. at next month’s eu-us summit, we intend to establish a strategic cooperation on energy, addressing the security of energy supply – including the diversification of supply routes, the enforcement of market rules, the protection of infrastructure, the promotion of alternative sources of energy, and, finally, energy sufficiency. the key is to increase predictability by creating the right market conditions and legal frameworks in both the producer and transit countries. this is the message we will bring to the eu-us summit, but also to the g8 summit and to other international partners. the second issue is visas. one of the commission’s top priorities is to resolve the issue of the us’s non-reciprocal application of visa requirements. unfortunately, it is unlikely that we will reach a solution soon, but we will continue to press for progress and will raise it again with president bush at the summit. last time, the issue was highlighted and we hoped that things would progress more quickly. in july 2006, we will issue a second report on visa reciprocity, which may examine the possibility of taking specific measures in response to any lack of progress. the third issue i wanted to tackle is china. as mr brok rightly points out in his resolution, we need to coordinate our approach to third countries, particularly china, effectively. we share the same approach: to help china move towards democracy and integrate fully into the world economy and the world market. two years ago, we took steps to coordinate ourselves better by establishing the eu-us dialogue on east asia. this has proved a very useful forum for discussing relations with china. let me address the transatlantic partnership agreement. i know that mr brok’s committee feels very strongly about this, and i fully support the desire to see transatlantic relations go from strength to strength. as you know, i have always been open to exploring the pros and cons of a contractual relationship with the united states. it is indeed strange that we do not have more formal relations with our most important partner. as the president-in-office of the council has already said, the question is whether a formal agreement would really bring much added value today to our relations. the most important thing is that the eu and the us work together for our common strategic cause. for this, we need political will, in particular, but also understanding towards each other. i feel that a treaty, for instance, would not have enabled us to bridge the differences which dogged our relations in 2003 and 2004, which were political in nature. at this moment there is no political appetite for such an agreement either in the us administration or among member states. however, this might change in the future. should attitudes change or should it become clear that there is, after all, an institutional obstacle to closer cooperation, then the time might be right to go further. let me end with a remark about the important role parliament plays in transatlantic relations. as you know, i have always been an enthusiastic supporter of interparliamentary exchanges. i am convinced that these relations benefit greatly from the links between the european parliament and the us congress. i very much encourage you to continue to build these ties. i thank you for what has already been done in the legislators’ dialogue and what might be done before the summit.
member of the commission. mr president, i want to thank erika mann for having shepherded this very impressive report through parliament with her characteristic effectiveness, dedication and attention to detail; it is very timely. the fact that the very comprehensive report on transatlantic economic relations has the input of half a dozen committees of this parliament, and that it would need at least the same number of commissioners present to discuss all the issues that are covered in it, shows how deep and intricate the eu-us economic relationship is. that relationship effectively operates at two levels. on one level, steady leadership is required to promote a further convergence of our shared economic rules and regulatory frameworks – our companies, our jobs and our competitiveness demand this. at the other level, what happens in the rest of the world also depends on whether and how the eu and the us are able to react to events together. no political relationship in our time is of greater importance to the global economy than the transatlantic partnership. the response to the rise of china and india, the pressures of globalisation, the current challenges in the energy field, the way multilateral institutions function, climate change: all these depend on the ability of europe and the united states to cooperate effectively together. nowhere is this more apparent than in the continuing wto negotiations. i am fortunate to have had a shared sense of purpose with my counterpart rob portman, and i look forward to continuing this with susan schwab, the new us trade representative – i hope she will be confirmed shortly by the senate. it is no secret that i think the european union and the united states need to bring our positions in the doha round closer together. the warning bell is ringing on the dda. the danger lights are flashing, because if the gaps are not closed in the coming weeks, we will face serious institutional, legislative and political obstacles to ending this round. the united states will shortly swing into election mode, trade authority is set to lapse, there are electoral constraints in other parts of the world too – a coming election in brazil, for example. the window on the doha round is closing rapidly. europe is ready to negotiate further; we have indicated our flexibility, and no sector is out of bounds to us. we look to our partners to express the same sense of purpose and openness. let me say a few words now on erika mann’s report itself. i am glad that it shows strong support for the work programmes adopted last november to implement the eu-us economic initiative. in the progress report which we will present to the next summit on 21 june, we will be able to list some good achievements. the eu-us action strategy for intellectual property rights enforcement in third countries will be endorsed. it will contain a number of concrete cooperation measures, especially between our customs services and embassies in selected third countries. on the high-level regulatory cooperation forum, too, i am satisfied that after two meetings in brussels and washington within a very short timeframe, we have now clearly established the forum as the place for eu and us regulators to exchange best practice. we hope very much that the benefits of increased cooperation will soon be felt by companies on both sides of the atlantic. the signature of the long-awaited air services agreement, as well as the agreement on civil aviation safety, is now expected during the second half of the year. on the air services agreement, we will have to keep pushing the us government to go ahead with the rule-making changes and resist protectionist pressure from congress. your help in this house will be most valuable to us in pursuing this goal. i welcome your continued interest in a more visionary and strategic approach – i happen to think that there is a place for vision in politics. i note your call for the 2006 eu-us summit to design a new transatlantic partnership agreement that leads to the achievement of a barrier-free transatlantic market by 2015. in my view, the eu-us economic initiative has shown that we are getting there, we are moving in that direction step by step. probably the right approach is to advance steadily on issues of mutual interest and to show – for example in the areas of intellectual property rights and regulatory cooperation – concrete successes to our citizens. we have to earn our way in our acceptance of the approach that i know is supported in the report. i think this pragmatic approach will be assured of support because in reality there is no us enthusiasm – either in the administration or in congress – for any ‘bigger bang’ eu-us scheme. on issues like investment and public procurement, which you rightly identify as areas of enormous potential – and they do have very great potential indeed – the us has not so far shown any interest in tackling bilateral barriers. on the investment side, the dubai ports fallout requires careful handling. we have now at least established a contact group to address both old and new problems, and have proposed to the us government to include an investment paragraph in the summit declaration regarding the importance of an open transatlantic investment climate. this should send a strong message to the us congress, which is currently debating the shelby bill on the tightening of the exon-florio statute. again, your continued help on this, along with that of the us congress, would be much appreciated. finally i would like to point out that we have had a very good first half of the year clearing out some important eu-us trade disputes on foreign sales corporations, on byrd, on the telecoms sanctions – i hope we can keep up the momentum in other areas. i am looking at two other disputes particularly at the moment to see whether they are susceptible to early resolution. fsc and byrd prove that the wto dispute settlement system works and that the availability of wto-compatible sanctions can be an efficient way of obtaining us implementation of wto judgments. given the good results of the wto system, i do not see at this stage the need for the kind of formal bilateral trade dispute settlement mechanism which you propose in your report, but i do not want to reject the idea completely. be assured that we will exhaust all our informal channels with the united states and try everything possible to avoid going down the expensive and time-consuming route to geneva, whenever this is avoidable. in conclusion, let me say that the transatlantic relationship is one which is too often simply taken for granted. especially at this time, what we need is renewed commitment in order to make it work better. i am grateful for your report, which has highlighted this need once again in a very timely way.
the committee on economic and monetary affairs has tabled an opinion. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i too would like to congratulate erika mann on the work she has done on the report. there is one objective in the report which i would like to emphasize in particular: the objective of achieving a common transatlantic capital market by 2010. it is an objective which in turn leads the way in other areas. already today we can see that the transatlantic economy is defined and operates by the direct investments and the flows of cooperation between businesses and research that carry trade forward. the intensity of investment and the volume of business acquisitions in both directions are what lay the foundations for a large-scale transatlantic trade, but a high level of research and innovation is also important. this is probably the best way to respond to and contribute to globalization, to contribute to a strong global economy, not just for ourselves but for the world at large. the report also touches on a number of other points, such as the importance of making it easier for companies on both sides of the atlantic to list on the various stock exchanges, of facilitating and opening up business investment to the same extent in both directions, of ensuring that reinsurers have the same opportunities, of ensuring that we have a truly common transatlantic capital market. if we can achieve that, we will also be able to achieve other objectives of transatlantic cooperation.
draftsman of the opinion of the committee on industry, research and energy. – mr president, erika mann’s report is proof not only of her skills as a rapporteur, but also of the underlying strength of transatlantic economic relations. on behalf of the committee of industry, i would like to make three very important points. firstly, as the commissioner mentioned, in the field of energy we need cooperation on efficiency sufficiency and on sustainability. secondly, in relation to research – which is extremely important – our committee is now working with the seventh framework programme. equally important is the cooperation with the institutions in the us as well as with industry. much can be done to facilitate more cooperation in this field. thirdly, we have discussed the setting-up of a body to improve discussions between industries. we would like to set up something analogous to the very successful iran-us claims tribunal, which, in a simple and elegant way, resolves thousands of disputes. we would like something similar in the future. finally, i would also like to underline the importance of parliamentary exchanges to begin discussions and to bring success on these three extremely important topics.
the ppe group is in favour of the report. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would like to thank erika mann for an excellent report and for the very close and good cooperation during the time we have been working on it. the united states is europe's most important trading partner, which we often forget. the public debate is focused on the few problems that exist in our trade relations. there is talk of crisis and conflict between europe and the united states. politically, that may be true at times, but economically, the reality is the exact opposite. i would venture to say that our trade has undergone a revolution. today, the trade volume is a staggering one billion euros a day. the deepest form of crossborder economic integration, namely mutual foreign direct investment, has increased significantly and now stands at eur 1.5 trillion. this is more than just numbers. even if we don't think about it, it gives us new opportunities in our everyday lives. the market we have, for example, has created almost seven million jobs in europe. it gives us all more services and better products. moreover, transatlantic trade is a major engine of the world economy. if our relationship is broken, it will have consequences for the whole world. trade with each other is therefore not an alternative to trade with poorer countries, but on the contrary a prerequisite and something that makes us all richer all over the world. we can't take it for granted. we must work further and integrate our economies. i therefore hope that together with the united states we can realise the idea of a barrier-free transatlantic market by 2015. yesterday i received an e-mail from the left-wing anti-globalization movement. they urged me and all the other members to vote against this report. their argument was that if this report is voted through by a majority in parliament, it will open the way to an accelerated process of increased free trade between the us and europe. i hope they are right, and that is why i intend to vote in favour of the report and in favour of a transatlantic free trade area, together with the swedish moderates.
member of the commission. mr president, i would like to thank all the members who have spoken in the debate. i welcome your contributions, whether or not they agree with the commission's view on the issues we are discussing today. i would like to say four things: firstly, the commission's attitude is of course in favour of the enlargement of the euro area. it cannot be otherwise: it is a right and an obligation of the member states, and we respect that right and demand that obligation. and we do so regardless of whether those who are due to join the euro area in the coming years are new or old member states - a distinction which, for us, belongs to the past - small or large, rich or less rich. and we are not going to invent any new conditions, mr rosati: we are applying the treaty, not just the treaty, but the whole treaty. it is the only way to ensure equal treatment for all, and i cannot imagine - and i am sure none of you want to do that - that anyone in this parliament would ask the commission to fail in its duty to be the guardian of the treaty and to fail to apply the treaty equally to each and every member state. can the treaty be amended? of course i will. this afternoon i had the opportunity to attend the debate in the presence of prime minister verhofstadt on the future constitution, the necessary changes and the future of europe. of course the treaty can be changed, and will be changed in due course. but today we have to apply the existing treaty, and you know, as i do, that it is not the commission which can change the treaty. what does the treaty say about the inflation criterion, which has been the subject of debate? it says that there is a reference value which is made up of the average inflation of the three best member states in terms of inflation. it does not say 'members of the euro area', it says clearly 'member states': all, including those with an opt-out clause; we cannot exclude any member state from the calculation of the top three states in terms of inflation. and to that average of the three we have to add 1.5%: that is the reference value. the value, the figure, which the european central bank, in the exercise of its independence, has defined as an objective identifiable with its mandate, which is to ensure respect for price stability, is quite different. it is not the same, it does not have to be the same, and the inflation target of the european central bank cannot be confused in the debate with the reference value of the inflation criterion in the treaty. they're completely different things. but the inflation criterion in the treaty says otherwise: it says that the inflation of the member state concerned must also be sustainable. some of you are referring to a small difference, and it's true. but the treaty, in the inflation criterion, in addition to the difference, in addition to the comparison between the inflation of the member state and the reference value, requires us to analyse whether that inflation of the member state in question is sustainable or not. and we cannot ignore the treaty, nor will we. and we will explain this again and again, because a rigorous analysis of the criteria is in the interest not of the present euro-area states but of the candidate member state to join the euro-area; because if this is not done, the state will pay the consequences once the decision to join the euro is irreversible. so i don't need references to the past either. if things weren't done perfectly in the past, someone is paying the price today. we learn from experience that this is why we are rational and reasonable politicians. we are applying the treaty, but we are applying it in its entirety, not just the part that interests us individually, and we are applying it by learning from experience in the interpretative margin that the treaty itself gives us. and of course, in relation to lithuania, i will not be the one to deny something that is obvious: that it has excellent economic results, that the effort made by lithuania to improve its economic situation is impressive. we all want lithuania to meet all the criteria of the treaty as soon as possible. today he has been able to meet all but one of them; let us hope that he will soon have overcome that gap. as soon as it has passed, i shall be looking forward to receiving a request from lithuania - as it may do under the treaty - in order to be able to give a favourable opinion on behalf of the commission. but do not ask the commission to put in the convergence reports anything that is contrary to the rational, clear and transparent application of the treaty.
in september 2003, the swedish people held a referendum on whether sweden should adopt the euro as its currency. the result, not surprisingly, was a resounding no. it is now two years since the eu added 10 new member states. opinion on the euro in these countries varies from country to country. however, currency exchange is a very important and serious matter. it is not just a question of replacing notes and coins, but also of giving up some of its autonomy. in the long run, you lose control of your own economic development. the junilista group believes that citizens must be consulted when important decisions are to be taken, such as on a new constitution or the euro. for the sake of democracy, it would be best if referendums were held if citizens so demand in those countries which intend to adopt the euro as a means of payment. the outcome of these must then be respected, as was the swedish people's rejection of the euro.
for the gue/ngl group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i fully support the agreement and would like to thank the rapporteur, mr niebler, for his cooperation and for his great attention to the work. despite the success of this directive, i would still like to raise a warning finger. however good the directives we can produce in the eu system, it is the concrete actions that bring about change. for example, we have had the equal pay directive for a very long time. despite this, we are still forced to live with gender pay. i regret that we did not go further with regard to parental leave. i would have liked to see a much stronger call to the member states to take this matter seriously. we have now received some of the documents, which is good, although i do not think they are sufficient and i would have liked to have gone further. however, this means that we must continue to work at all levels to get to the end.
mr president, i would like to thank mrs niebler for her excellent work. the recast directive to simplify and modernise the issue incorporates the relevant points in this area. standardisation and definitions are clearly important but, most importantly, what we need now are sanctions. to reconcile work and family life is one of the most urgent issues of the europe of today and the europe of the future. there is today no country in the union that reaches the reproductive levels necessary to avoid extinction. women want to work. if forced to choose between working and having a family, an increasing number of women choose to work. one of the most obvious forms of discrimination against women is the gender pay gap. for more than 30 years we have had a directive dealing with this, but how much has the situation changed during that time? not at all. there has been no improvement in this area whatsoever. will the recast directive change this? it remains to be seen. to put the issue of reconciling work and family life as a top priority, as commissioner ferrero-waldner stated, is very good, but we desperately need sanctions. nothing less than the future of europe is at stake.
mr president, as we have heard, many member states still discriminate systematically against roma, and romani women suffer this even more than romani men. reproductive rights is a key issue. as stated in the járóka report, there are recent reported cases of sterilisation in two member states. this is beyond what should be possible in the eu of today. the only reason that this has not been more widely condemned is precisely because it affects romani women. in this area, too, we need sanctions. i should also like to underline that it is necessary to collect data on the basis of gender and ethnicity. only through this will we be able to prove beyond doubt the true scope of the problem of discrimination and marginalisation. finally, mrs járóka has proven to us all how important the contribution of romani women can be.
for the gue/ngl group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. in peace movements around the world, women are often the initiators and the majority. they've realized that bombs don't bring peace. on the contrary, military violence breeds hatred and demands for revenge and suicide bombers. an escalation of military violence also means tremendous, horrific abuses against women and children, as this report also shows. women in the peace movement have shown a different and better way, a way of conflict resolution and peace-building. i think that the report largely points to this different and better way, for which i thank the rapporteur in particular. however, paragraphs 14 to 20 point to a risk of further militarisation. i therefore believe that these points should be deleted so that we can have a report that is in favour of women and peace, not militarism.
in writing. there is no doubt that smes play a very important role in the economy of countries. in developing countries where there are no large enterprises, it is of course more important to ensure that there is an institutional framework which enables smes to operate. it is not, however, the task of the european parliament to dictate the conditions that must prevail in the developing countries. each state, both in the eu and in the rest of the world, has the right to own and shape its own development. that is why i voted against this report today.
in writing. as at the first reading of this case, the junilist votes against the report on grounds of principle. we fully support equal treatment for women and men in the world of work. this is a key area which is being dealt with with merit by international organisations such as the ilo. we do not believe that the eu should regulate these kinds of issues in long-term reports, which have the character of political programmes. the eu should not control working time, parental leave and other important national issues. this is to the advantage of the member states, which are able to do so independently, in accordance with already adopted international agreements.
in writing. we have voted today in favour of the report on the european year of intercultural dialogue. cultural exchanges are of course enriching and important for increasing understanding between citizens. however, there are details in the report which we are sceptical about. we do not think that days, weeks and years have any practical significance for citizens for one reason or another, but that they may be some kind of objective description for the actions of the institutions. as always, we are sceptical of centrally designed campaigns and opinion-forming and note that the report tightens up the commission's proposals in this area. a 'no' vote would mean more money for the union's institutions and for public relations campaigns.
in writing. the junilist believes that the whole idea of the european year is unnecessary and not something that the eu taxpayers should be funding. the commission's proposal for a regulation on the european year of intercultural dialogue is a very good one. the european parliament's committee on culture also wants, incredibly, to increase the eu's share of the contribution to the type of activities related to national events and initiatives from 50 per cent to 80 per cent of the contribution (amendment 33). i am voting against the report as a whole.
in writing. i am voting in favour of the report because the main objective of combating discrimination is very positive and necessary. i am voting against an increase in the budget and against amendment no 18, which is particularly unfortunate in its neo-colonial desire to export opinions and values.
in writing. the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a decision on the introduction of the euro. the question is thus settled for sweden for the foreseeable future. we believe that the eu should not test the swedish people's opinion by taking a position for or against a possible exception clause.
in writing. in the vote on the euro, i have abstained on the question of whether the swedish referendum should be regarded as an exception. the claim is based on the mistaken notion that sweden will be forced to accept the single currency contrary to the result of the referendum. of course, that is not the case. however, this demand goes too far, since it is based on the unreasonable idea that individual countries should be able to opt out of parts of the common treaties by means of a referendum. the consistent application of such a principle is, of course, unthinkable.
in writing. i voted for amendment no 9 because i believe that sweden should request an exemption after the 2003 swedish vote on the euro. however, i believe that it is up to sweden to apply for the derogation. the eu should not automatically consider sweden's refusal to grant a derogation as equivalent to a waiver.
in writing. i am voting against the amendments on how countries should technically implement the changeover to the euro. however, i am voting against proposals which seek to maximise the fight against inflation and to disregard other objectives. i am voting in favour of the report because of the massive criticism of the way lithuania has been treated.
in writing. we swedish social democrats are against parliament sitting in strasbourg and wonder why parliament has set aside a significant amount of money to buy buildings in strasbourg. this issue is particularly controversial, as the investigation into irregularities in the dealings with parliament's buildings in strasbourg is still ongoing.
in writing. we have voted in favour of the report on the estimates of revenue and expenditure for the european parliament for the financial year 2007. we appreciate the ambition to treat the administration's proposals rigorously. our approval does not mean that we support the budget in all its aspects. for example, we are still sceptical about the european parliament's information activities, which tend to take over the role of members and political groups. we also do not think it is justified to build a house of europe in brussels or for the european parliament to buy the buildings in strasbourg.
in writing. this report could have contained much more of a general interest in the budget of the european parliament. the report should make it clear that under no circumstances should the budget of the european parliament cover the deficit of the pension fund of the members of the european parliament. the report should also have included a statement that the european parliament's generous travel allowance system should be reformed and that only actual travel costs and nothing else should be paid to them for their travels. the report calls for an increase in appropriations for political groups and european parties by eur 4 million. i cannot support this, which is one of the reasons why i am voting against the report as a whole.
in writing. those who vote against paragraph 4 (€50 million for the purchase of buildings in strasbourg and €25 million for propaganda) do not affect the budget but only try to hide the waste from the electorate. i am voting against the report, however, because i do not want the money to be wasted.
in writing. globalisation offers a number of opportunities, not least in reducing poverty. trade and technological cooperation both help to strengthen the economies of poor countries. but there are risks and problems. the poorest countries are often unable to participate in economic cooperation because of capacity shortages, etc., and risk falling further behind. furthermore, environmental legislation at international level and in many poor countries is characterised by major shortcomings, which is causing waste products, plunder and unsustainable exploitation of various natural resources, such as forests and fisheries, to increase rapidly and to be reinforced by the rapidly growing trade exchange. these problems must be tackled urgently if the benefits of globalisation are not to be turned into disadvantages.
in writing. the report deals with the situation of roma women in the eu and the complex and multiple forms of discrimination against them in the member states of the union. junilista is committed to ensuring that roma women have access to better protection of their reproductive and sexual health. the basic position of junilistan is that both gender equality and ethnic discrimination issues should be given priority, as they are both necessary and desirable. but that does not mean that the eu should do so. the junilista is convinced that this work is best done at national level. the member states display a great diversity of culture and traditions. we therefore believe that a more pluralistic and flexible approach to the achievement of the goal of equal opportunities for women and men can be taken at national level. i have therefore decided to vote against the report as a whole.
in writing. we have abstained from the vote on the report on the situation of women in armed conflict. we are, of course, as appalled by the horrors of war and the human suffering caused by war and terrorism as the rapporteur ever was. however, the report's premise leads to a strange line of reasoning in which the suffering of the men's group and the women's group, as well as the guilt and responsibility, are graded to fit the requirements of quotas for women in different contexts. in the quest to prevent conflict and stop terrorism, that must be the goal, and the best methods to achieve it must be used. the report's many proposals, however benign, risk shifting the focus from the aims of peace work to its forms. the quota of women in peacekeeping and peace-making forces and in peace negotiations risks undermining the objective of preventing suffering.
in writing. even though the report as a whole is progressive concerning the situation of women in armed conflicts and their role in the reconstruction and democratic process in post-conflict countries, we have decided to abstain due to the paragraphs which state support for the european security and defence policy.
mr president, i would like to thank you. two weeks ago, the police in sweden raided a 50-man operation to shut down a website that facilitated file sharing. a large number of servers were seized from a number of companies that had nothing to do with the business, and the business of these small companies was shut down. in addition, a number of persons were forced to submit dna samples on suspicion of a very minor crime. even more serious, a political opponent of the music industry who had forced this raid was having his website taken down in the middle of our campaign and was unable to engage in the debate that had been openly being conducted, namely to advocate for liberal legislation on file sharing for private use without profit motive. the music industry has always tried to stop technological developments, such as cassettes, digital music players, writable cds. they've always been wrong. all these new inventions have always led to increased sales. that's why it's a shame if the police help stop the debate.
for the gue/ngl group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. mr commissioner, i would like to thank you for your reply. in the context of the hearing held on 8 march, organised by the equality committee, representatives of the commission made a number of promises to the commission on measures to prevent the abhorrent sex slave trade. today we are forced to admit that not many of these promises have been fulfilled. the world cup is in full swing and the brothel business is booming. women and children are brought into the eu from other parts of the world for sexual exploitation. the united nations estimates that each year four million people are trafficked within or between countries for sexual exploitation. they are transported around, sold and bought for consumption by human traffickers, sex tourists, porn producers and so-called common men. young women from poor backgrounds are lured by promises of work, livelihood, and a better life. then they are stripped of their ids, kept in confinement, rarely know where they are in the world, hear only foreign languages, are beaten, threatened, and above all, they are to serve their bodies in any way the customers may want. prostitution is one of the most profitable activities in the eu, along with arms and drugs trafficking. there is only one way to stop this trade, and that is to reduce the demand for sexual services. reducing demand means making the buyer, the customer, visible and criminalizing him. without customers, there is no demand. if we do, we will have taken the decisive steps to stop this terrible trade. the links between the legalisation of prostitution and the increase in the number of victims of trafficking need to be thoroughly investigated. the sex buyer does not care where the prostitute comes from or under what circumstances she works as a prostitute, but she is reduced to a body that can be bought. women in the sex trade are victims of men's violence against women. i don't make any moral judgments about prostitution. these are strictly political issues. no one will have to prostitute themselves. for example, a 1999 study revealed that 80 percent of the women in in writing. the initiatives of austria, finland and sweden seem to be commendable. it is in principle positive to facilitate the acceleration of the transfer of sentenced persons to a particular state with which they are in any way connected and where it is considered likely that optimal social reintegration can take place. it is worth noting, however, that there is already a convention and an additional protocol of the council of europe on the transfer of sentenced persons. the junilist also considers that judicial and police cooperation should be intergovernmental. these issues are to be decided by unanimity in the council and subject to the control of national parliaments, not supranational institutions such as the european parliament. the junilist has therefore voted against the report as amended by parliament, a report which contains, among other things, writings on the promotion of a european criminal code.
in writing. we want to emphasise that junilistan is against the promotion of drug-free activities. we have therefore voted against texts which go in this direction. any handling of narcotic preparations outside of medical use is harmful and something that society should not concern itself with. as regards the european centre for the control of drugs and drug addiction, we consider that this centre is not justified in principle, given the wide disparities in the policies and approaches to the fight against drugs in the member states. we have therefore voted against the report on strengthening the role of the centre.
in writing. we voted in favour of the resolution as a whole but have some important objections. firstly, the risk of pandemics should not be exaggerated. in the event of an epidemic, the eu should have a coordinating role. the main responsibility lies with the member states. the resolution places too much of the responsibility on the eu. there is certainly a need for cooperation on vaccines in special situations. this may involve the use of the vaccine where it is needed most. however, we are wondering whether the eu central vaccine stockpiles proposed by the rapporteur are the most efficient way of working. we also see many practical problems with this.
in writing. this report deals with a highly transnational issue. there is therefore good reason for the eu countries to coordinate the measures taken and the political decisions taken. we have therefore voted in favour of the report as a whole. however, we would like to stress that isolated action by the eu is insufficient to address the challenges posed by the global influenza pandemic. it is therefore of the utmost importance that the eu acts in cooperation with the world health organisation (who). we question certain points in the report, such as the need for the commission to develop a comprehensive communication strategy together with the european centre for disease prevention and control (ecdc). we assume that the eu countries are fully capable of communicating with their citizens on their own, without the assistance of eu bodies. we are also opposed to the establishment of a common emergency stockpile of pandemic vaccines in the community. we believe that internationally recognised organisations such as the who should be working on global health issues. we criticise the fact that the european parliament is thus exploiting a major political issue to extend the international political role of the eu.
in writing. the report deals with the consequences of the ecj judgment c-176/03 which gave the eu the power to adopt the necessary penal sanctions under the first pillar to ensure the full effect of the provisions of the first pillar, in this case concerning the environment. however, there is no explicit division of powers in the area of criminal law in the ec treaty. given the considerable importance of criminal law for the sovereignty of the member states, it should not be permissible to implicitly transfer that competence to the community. furthermore, articles 135 and 280 of the ec treaty expressly lay down that member states are responsible for the application of national criminal law and the administration of justice. however, the commission has indicated that this transfer of criminal law to community competence can be of any scope and in principle cover all the areas covered by the ec treaty. the court's interpretation of the treaties thus brings together the pillars of a single institutional framework, which was a feature of the european constitution that has now been rejected. the junilista strongly opposes this erosion of national self-determination and has therefore voted against the report.
in writing. i am voting for mr bondes's motion for an alternative resolution because he criticises the ecj for trying to use environmental law as a springboard for the eu to take over large parts of criminal law. i am not, however, categorically opposed to all forms of criminal law for transnational environmental crimes. this may sometimes be required to access, for example, ships at sea that are destroying the environment.
in writing. junilistan is opposed to all forms of discrimination, whether based on gender, race, ethnic origin, religion or belief, disability, age or sexual orientation. we have therefore decided to support the report in its entirety to show our support for the fundamental right of all citizens of the eu member states to be free from discrimination, even if we do not agree with all the texts and have therefore voted against them.
in writing. although there are points where the rapporteur wants to give the eu too much power and where national legal practices are questioned, the positive parts of this report consider non-discrimination and equal opportunities for all. the report is good on the whole, and that is why i am voting for it.
in writing. we have voted today in favour of jo leinen's motion for a resolution on the next step in the period of reflection and analysis on the future of europe. as we have said before, however, we do not believe that the charter of fundamental rights should be legally binding.
in writing. the french and dutch referendums have led to the defeat of the draft constitution. if there can be no doubt about it. two countries have voted no in referendums, and with large majorities and high turnout. the fact that the eu's power elite is now trying to explain away this is a democratic scandal. politicians and high officials are now openly discussing how to get around these currency issues. they have the audacity to interpret the votes as a dissatisfaction with the incumbent governments of both countries. they start counting the number of countries that have said yes, even though it's clear that all countries have to agree to the proposal. the gap between the political elite and the people in eu affairs has rarely been so clear. all indications are that a 'no' vote would have been taken in germany too if there had been a referendum there. the same is true in the united kingdom, sweden, denmark, and perhaps several other countries. democratic rashes are just a setback for those who have lost it because they lack popular support. let us instead create a new treaty that emphasises eu intergovernmental cooperation.

mr president, i would like to thank you. thank you, caroline lucas, for a good report that is clear. it is better to have action on quotas than free and complicated allocation. it is important that we decide clearly on separate aviation trading systems. this makes sense because the climate change impact of aviation is two to four times greater than the carbon dioxide impact alone. the commission considers that the aid is compatible with the internal market if it is granted through state resources. aviation is a rapidly growing and flying tax haven. the exemption from vat and fuel tax is the most egregious example. this benefits the wealthiest in society, who fly the most. paradoxically, then, low-income earners would benefit most from higher emissions taxes. this would reduce the need for government revenue in other areas where low-income earners have a larger market share, such as railways. i welcome the support of the conservative group for separate systems, and i expect the social democrats to stand up for the economically weak too.

in writing. i have decided to abstain when the report on the proposal for a council decision on the system of the european communities' own resources is voted. as problems and opportunities become increasingly transnational, the european union is becoming an increasingly important forum for solving these problems and making the most of these opportunities. while the process of better regulation can hopefully clear up a lot of issues that i consider to be national and why not regional, i see the majority of european citizens as important areas that need to be dealt with jointly. these include the environment, research and development and energy, where we can create a healthier environment with more jobs and long-term growth. that is why i am in principle in favour of a new system of financing for the community. however, i do not think that the current contributions from the member states, which are based more on the ability to negotiate rebates than on reflecting the needs of the countries in the eu, should be replaced by a new form of own resources based on energy, vat or corporate profits as proposed by the commission. it doesn't mean more justice. i do, however, support a large part of the report we are voting on. of course, greater transparency and simplification are welcome.
in writing. we moderates want aviation to be included in the european emissions trading system. this is an efficient and fair way of internalising the environmental costs of aviation, in accordance with the polluter pays principle. we regret that the swedish government has chosen to introduce a special solution with air taxes in sweden. this distorts competition and weakens sweden's competitiveness, while providing little or no environmental benefits compared to the introduction of aviation emissions trading. the report's call for a separate emissions trading system means that we moderates cannot support it. with a separate system, we risk achieving a smaller overall reduction in greenhouse gas emissions, and at a much higher cost than necessary. we moderates believe that emissions trading should be introduced but in the right way. the dramatic fall in airfares in europe is fundamentally a positive development that creates prosperity, freedom of movement and connections between people. we regret that the rapporteur and the majority of this parliament seem to be of the opposite opinion. we therefore do not share the report's call for further tax increases of various kinds on the air.
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, the feelings of those who lose their jobs need no explanation. it does not take much imagination to understand the hopelessness, the fear of the future and the financial worries they go through, day after day. in that, parliament stands united: across the political parties, across the european countries there is support for the european social model and the support of the workers and the unemployed. when it comes to what concretely should be done, our opinions differ greatly. i firmly believe it is a serious mistake to try to protect jobs by preventing modernisation, restructuring and globalisation. it may be good for today, but it is disastrous for tomorrow. our answer to globalisation must not be evasion and conservatism but to embrace it and prepare our citizens for it. it must not stop restructuring from happening but make the transition for those affected easier. that is the starting point for the alliance of liberals and democrats in parliament when addressing the challenges presented to us by industrial restructuring and globalisation. we believe that the european union can and should play a role, but one of facilitating transition rather than one of obstruction. we are not against change. we are for change. otherwise, in ten years we will have all our jobs in china or other nice regions outside europe. we are for cooperation, education and economic growth, but they should be brought about with social and environmental responsibility. that is our task in parliament. there are a number of issues where we need european politics, not only to keep but to create new jobs all over the union. at the very heart of that we find the lisbon agenda. first and foremost it is about strengthening the individual. europe can help by encouraging and funding programmes for lifelong learning, language skills and cultural exchange, to mention a few examples. they help prepare for a labour market in transition where few jobs can be guaranteed for life. there are no jobs for life anymore but there could well be work for life. secondly, it is about strengthening european industry to be competitive in the 21st century. by giving access to an even greater internal market supporting european research and innovation, we open new opportunities that will eventually create new jobs. thirdly, to accomplish that, we need strengthened social dialogue addressing issues of change such as the need for companies to facilitate transition for those losing their jobs, and environmental rules for sustainable economic development. it has a lot to do with european leadership, about explaining the challenges we are facing and about doing what we can to facilitate the journey into the future by preparing individuals and businesses; but not about making false promises, protecting jobs and hoping for short-term political gains. that is the liberal answer to the challenges of globalisation and industrial reform.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i shall concentrate my remarks on the principal aspects of restructuring. i would like to say, however, that i support the portuguese workers in their efforts to maintain and develop their employment. i also know from previous experience that, as martin schulz mentioned, gm has not always been good at social dialogue, for example in the case between the german and the swedish factory, where workers from different countries were pitted against each other. that's an attitude i don't like. i share the view that restructuring is not new. we've always lived with restructuring. in my town, there was once a rubber factory that no longer exists. almost everyone worked there. restructuring will continue. in some cases, the restructuring is poorly managed. this leads to job losses and the loss of regions. but there are also good examples of restructuring, where it has meant that new jobs have been created and that regions have been able to survive and even develop with more long-term employment. what's the secret? yeah, there are a few things. it's long-term, it's participatory and it's socially responsible. firstly, the long term. companies that have no long-term development of their own on how to cope with global competition and that take subsidies only for the short term and then leave the region, i don't give much for. in addition, you must have a share in the most important asset you have in your company, namely your employees. employees are the most important resource and should not only be informed and consulted when changes occur. they must be part of the restructuring process, be able to participate and influence it. then you don't have to go out on the streets and protest. then you can create the new jobs. it is a question of taking social responsibility at an early stage, of training staff to be able to meet the long-term challenges of the future and of the regions, because it is often companies that play a major role in regions where social responsibility is required. as for the automobile industry, it has become increasingly globalised and includes fewer and fewer companies. it's going to need a restructuring. at european level, we can do a number of things. we can strengthen the works councils, as pierre jonckheer said, and the directives on works councils. we can work on information and consultation. i believe that we need framework agreements between the various sectors and trade unions at european level to cope with the changes in the future. it's becoming more important. we have to live with restructuring, but it can be made successful if employees are involved and if companies take social responsibility. madam president, i would like to thank you. commissioner, what is it? i'm all for fair trade. for me, it's free trade, a trade without tariffs and quotas in an environment where free enterprise and property rights are respected. this report takes a different view in some parts and in some places it seems to be almost the opposite. the report mixes two different kinds of fair trade. when companies and organizations themselves define what they think is fair and then offer the consumer products that are made according to those criteria, that is consumer power and something very good. the second is when politicians get involved in a voluntary business relationship and set terms and prices that they consider fair and then call that fair trade. to me, that's socialism and something really bad. the report proposes to give fair trade companies their own legal basis and to set policy objectives and criteria. it's to transform the important power of consumption into some kind of socialist planned economy. i am surprised that so many members of this house think this is desirable. although fairness is an expression of consumer power and something very good, i would like to conclude by urging everyone, politicians and citizens, to continue to be critical consumers, especially in relation to fairly labelled goods. they often give more money and influence to organizations that are guided by leftist ideals and resistance to free trade than to the very poor in underdeveloped countries. tropicana and dole's orange juice probably do more to help development and poverty reduction than, say, this one from oxfam. the european parliament's headless embrace of oxfam is a good example of how crazy it can be when we are uncritical in embracing fair products. the oxfam orange juice that is sold in this house, for example, comes from cuba. until someone explains to me how it can be called fair trade when i buy government juice and my money goes to a communist dictatorship, i demand that oxfam's products be removed from the entire range of parliament's products, because that is not fair trade.
in writing. we abstain from voting on this report, as it presents parliament with an unsolvable dilemma. on the one hand, it is implementing an international treaty and voting against it would simply reinforce the commission’s position. on the other hand, we cannot support the introduction of ever more legislation intruding on privacy without further evidence that a massive surveillance of innocent citizens increases safety or is helpful in preventing acts of terror. it has been shown that money flows for the funding of terrorism are increasingly going underground, making use of cash and transferring money using couriers. further, no matter what steps are taken to protect privacy, the temptation to use these for other purposes by authorities will be great and so far no-one has created a register that does not leak information. we are also deeply concerned about the effects on ngos promoting democracy and human rights in undemocratic regimes. an extensive register, even though we are told it will not be used that way, will undoubtedly impair their activities. in abstaining, we also urge the parliament to start discussing a comprehensive policy on protection of privacy and civil liberties. the present course, where liberties are curtailed step by step, must come to an end.

on behalf of the verts/ale group. – mr president, it is clear that the wto crisis is deepening. there is a risk that the failure at the wto ministerial meeting last week to bring members closer to trade deals represents another step towards the final betrayal of the very idea of a development round. some have even said that the meeting was counterproductive, with an atmosphere even more difficult than before, but perhaps the silver lining is that developing countries are united in insisting that rich countries put development at the heart of the process as a matter of course and deliver on their extremely long-standing promises. more than two thirds of the wto membership – developing and least-developed countries – declared that they were prepared to make a deal, but that industrial countries have to make the greatest contribution to show real leadership. i am glad the eu signalled that it was prepared to move closer to the g20 position, particularly by lowering domestic support, but there are still loopholes in the eu offer that would undermine the potential of a true development deal. the us offer is of course still far removed from what is necessary to stop dumping and protect food security. as regards the special and differential treatment in agriculture, the development box, it is not clear whether the commission will support the g33 request for a significant percentage of products to be covered in order to guarantee food security. i would be grateful if commissioner mandelson could set out the eu position and hopefully distinguish it from the extremely damaging proposals from the us. on nama, the commission is still demanding too much extreme liberalisation, which will threaten the very survival of some developing countries’ local manufacturing and it is hardly in the spirit of the development round to demand action on applied tariff levels rather than, as usual, bound tariff levels. it looks very unlikely that we will have an agreement in july and the timetable is too hurried. we should have a reflection period on the reasons for the failure of the doha round. we must learn lessons from this and ensure things are better processed through a much reformed wto and obtain a clear understanding of the role of trade and delivering sustainable development. perhaps in that way we can devise a development round generally worthy of the name.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to begin by thanking commissioner peter mendelsohn for the openness you showed to the four of us who made up the parliament's delegation in geneva last weekend. it was very rewarding and i think we had a very good working relationship. there is an intrinsic value in getting a new wto agreement. trust is needed, both in the wto and in countries have strong pragmatic reasons to take a further step. in the united states, for example, even the most dogmatic agricultural protectionists should realize that it is better to give up their (applause from the left)
mr president, i would like to thank you. protectionists are reluctant to admit that their proposals are protectionist, but often conceal their intentions. mandatory origin marking is one example. i think it is time to dispel some of the myths that the protectionists are spreading about this particular proposal. the first myth is that consumers demand it, but the truth is that most people don't care. the advocates rely on the commission's internet consultation on the issue, but they do not mention that 96,7% of the responses came from a single country italy, where the industry is driving the issue. the second myth is that labelling provides relevant information to consumers, but in the global marketplace, products and components are often of different countries of origin. it may be directly misleading to indicate a country of origin. the third myth is that labelling strengthens european competitiveness, but new technical barriers to trade to protect european industry have nothing to do with competitiveness. it is only strengthened by open markets in a good business climate. the fourth myth is that mandatory labelling would provide more consumer protection, as if safety were a matter of geography. it may at most be relevant for agricultural products, which are excluded from this proposal. the last myth is that it is a cheap proposal, but the commission's own impact assessment finds, for example, that imported clothes are on average €11.5 more expensive, and a pair of shoes €2 more expensive. the cost will be in the millions, if not billions. in my view, origin labelling is a poorly concealed protectionism which will be expensive and all just to impose new non-tariff barriers on foreign competitors. why else would there be a clear rejection of the introduction of 'ec' labelling, something which is dismissed as creating unnecessary costs for european producers? it is now forbidden to require origin labelling from other member states, because it is protectionism. to demand that from third countries without acknowledging that this is precisely protectionism is, in my view, hypocritical.
madam president, i would like to thank you. i would like to begin by congratulating karin riis-jørgensen on her report. i would like to stress that this proposal does not constitute a european tax policy or that the european union is infringing the sovereignty of the member states in deciding on taxes, but that even with this proposal it will be entirely up to the individual member states to decide how much they want to tax motoring. what we are deciding here is the structure and the system of taxation, because it facilitates the internal market. this will make it easier to buy and sell cars across national borders. the beneficiaries of this are basically the individual consumer. i would like to say to our danish colleague, mr auken, that what is important to us is to have new, modern cars. there's nothing more environmentally dangerous than the old car park. new technology is good for the environment. we shall do everything we can to ensure that cars are sold and modernised rapidly in europe. i would like to stress that it is good that karin riis-jørgensen has also included in her report a proposal for tax incentives for new types of engines, such as hybrid cars or cars powered by other fuels. it also provides an opportunity for more rapid development in terms of increased environmental considerations in motoring. a number of years ago, in sweden, we introduced a special incentive for the use of catalysts which was very quickly adopted and led to very rapid reductions in emissions. it's an opportunity to move forward on that path. it is also important, however, that this focus on the taxation of cars will give the european car industry an opportunity to develop more quickly in terms of environmental considerations. it also means a more rapid modernisation. better cars in europe are good for the environment, but it can also result in a better and stronger car industry.
on behalf of the alde group . – madam president, daphne is the young woman in greek mythology who was chased by the god apollo. his intention was to rape her, but he failed. the moment apollo touched her, daphne was transformed into a laurel tree and she was saved from being raped. in the european parliament, the name daphne stands for an important initiative. ten years ago, in the late summer of 1996, europe exploded with the knowledge of what came to be known as the dutroux affair. the discovery of the bodies of a number of missing girls in a town in belgium raised serious questions about what we do to protect women and children from those who abuse or exploit them for profit. in april 1997, a large number of representatives from ngos, the european parliament, the european commission, law enforcement agencies and other experts, gathered in brussels for a hearing on these subjects. maybe the most important result of that hearing was a commitment from the commission to launch the daphne initiative. what it amounts to is to put force behind the words; to give the campaign against violence a substantial budget. the idea behind it was simple enough: to provide financial support for projects which would bring together ngos from at least two member states to cooperate in research, data collection and analysis, good practice identification and sharing, training exchange and networking, awareness-raising and information campaigns, but also direct action to support victims of violence and produce guidelines and protocols. it seems simple enough, and who could possibly be against it? but, as we all know, our society is characterised by a systematic devaluation of the things women say, do and decide. that gender power structure affects everything and is at its most visible in the phenomenon of men’s violence against women. because of that, the daphne initiative has been under constant threat ever since it started. it should be obvious that we should not mix the fight against violence with the fight against drugs; they are simply two different areas. it is the commission that runs the daphne project, but in this house it is lissy gröner who should receive praise and support when we discuss the ‘reborn’ daphne initiative. she is one of the pioneers. of course, there is still a long way to go before we reach the necessary zero tolerance towards violence against women and children. the long-term importance of the daphne initiative is the way it can affect grassroots organisations, deeply embedded in all member states of the union. zero tolerance is the goal and daphne is one of the means of achieving it.
for the gue/ngl group. president. - the debate is closed. i would first like to thank rapporteur lissy gröner for her excellent report and for her commitment to these issues. i would also like to thank my colleagues on the committee on equality for their commitment to combating violence against women. violence against women and children is a priority for the daphne iii programme. men's violence against women occurs in all social groups. it is not possible to single out specific groups, specific factors such as alcohol and drug abuse. violence against women and children is a fact of life. although survey after survey confirms that violence permeates all social groups, many still refuse to see the truth that violence is everywhere. there are still myths about alcohol or drugs being involved. to combat violence against women, we must dispel these myths that it is 'those', 'the others' or 'the abusers' who abuse women and children. it may just as well be our neighbor, workmate, or relative who is the abuser. this is one of the reasons why the daphne programme has to be divided into two separate programmes, namely a specific programme for the prevention and combating of violence against women and children and a specific programme for the prevention of drug abuse and information. if a joint programme were to be developed for these two different social problems, the myth that there is a link between abuse and violence by men against women would continue to be reinforced. so it leads to the belief that if we solve the problem of addiction, we solve the problem of violence. however, this is not the case. both are societal problems that need to be solved, but they require different measures. i therefore note with appreciation that the commission has accepted a division into separate programmes. the gue/ngl group fully supports the report. we believe it is particularly important that trafficking in women and children for sexual purposes should also be included in the programme. this is important because everything must be done to combat the modern-day slave trade. however, it is also important to highlight that this slave trade is another expression of the violence against women and children. there are two concepts in the report which i would like to rename. firstly, there is the concept of 'domestic violence'. i think we should call it what it is, namely, male violence against women. violence against women does not occur only within the walls of the home. women who live in abusive relationships live with this physical and mental torture around the clock. the second concept i question is the concept of the 'private sphere'. there is no reason to make a division between a public and a private sphere. violence against women is a criminal act, whether it occurs in public or in private.
