for the pse group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would like to begin by thanking the two rapporteurs who have done a very good job together. it has become a good report. i am often asked at home in sweden whether there really is a european social model and whether we do not have different models. i would say that it is true that our social systems are different, but that they also have a great deal in common, and that is what the european social model is all about. we all have systems that are built on solidarity with people who become unemployed, sick or injured at work. we all have a public sector where we fund what is common, to varying degrees. we also have the social partners and civil society that play a big role in the process of shaping our societies. those are the common denominators. if the social model is based on shared values within different systems, we can learn from each other in the process we are in. it is not that the social model or the various social systems are immutable. as so many others have said in this debate, they must constantly change, not in terms of values but in terms of the systems themselves. the two major challenges now are demographic change and globalisation. the demographic development means that we must also show solidarity with the next generation. the next generation should not have to pay an unreasonable amount of what it produces to us who are retiring and needing a lot of health care. that is why we must create sustainable pension systems. however, we must also ensure that we create a working environment which allows people to remain in the labour market, which means, among other things, reasonable working hours. we must have skills development that also allows the older workforce to be part of the change in terms of skills development and the like, and thus remain in work. that way we can get more people into the workforce. we may also need to take in more people from outside europe in the future, which is probably what will happen. there are two ways to go about globalization. one way is to adapt to the competitive economies of china and india, that is, in terms of wages, in terms of labour market conditions, etc. the other way is that we actually try to compete by having well-trained personnel, by research and development, etc., that make our workforce and the products it produces the best in the world. however, it presupposes security in the labour market, since it implies change and restructuring. people in a changing labour market must feel secure, 'safe in the face of change' - i know the finnish presidency calls it 'flexicurity' rather than 'flexicurity', but it is the same thing. if you are safe, you are also part of the work of change and development in europe. we can then develop social systems into a productive factor in the process of change.
thank you so much for the answer, which i thought was good. nor do i see any contradiction between competition policy and employment policy. i just think that perhaps more emphasis should be placed on employment policy in the future. it is important that we have a strong industrial policy in europe in order to preserve employment opportunities. however, in the light of demographic trends, it is also important that employment opportunities in the sector are of high quality and that we invest in skills development, among other things, so that young people seek employment in the industrial sector. in view of the competition, recruiting new labour should not be difficult. the solution of this problem is a very important task in view of demographic developments.
mr president, this spring i went with a group of european journalists to follow the seal hunt. our car was run off the road, our helicopter was grounded and we were forced to remain in our hotel by an angry mob. despite all this we managed to witness the cruel hunting of very young seal pups which left many suffering on the ice. climate change and the receding ice threaten their survival because they need ice cover for their nursing. europe is the main market for seal products. i thank with all my heart all the colleagues who signed this resolution. the last time the eu made a reduction in its market for seal products, in the 1980s, it led to a virtual closing-down of the hunt. this demand is an extension of the ban and will finally give eco-tourism the chance to replace mass slaughter. i urge the commission to propose legislation as quickly as possible in order to scale back next year's hunt, which will start in march. i thank all of you very much. before the important summit this weekend. the eu's relations with china have become increasingly comprehensive, not just in terms of trade policy. a more strategic approach to this relationship is therefore desirable. china is a growing economy and an increasingly important trading partner. we liberal democrats see trade as an important way for countries to get closer together, to increase contacts and exchanges. trade also leads in the long run to greater transparency, better regulation, less corruption and eventually, we believe, democracy. trade has also led to economic growth that has lifted millions of chinese out of poverty. then, of course, we can also discuss how that growth should be distributed. china has growing ambitions on the international stage. that is good, but it also means that the country must take on greater international responsibility and be a responsible partner. we believe that china can play an important role in relations with north korea, but we cannot accept that china uses its un veto to stop global intervention to end the genocide in darfur or to pressure iran to open its nuclear programme to international scrutiny. china, with its size, for a strategic partnership with many concrete elements. we welcome this and hope that the european parliament can play a critical but also constructive role in this work.

in writing. the junilist deeply regrets the current situation in lebanon. in war, it is always the defenseless and the innocent who pay the highest price. the conflict in lebanon is a textbook example of violence begetting violence, and we strongly condemn all acts of violence on both sides. the report as a whole contains many good proposals and ideas, but we believe that the un should be the highest authority in this area. the report also touches on a sensitive foreign policy issue on which the governments of the member states are divided. we believe that it is up to the governments of each member state to take a position on this matter. we believe that the whole issue is a matter for the un and not for the eu. the june list therefore abstains.
in writing. we have voted against the joint resolution on forest fires today. we deplore the many and widespread forest fires and floods that have struck europe. we believe that in certain cases the eu should provide financial support to affected countries and regions when extreme natural disasters occur. however, the aid must not lead to forest fires being able to generate economic benefits. we do not believe that the annual occurrence of fires and floods in the same regions justifies common instruments to combat them and common financing to compensate those affected. on the contrary, we believe that the most important work to combat these fires and their causes should be done, initiated and financed locally and nationally.
in writing. the junilist welcomes international solidarity when a country is affected by forest fires or floods. we regret, however, that the eu institutions are trying to use this kind of tragic events to strengthen the union's influence in various policy areas. it is primarily the responsibility of the member states to take measures to reduce the likelihood of, for example, fires. coordination of resources and joint actions may be carried out outside eu cooperation. i therefore vote against this resolution.
in writing. the junilista group considers that, in accordance with the principle of subsidiarity, the member states, the regions and the municipalities can and should manage the protection of the natural, built and cultural heritage. we are firmly opposed to the artificial creation of a common european cultural heritage at eu level. the idea of creating a legal framework at eu level to protect rural cultural heritage is not realistic. this will be handled at member state level. as always, the european parliament's committee on culture is very imaginative in its reports. the draft report, in paragraph 21, proposes a system of measures to assist small traditional communities similar to the system of cultural capitals. this would impose new costs on the eu budget. the proposal for a report, in paragraph 24, proposes that the establishment of a european year of cultural heritage be promoted. the european parliament often proposes the implementation of different thematic years, but it would be impossible in practice to implement them all. i am voting against the report as a whole.
for the gue/ngl group. air pollution is a major public health problem in the european union. the commission's own estimates show that up to 370 000 premature deaths per year are caused by emissions into the air. the main source of these premature deaths is the emission of particles. to this must be added all the health problems of asthmatics, allergy sufferers, and others who are hypersensitive. a particularly vulnerable group among them are children. air pollution also causes major environmental problems in the form of over-fertilization, acidification and ground-level ozone. health and environmental considerations make it clear that these emissions should be vigorously limited. so do the socioeconomic calculations. it would be a significant gain for society to avoid these costs of emissions. the commission's proposals for both a thematic strategy and a directive on better air quality are therefore a disappointment. the ambitions are very low. once again, we have received a sad confirmation that an ambitious environmental policy is not a priority for this european commission. unfortunately, the draft reports drawn up by the committee on the environment are also inadequate compromises. the decisions proposed there are simply not good enough to really address the problems. that is why we in the united left group have tabled a number of amendments to tighten up the proposals for directives. these various proposals serve several purposes. firstly, we want to remove the very generous possibility of derogations for member states which means that we have to wait an unnecessary number of years to see the directive put into practice. as regards the exposure target, we believe that it must be binding and we believe that it can be raised to 25%. as for the concentration limit for particulate matter, we want to bring it down to 10 micrograms, which would bring it to the level recommended by the world health organization (who) for industrialized countries. we also believe that the same requirements for low levels of pollution should be imposed everywhere regardless of the natural sources of emissions, otherwise different good environments are required for different people depending on where they happen to live. this is our position. mr president, i would like to thank you. i'd like to use the last half-minute to thank you for me. tomorrow i will be resigning as a member of the european parliament, so this is my last speech in the house. i want to thank the colleagues i've worked with for these 11 years. it's been a pleasure working here. i would also like to thank the services of this parliament and, above all, i would like to thank the interpreters very much. thank you! member of the commission. not only the debate, but also the very governance and atmosphere around qualifications in europe is a great challenge to create a space that has a more european content. this means rules and standards that recognise a certain quality and therefore also mobility for citizens. i would like to highlight a few lines that are very important in the whole agenda. one is to create an area that not only means the availability and quality of basic education, academic education and vocational training, but also an area for further education, continuous or lifelong, for the recognition of non-formal and informal learning. the european qualifications framework is moving in this direction. secondly, it is a contribution that builds on what we have already done: the europass adopted in this parliament helps to make qualifications transparent, but the qualifications framework will help to make qualifications compatible and comparable, and thus also to make their transfer possible. this is a very important shift. if we can get final approval, it will be a good framework for employers, for employees, for providers of education and training, because it opens up this space and at the same time it will push for quality. openness means pressure on the quality of education and training provided. mrs. jensen mentioned 32 states. i think that their number will gradually increase if we succeed in developing this process in copenhagen, together with the bologna process, as a credible process, full of quality and guaranteeing quality parameters. i am convinced that this will be to the benefit of the union as a whole, its citizens and its institutions. i believe that this will help to ensure that the plumber, whether polish or any other, is not a threat but a very important contribution to mobility, competitiveness and a common europe. we're going to continue to work on this qualification framework.
the commission is also responsible for the implementation of the budget. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, services of general interest are at the very heart of our european model of society. they cover a wide range of activities, from large network industries energy, telecommunications, transport and postal services to social and health services, water and waste management. the so-called network services, such as transport, energy, telecommunications and postal services, account for about 7% of our collective gross domestic product, 5% of total employment in the european union. they are therefore an essential part of the daily lives of all citizens, but also of businesses in the union. the success of the european strategy for growth and jobs, the lisbon strategy, which is crucial for our prosperity, also depends on the quality and efficiency of these services. since the commission published its white paper on services of general interest, there has been an intense debate on the possible role of the european union in relation to these services. it is a debate i look forward to. i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr rapkay, and his colleagues in the committees who, with this excellent report, have brought this debate forward. the reality of services of general interest is complex. it is also constantly evolving. technical progress, new societal and consumer expectations, financial pressures, modernisation of public administrations - all these have profoundly transformed the way these services are provided. in times of rapid economic and technological change, services of general interest require a useful and effective legal framework, one that enables service providers to achieve their objectives and to continuously adapt to a changing environment. the commission is prepared to cooperate in the creation of such a legal framework on the basis of four essential principles. first principle: the framework for services of general interest provided at national, regional and local level remains the responsibility of the member states, which allows us to respect the important principle of subsidiarity. there is simply no other option. i am firmly convinced that the european union must respect the diversity of situations and practices which exist in the member states. this diversity reflects different histories and cultures in terms of state intervention. i agree with the view expressed in your report that it is not at all appropriate to adopt uniform definitions at community level. member states must remain free to define their services of general interest according to their needs, structures and traditions, which are varied. second principle: the promotion and respect of services of general interest characterised by universal access and a good price/quality ratio are compatible with open and competitive markets. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's energy policy is not distorted by the excessive use of fossil fuels. it should be clarified, however, that in the event of an insoluble conflict between the internal market or competition rules and a general interest mission, the general interest mission must take precedence. this is what the treaty provides for and has been confirmed by the court. the commission is prepared to provide any advice or clarification which may be necessary in this respect. third principle: any framework applicable to services of general interest at the level of the european union must be open to differences and favourable to modernisation. it must be based on the objectives of high quality, good value for money and universal access. the aim is to meet the expectations of citizens and businesses, but also to ensure social and territorial cohesion. fourth principle: as i have said, legal certainty is essential, both for consumers and traders and for public authorities. it is the responsibility of public authorities at all levels to specify the rules applicable to services of general interest. at european level, given the diversity of situations and sectors, it remains to be determined whether this can best be achieved through a single legislative text or through specific sectoral actions. i noted that question in your report. the four principles i have just outlined serve as a guide for action at community level. this action is essentially pragmatic. we are developing sectoral policies at eu level in areas where there is a clear need for them and where they can bring real added value. problems of a more horizontal nature are addressed as they arise. the commission has been very active in this area with your assembly and with the council and i think i can say that we have made good progress in several areas. we have simplified and clarified the state aid rules applicable to services of general interest through the altmark package. we are currently monitoring the application of the new rules on public procurement which came into effect in january. we have undertaken extensive consultations on the issue of public-private partnerships and announced new initiatives for 2007. we have reached an agreement on public transport services. we have adopted a communication on social services and are now starting a consultation on health services. we have launched a wide consultation on the energy sector and we will be putting forward other proposals, including - and i stress this - legislative proposals, not just communications. in fact, we are currently reviewing the regulatory framework for electronic communications and will be proposing a new directive on postal services by the end of the year. these recent developments show that the european union is taking the initiative and is showing ambition in the area of services of general interest. they also illustrate the important role played by the parliament and the council. following the adoption of the rapkay report, which we will analyse carefully, the commission is determined to go further. we will be presenting a communication on this subject by the end of the year. ladies and gentlemen, the main message i have taken from your report is a twofold call for action, on the one hand, for greater legal certainty as regards the union's rules applicable to services of general interest and, on the other hand, for respect for the great diversity of sectors and situations throughout europe. i believe that the time has come for us to consolidate the regulatory framework for services of general interest in the european union. this is what our fellow citizens expect. the commission will take your report fully into account when it considers this matter by the end of the year.
on behalf of the ppe-de group. – madam president, it is important to state one thing in this debate: there is no better way to ensure high quality, accessibility and low prices than a broad range of competition and entrepreneurship in the european economy. we have all seen the results and achievements in a number of sectors that 10 or 20 years ago were looked upon as monopolies on typical services of general interest. we have seen achievements in telecom and airlines and we will see achievements in new areas, because we are talking about services that are by nature dynamic and convergent. they are a huge and important part of the future knowledge economy. we have to open up and, at the same time, respect the specific solutions each member state needs for what it defines as services of general interest. i should like to underline in this debate that there is no call in the report for a framework directive or a horizontal framework of any kind, but there is an emphasis on legal clarity based on a sectoral approach and a call for sector-specific directives where they are needed. mr barroso, you mentioned healthcare. we can look back and see how the sector-specific approach has been quite successful in recent years. so we are calling for legal clarity, based upon the sector-specific approach, and we look forward to seeing future sectoral directives. we underline that there is no legal basis in the treaties for a proposal that would interfere with the internal market, competition rules or public procurement. we stress that this is an area in which there must be respect for member states and for the principle of subsidiarity. in the coming decades, we will see opportunities arising for more and more cross-border activities in new areas. i believe that there will be fantastic opportunities for patients to get better healthcare in different countries and that could also create vitality for the european economy. but we agree on the need to respect the internal market, at the same time as we respect subsidiarity. i hope, mr barroso, that you will read this report and see the need to go further on sector-specific directives and the sector-specific approach, in order to create the legal clarity that also respects the principle of subsidiarity.
for the pse group. i shall not prolong the debate too much. i would first of all like to thank karin jöns, who has worked on the progress programme. she's done it in a very good way. her work has enabled us to reach a solution quickly and, unlike last time, to get it started very quickly. progress is an important programme, a coherent programme for employment, social affairs and gender equality and against discrimination. we have supported the concept of a joint programme. one of the solutions was that we in the european parliament should work to ensure that more money is allocated to the programme in the longterm budget, because it is about the social europe we talk about so often. the agreement on more money for the progress programme also facilitated the agreement on the programme as a whole. there was some disagreement on the issues of gender equality and social inclusion, but those issues have now been resolved. these parts of the programme have received increased resources to increase gender equality, social inclusion and social protection. this has not been at the expense of other parts, but instead funds have been taken from the reserve. i think this has become a very good solution and i believe that progress will play a role in the construction of the future social europe. i would like to thank karin jöns again for a very good job!
in writing. the rapporteur's report addresses the important role played by the media in the development of a country. freedom of expression and of the press are essential in a democracy, but good access to the media for all sections of society is also necessary for the proper functioning of a society. however, there are certain elements of the report which we strongly oppose. we are of course firm in our position that aid should not be provided at community level. this is a matter for the member states. furthermore, the eu shall not conduct information campaigns in the member states. on the whole, however, we think the content is good. we have therefore voted in favour of the report in today's vote.
in writing. junilista has pointed out in the european parliament before, and does so again, that only the member states are responsible for the organisation of education and the content of education systems. the european parliament has previously expressed its wish to address issues such as language teaching and the adaptation of education systems to new technologies and digitalisation. the european dimension is now to be incorporated into the curricula. but how should the european dimension in europe's common history and cultural heritage be defined? and by whom or by whom? we must act in accordance with the principle of subsidiarity. member states themselves must promote democracy and tolerance in europe in their curricula and to their citizens. this cannot be regulated at eu level. we have therefore voted against the report.
in writing. the junilista group considers that the president should not be granted a discharge for the 2004 financial year, for two main reasons: firstly, we believe that the management of the european parliament bears as much responsibility as the city of strasbourg for having paid too high a rent for the premises in strasbourg for years. this has cost the eu taxpayer a lot of money and parliament should take the lead in this case in order to recover the exorbitant rent. as there is still no information on the amount of the excess payments (see the fifth indent of paragraph 19 of the draft report), we consider that the matter has not yet been settled. secondly, we believe that the european parliament as an institution should not be held responsible for the shortfall in the special pension scheme for members of the european parliament. members' pension rights must be linked to the investment performance of the pension fund. this is how it works for ordinary citizens of the eu and members of the european parliament should not have a special luxury in this respect.
in writing. today we have voted in favour of the report by mr markus ferber on the discharge for the implementation of the general budget of the european union for the financial year 2004. no criminal acts have been confirmed in connection with the rental of two of the buildings used by the european parliament in strasbourg. at the same time, it should be clear that the principle of 'sound financial management' as laid down in the financial regulation has not been fully respected. the purchase of the existing buildings is a good deal from a strictly economic point of view in the long term. nevertheless, we believe that in the present situation it would be desirable to postpone such a decision for the future. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the budget is properly managed. one million eu citizens have signed up to the citizens' initiative to end the travelling circus between brussels and strasbourg. we fully support the demand that the european parliament should have only one seat, that is, brussels. the report also deals with important issues such as members' contributions, secretarial contributions and the voluntary pension fund. we've decided to opt out of the pension fund. we believe it is unreasonable for the taxpayers of europe to be involved in the financing of a fund, regardless of whether the return is profitable or not.
in writing. there is every reason to criticize some of the actions of the european parliament. however, no criminal record has been established. the voluntary pension schemes for members of the european parliament are a supplementary pension insurance policy in addition to the ordinary members' pension. the scheme means that two thirds of all payments (currently eur 2 088 per month and member who opts for this insurance) are made by european taxpayers. the remaining one third has so far been paid by the secretarial fees of members, despite the fact that the court of auditors already in 1999 commented on this construction and considered that the payments should be made in a completely private manner, to avoid accusations that 'public funds are used for private pension payments'. this may seem strange in itself, given that the system is designed so that two thirds of all payments are made from the eu budget. i think the whole system should be dismantled as soon as possible. i also voted in favour of the amendments which seek to prevent parliament from buying the parliament building in strasbourg. in the short term, a purchase will bring financial savings, but the ambition must be that a purchase in such a case be financed by a sale of the parliament building in brussels. the most important thing is that the seat of the eu is limited to one place.
in writing. junilista is a strong supporter of increased mobility in europe, not least in the education sector. but you can always ask yourself when bureaucracy takes over a good cause. we vote for the compromise, but we object to amendment no 47. it adds that 'the charter, which is a basic reference document, should be distributed in the respective languages to all students and persons undergoing training by the authorities, organisations and other actors concerned by mobility in the home and host countries'. we believe that it is up to the individual member states to implement what they have committed themselves to do.
in writing. the rapporteur's report deals with the commission's thematic strategy on air pollution. the junilists believe that transnational environmental problems are a matter to be dealt with at community level. air pollution is one example of such a problem. paragraph 25 calls on the european parliament, the commission and the council to conclude an interinstitutional agreement setting out their positions on the air quality objectives. the junilists also want better legislation, of course, but we think it is difficult for the legislative and executive branches to agree in advance on the rules that will apply in the future. that is why we voted against the report in today's vote.
in writing. i am voting in favour of this report, even though there are negative points such as per capita green space and how to reduce music from city apartments, which are clearly local issues that should be decided at local level. but the positive aspects such as congestion charges, reduced car traffic and local sustainability plans are being considered and the bourgeois government may certainly need a push from the eu on these issues. however, the content should retain its character of recommendation and not lead to extensive legislation.
the junist party's representatives in the european parliament voted against the proposal to open membership negotiations with turkey. our refusal was not motivated by the fact that turkey is outside europe, nor by the fact that turkey is a muslim country. we pointed out that turkey still has a long way to go to meet the requirements we are setting for eu membership. and we expressed our concern that the opening of negotiations would reduce the pressure on turkey to establish a democratic state of law with full human rights for women, for kurds, for christians, for trade union movements, for writers. and that's what happened. the previously promising trend has been broken and in some respects replaced by a decline. we have found ourselves in the undignified situation of the european union beginning to demand the form of the electoral system and the drafting of a new constitution in the sovereign state of turkey. this is not the eu's job. it is up to turkey to choose how it wants to live up to the eu's demands, it is up to turkey to choose its own solutions. pending sufficient reforms, membership negotiations should be opened.
i would like to thank mr camiel eurlings for his report. we in the swedish social democratic group are in favour of turkish membership when the criteria we had for all the others are met. we should not make any higher demands or give turkey special treatment, as some here have said. at the moment, the eu is the guarantor of development in turkey, with encouragement but also demands on human rights, among other things. a former member of the european parliament, now a social democrat member of the swedish parliament, has written to the turkish ambassador about the fact that 1 200 children's books by swedish children's authors, used by swedish schools and financed by sida and the olof palme centre, have been stopped at turkish customs. this is incomprehensible to me and i have therefore put a question to the council on this. i support the democratization of turkey and we must solve problems like this. i share the critical views expressed by the other speakers from the socialist group on the report. let us encourage progress and solve the problems, but let us be firm that we in the eu want to see turkey as a member when the time is right and the democratic deficit is over.
first of all, i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr agustín díaz, for his work on this subject, which is perhaps not so exciting, but is certainly so important. díaz has produced a good report, and i would like to emphasize this, since i myself was rapporteur on this issue when it was on the agenda during the last parliamentary term. then there was a slight difference of opinion, not least as to which denominations would be counterfeited. so it has been shown in retrospect that the ppe-de group's line was the right one. many people thought that the 500 euro note would be the most vulnerable to counterfeiting, but we in the ppe-de group considered that the smaller denominations, such as the 20 and 50 euro notes, were at greater risk. agustín diaz is pleased to see the idea of close monitoring being followed also in countries which have not yet adopted the euro as their currency. the risks of counterfeiting are usually greater there, as the public is not prepared in the same way as in the euro area countries. people in these countries have a harder time distinguishing between genuine and counterfeit notes and are often less suspicious. fortunately, the problem of counterfeiting has become much less serious than feared, perhaps thanks to the pericles programme and, not least, to the extent to which it has been implemented. here is another example of a good thing the eu has done. it is an area where we do not have so much conflict anymore, but there is every reason to inform citizens about this programme that protects us all in our daily lives.
in the long run, we cannot survive without a common immigration policy and common strategies. the member states have the primary responsibility for integration. the current exclusion and benefits in lieu of work must be reversed and integration policies activated. work must be the rule and subsidies the exception. in sweden, the new government is to make the unemployed more attractive on the labour market. integration policy must also be combined with measures to combat trafficking in human beings and the development of partnership agreements with neighbouring countries. i would like to commend franco frattini for his efforts in this area. together we must also defend the human values on which the eu is founded. an area of freedom, justice and security requires enhanced legal certainty and strengthened individual fundamental rights which the court of justice can and must apply. although we lack this and the protection of data in the third pillar, we have acquired measures that go far into the private sphere. how far does the council think it can go further without fundamental rights and an appeal court at eu level? the rule of law and fundamental rights should now be the focus. it would help to make law enforcement more effective in the long run. law enforcement and law enforcement go hand in hand.
the debate we are having now is a high priority among the citizens of the eu. the urgent problems should have been solved long ago, as several speakers have said. it is about the flows of refugees to the canary islands and malta, a message to all those waiting to stay in the eu and better support for those struggling to be accepted as new citizens of the eu. we should have gone further in influencing attitudes in our communities and countering segregation in employment, housing and schooling. in sweden, we had an election two weeks ago, and a xenophobic party got seats in the council in every third municipality. i'm concerned about their message that too many immigrants have been allowed to come. they talk about it, but not about our responsibility and solidarity. the eu's population is ageing rapidly and we will need to grow. we have to get through this debate. we in parliament must be given more powers, access to faster decision-making and greater responsibility. only a common eu policy in this area can bring results for the citizens of the eu.

in writing. the report is clearly a further step towards the total harmonisation of the criminal justice systems of the member states. as always, proposals of this kind involve a lengthy and very complicated compromise between the countries. junilista is always a strong supporter of the right of member states to self-determination in criminal matters and we are very concerned about the rule of law. the report gives, inter alia, private operators the right to access, under certain conditions, highly sensitive personal data, which, in the opinion of junilistan, cannot be considered as providing sufficient legal certainty. the amendment of parliament does contain some guarantees for the legal certainty of individuals, but the proposal as a whole represents a major step towards supranational control of the core of the right to self-determination of the rule of law, namely criminal law.
in writing. i abstain from voting on this report because i believe that the eu should devote equal resources to protecting the swedish krona and other eu currencies and not unilaterally protect the euro. the fight against counterfeiting is of course a positive thing in itself, especially when it is based on cooperation and conferences and not increased police surveillance.
in writing. the junilista group considers that the legal certainty of individuals is a very important issue on which no compromise can be made. the criminal law is at the heart of a country's sovereignty and right to self-determination. there is no doubt that there are today widely different legal cultures within the eu, and the same applies to the view on the rule of law and the impartiality of the judiciary. the proposal risks undermining the hague convention on the international effects of criminal tribunals. the convention was signed in 1972 and has worked well. the junilista group considers that the parliament's amendment is more thoughtful, but that the proposal as a whole is another step towards a supranational union. we therefore vote against the proposal on grounds of principle.

in writing. the developments in turkey are very worrying in a number of areas. several cases of journalists and writers being arrested and charged with committing crimes against "turkishness" have been brought to attention. while the developments are of course very worrying, we must not forget the democratic and reformist forces in the country. it is now our task, the eu's task, to continue to help and support these forces to put turkey back on the right track. in the report, carmiel eurlings makes a justified criticism of turkey. he also criticised the way turkey was treated in comparison with other candidate countries. this is not acceptable. it is important that turkey reconciles with its history, and with armenia. but it should not be the decision whether or not to continue negotiations with turkey. it is important that the eu now feels its responsibility and does not play into the hands of fundamentalists and backward thinkers without our voting through a balanced report. we have therefore voted in favour of paragraph 50 and the amendments which advocate a constructive attitude towards turkey.
in writing. the report reviews the progress made by turkey so far towards future eu accession. only one chapter, 'science and research', has been opened and provisionally closed. consequently, there is still a long way to go. the junilists do not see turkey's geographical position or its religion as an obstacle to possible eu membership. we believe that the same requirements should be imposed for accession as in previous enlargements - no more, no less. turkey does not meet the copenhagen criteria and therefore eu membership is not currently an option. however, it is not only turkey that needs to adapt. the eu's agricultural policy and structural funds need to be reformed. a possible future treaty is another issue that must be negotiated before further enlargements of the eu can be implemented, mainly in view of the voting power of the member states and to avoid a few large member states having the opportunity to dominate the whole union. both turkey and the eu have a very long political and economic road ahead of them before membership can be considered, but if and when that day comes, junilistan considers turkey to be welcome. that is why we have voted in favour of this report.
in writing. turkey has a very long way to go before eu membership can become a reality. i have previously voted against opening accession negotiations with turkey because it will take a very long time before the requirements for eu membership are met. it is not possible to maintain a negotiating process over a period of 20 years. the pressure to ease membership requirements risks becoming unmanageable. it is important to make clear demands on turkey. the copenhagen criteria for human rights must be met. the sovereignty of cyprus must be respected. the genocide of armenians and syrians/assyrians in 1915 must be recognized. before turkey can become a member of the eu, a change in the voting power of the member states must take place, lest a few populous member states come to dominate eu decision-making. the financial implications of a possible turkish membership of the eu must be carefully examined. the eu's common agricultural policy and regional policy must be reformed before turkey can become a member of the eu. the eu budget should be limited to 1% of the total gnp of the member states. any turkish membership must not exceed this ceiling. it is important to note that the opening of negotiations with turkey does not automatically mean that turkey will become a member of the eu.
on behalf of the alde group. – madam president, one of the basic truths of our society is gender inequality. this gender power structure is present in every aspect of our society and it hampers growth and every kind of sustainability. of the people living in poverty in the world, almost three out of four are women, so equal opportunities for women and men is not just an issue of human rights or some sort of luxury. when every other problem is solved it is crucial for our common future. this report by my colleague mrs breyer, on behalf of the committee on women’s rights and gender equality, is a very good starting point for the urgent work ahead in the specific area of trade relations in the world. as she points out, women in general experience more difficulties in gaining access to education, property, credit, other resources and factors of production, as well as political decision-making bodies, and these inequalities restrict women’s ability to use the advantages of trade liberalisation. for instance, a large number of women throughout the world are denied opportunities to improve their economic and social conditions and, at the same time, many of them have the added responsibility of caring for children and the household. a majority of the farmers in the world are women! a structural change in the eu’s common agricultural policy is an important strengthening of these women in third world countries. however, gender inequality is not just something that exists in poor countries: the representation of women in politics and business is also very low in the member states of the union. in order to raise the discussion of quotas to a new level, we must consider that this instrument can be used in a number of different ways. as i see it, the quota instrument should be used only when every other way has been tested and proved unsuccessful. like the rapporteur, i welcome the norwegian government’s decision to impose by law a 40% quota for female representation on the boards of joint stock companies. but, basically, this report addresses a number of issues concerning the systematic and thoroughly unjust gender inequality affecting international trade relations. it includes a number of very precise suggestions, which i believe could really make a difference in a political world so much characterised by words and empty promises. it will make for good reading in the commission and in the council. finally, let me just say: praeterea censeo prostitutionem esse delendam.
for the gue/ngl group. a sincere thank you to mrs hiltrud breyer for an excellent report and for raising a very important issue on trade and how wto agreements, gats agreements and imf decisions affect people's living conditions and livelihoods. just as agreements often benefit developed countries and disadvantage developing countries, trade agreements benefit and disadvantage women and men in different ways. traditionally, world trade is the arena of men. the imf's board is 100 percent male, and the world bank's board is 91.7 percent male. trade and gender have a variety of interconnections that are necessary to study in order to understand how people in different places, people of different genders and different classes are affected by trade agreements. gender is relevant because gender relations affect the distribution and access to resources, labour, income and power. gender affects our behavior as economic actors. men and women react differently to economic change. economic institutions are not only male-dominated, but more importantly, they perpetuate and maintain the gender structures of the economy. men's and women's work is valued differently. women's work in the reproductive sphere is both invisible and untouched. these factors affect women and men differently, and so gender analysis is crucial to the design of trade policies that promote gender equality and break down patriarchal structures. the role of women in the economy must be made visible. the effectiveness of trade negotiations must take account of all conventions, such as the convention on the elimination of all forms of discrimination against women.
i am very concerned about the report by mr pedro guerreiro on improving the economic situation of the fishing industry. the way in which the community's fishing fleet is operating will lead to an irreversible depletion of the world's oceans, all to protect an industry which is not competitive internationally. let me give you an example. the commission presented a proposal on fisheries in the baltic sea at the beginning of september. the international council for the exploration of the sea (ices) reiterated its call for a total cessation of cod fishing in the eastern baltic sea, but the commission considers that a reduction of 15% is sufficient. this clearly shows that the eu does not put the environment first, but rather looks to the interests of industry. the latter, however, is a modified truth, since the fishing industry will die out when the seas are depleted. a global justice perspective should also be adopted when discussing eu fisheries policy. the rapporteur explicitly states that the eu's fishing fleet is forced to compete with the fishing fleets of third countries. the latter have much lower costs and their products are consequently cheaper. by introducing tax relief, which is definitely not a community matter, pedro guerreiro wants to distort the international market and thus deprive poor people of their only source of income. it's terrifying and at the same time very tragic. on the other hand, the eu is showing its true face here - a non-competitive economy that is prepared to do anything to protect its production, while excluding the world's poor.
for the verts/ale group. when you think of sole and redfish, they are a source of joy and very good fish, but future generations will not experience that joy unless we take our responsibility today. i think that the report by albert jan maat is generally good, but i am criticizing the same things as the commissioner. amendment no 8 to have a three-year quota means exactly what chris davies said. it cannot be adapted in time and is not appropriate. the same goes for cod. this is connected and therefore amendment no 14 cannot be accepted either. if the number of days at sea is to be limited for the reasons stated, then this must also apply to these vessels, no exceptions can be made here. as regards amendment no 15, which introduces a tolerance margin of 10% instead of 8%, i would point out that this is not complicated with modern computer technology. we can figure out 8 percent instead of 10 percent. the increased flexibility is detrimental to the stock. the same applies to amendment no 20. increased flexibility for member states to interpret when action needs to be taken is not working. when you try to increase flexibility, it actually leads to decreased flexibility. because if the fish are depleted, there's no flexibility left. there are zero fishing quotas, zero fishing days and 100 per cent unemployment among professional fishermen. nobody wants that, right?
for the ind/dem group. nanotechnology is certainly of great importance for the future of mankind. progress is rapid, and the technology will have a dramatic impact on virtually all fields. there is a deep-rooted belief in this house that all such important developments must be managed by the eu. all the reports argue that the organisation, legislation, supervision and financing must be the responsibility of the eu. all the reports stress the importance of the eu not falling behind the us, japan and china in the global arena. but we never see a convincing analysis of what it is that does not resolve itself in the market but requires public action. we never see any convincing analysis of the level at which such public measures should be taken. the starting point is always that the european parliament has perfect knowledge and can and should therefore instruct the commission and the member states to follow its instructions. the european parliament calls for, emphasises, underlines and reminds everyone of the nature of the world's problems and how they should be solved. the rapporteur, miloslav ransdorf, for example, wants parliament to state that nanotechnology should be geared towards the development of hydrogen energy. i would argue that the european parliament is certainly incompetent to decide such a question. nanotechnology develops most rapidly and efficiently when it is not controlled from the top by international bureaucrats. the international scientific community, companies and national institutions are best placed to experiment and compete in the field of nanotechnology. and it is private and governmental organisations in the nation states that are best placed to produce information material on nanotechnology, tailored to the values and knowledge of their own people. the eu's role in this context will be limited to establishing a patent monitoring system in the field, establishing ethical and environmental standards and possibly securing funding for very large-scale projects of the same type as fusion research.
nanotechnology has potential, but its real enemy is the one who wants to unleash it on the consumer market without regulation, without knowledge, without protection. we greens want knowledge first, then regulations to protect the environment and health and then marketing. that's the right order. nanoparticles do not have the same toxic properties as ordinary particles. carbon powder is harmless, but nanoparticles in the form of carbon clusters cause serious brain damage in fish within 48 hours even at concentrations of 0.5 ppm. nanotubes can destroy mitochondrial dna, and nanoparticles on the skin can migrate to the brain and lymph nodes and damage our bodies. our immune systems aren't designed to deal with nanoparticles. the eu's own research shows that non-biodegradable and non-biologically compatible nanoparticles can be life threatening and should be avoided at all inhalation and ingestion.
in writing. the junilists are, as we know, opposed to eu-led aid for a number of reasons. aid policy, like foreign policy, is a national concern and should not be delegated to brussels. we oppose most of the report, including the calls for increased budgets. the rapporteur also deals with other strictly national matters such as the control and monitoring of bilateral aid. it is also proposed that the commission should carry out strategic planning of member states' assistance. this is completely unacceptable.
in writing. the basic view of the junilists is that fisheries issues should be dealt with within existing international organisations. this means that the eu will not have to pursue a common fisheries policy or set fishing quotas. sustainable fishing is a prerequisite for the survival of the fishing industry. experience shows that the eu does not give priority to sustainable fishing. for example, the fishing quotas for cod in the baltic sea have been the subject of widespread criticism from, among others, the fao.
in writing. junilista understands that the same requirements must be imposed on organic products from third countries as on organic products from the eu. but the control systems must not be too costly and the subsidies that eu producers receive to produce their organic products must be taken into account. we are skeptical of the agriculture committee's supplementary proposal for stricter requirements for organic products from third countries and wonder whether there are hidden protectionist motives behind their amendment to the proposal for a regulation. we are therefore not prepared to support the committee on agriculture and have voted against their amendments and motions for resolutions.
in writing. the moderate delegation has voted against the ppe-de resolution because we are firmly opposed to the common minimum list of safe countries of origin referred to in the last sentence of paragraph 9. we also believe that this version of the resolution does not sufficiently emphasise that all measures to combat illegal immigration must be compatible with the guarantees and fundamental rights of the individual laid down in the charter of fundamental rights of the european union and the european convention for the protection of human rights and fundamental freedoms. we are not totally against paragraph 4 (information requirements in the case of more generous rules), but we are hesitant because it could be a first step towards full sovereignty in the area of asylum and immigration.
in writing. the eu's common immigration policy is, in the junist's view, one of the main reasons for the tragic situation many migrants find themselves in when, in the hope of a better life, they embark on a literally life-threatening journey towards the eu. the resolution rightly emphasises that the dublin ii regulation is and has been a failure. the regulation has meant that the fate of migrants has been decided mainly by the countries of the southern and eastern part of the eu, without taking into account the immigration policies and needs of other member states. it is extremely strange and unacceptable that the eu has been working on a common migration policy for a decade. all these policy experiments have eroded the right of member states to self-determination in migration matters and caused great suffering to migrants. the solution to the current situation is not to give the eu more powers on immigration to continue its failed immigration policy, but to give the member states back their right of self-determination.
in writing. the junilists strongly support the completion of the internal market. this also means that the eu must have a common trade policy towards other states. however, it is not the eu's role to object to india's domestic affairs. the rapporteur notes, among other things, the indian bureaucracy, the structure of the public sector and the regional policy of the central government. junilists are committed to national self-determination and sovereignty, whether it concerns a member state or any other state in the world. that is why we have voted against the report. we also do not exclude the possibility that india may have views on eu trade and agricultural policy.
in writing. we have voted against the report on the future of women in international trade. free trade leads to increased prosperity and reduced poverty. in recent decades, globalization has led to dramatic improvements in the living standards of people, especially women and children. free trade creates more formal jobs and, contrary to what is claimed in the report, free trade also provides women with informal jobs with greater security and a way out of poverty. the report focuses on form rather than content. gender equality is important, not least in working life, where a salary is a strong contribution to women's independence and autonomy. more globalisation, not expanded eu institutions, is the right way to increase women's autonomy.
madam president, i would like to thank you. the lahti summit next week will deal with the question of a common energy policy. this is yet another example of the political establishment's deliberate use of social issues to advance the eu's positions for purely power-political reasons. in fact, there is very little reason to pursue an energy policy at eu level. we are at a historic juncture where it is becoming increasingly clear that the emissions of greenhouse gases from the human combustion of coal and hydrocarbons are causing climate change. we must find solutions to this situation. however, no one in the eu leadership can decide which energy type we should choose to meet the needs of a sustainable energy supply in the future. in addition, the conditions vary widely from country to country. some countries have a vested interest in finding ways to capture and store carbon dioxide from burning coal and oil. some countries are prepared to become dependent on russian natural gas, while others want to invest in nuclear power, hydropower, wind and wave energy, biomass, geothermal energy. everyone can reduce their energy consumption in different ways. in this process, countries must be able to experiment and seek their way through different paths. it is through such institutional competition between countries that development progresses. the idea that the eu should determine how much of which energy types are to be used is a foolish idea that is dangerous for europe's future.
mr president, the murder of anna politkovskaya is a threefold tragedy. it is personal – a tragedy for her family – but it is also a tragedy for russian society, because it is a sign of an emerging development in that large country, and it is a tragedy for democracy, because it was a murder of an important democratic element in russia. it was a step down the ladder which is weakening democracy in that country. it was said earlier here today that we should be realistic about democracy in russia. i do not know what the speaker really meant, but let us indeed be realistic about democracy in russia: if we do not stand up for democracy in russia, we will leave people like anna politkovskaya and thousands of others on their own. if we do not stand up for democracy and the rule of law in russia, we will not strengthen the forces in russia that can make russia a better country and a better partner. not standing up for democracy will not make russia a better partner in energy policy or in any other area. (applause)not calling for democracy and the rule of law does not make russia a better society. russia will be a credible energy partner only when democracy is stronger and when the rule of law is stabilised. that is what it means to be realistic about democracy in russia, and let that be stated here today. mr barroso, some of the competences we have in the european union – the internal market for energy policy, the trans-european networks, competition rules and trade policy – are the instruments that we must use with relation not only to russia but also to other parts of the world. they are the best basis for the future energy policy of the european union. let us use them and let us go forward step by step.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the imposition of duties on shoes from vietnam and china is a magnificent self-interest on the part of the eu. it's bad economic policy, it's bad trade policy, and it's moral bankruptcy. first, let me explain why it's bad economic policy. what we are doing at the moment is protecting and defending the industry which is not competitive, while punishing the industry which has adapted to globalisation, for example by moving its production to countries which are more competitive. in this way we succeed in weakening the eu's competitiveness twice with the same decision. i fear that the commission's vision for the future is that the eu will compete globally with cheap shoes. i think the worst thing that could happen is that the commission succeeds and we preserve this industry. if that is the case, then in 30 years' time we can expect europe to export shoes to vietnam, while vietnam exports cars, or something even more valuable that we cannot even imagine, to europe. secondly, it is bad trade policy. at the end of this month, vietnam will become a member of the wto. the eu's welcome gift is tariffs on vietnamese shoes. the signals we are sending to vietnam, from one of the world's largest trading blocs, are a disaster, not least as the country undergoes major changes to meet the requirements for a future wto membership. third, it is morally bankrupt. this is because it is hard on individuals to satisfy small well-organized special interests. you are not content to extend the shoe-leather, but now you are extending it to include children's shoes. what do you say to swedish families with small children who may have to buy several pairs of children's shoes a year? do you think families with children have too much money? have they gotten away with the earlier measures? this is an example of when the eu shows its worst side, namely when well-organised special interests are allowed to take precedence over the good of the citizens. i think we should avoid that in the future.
mr president, i would like to thank you. you recently said that the historical dimension of the european union's seat in strasbourg cannot be understood in the same way in some of the nordic countries which did not take part in the second world war. nordic citizens did not suffer enough during the second world war to understand the symbolic value of the european parliament in strasbourg. the european parliament has also been asked to make a statement on the situation in the baltic sea. your statement was, of course, historically completely wrong - finland, norway and denmark were involved in world war ii - but it is also remarkable in another respect. the president of the european parliament is therefore quite serious about not listening to the views of citizens and members of the european parliament on the floor of the european parliament if they come from countries that did not participate in the second world war. it is a grotesque idea, whose only conciliatory feature is that we do not have to listen to you, mr president, because spain was not involved in the second world war either. the thinking is unreasonable. today's irish, portuguese, spanish and swedish people have the same right to be heard in the current debate as other nationalities. it is scandalous that the person who has been entrusted with the presidency of the european parliament has such poor judgment that he thinks he has the right to tell us whom we should listen to and whom we should try to silence. the committee on the environment, public health and food safety has tabled an opinion. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, and commissioner! every day, millions of animals suffer in the meat industry's animal factories, during animal transport, in painful animal experiments and at slaughterhouses across europe. now we have a chance to actually improve the conditions for these animals. i'm speaking on behalf of the environmental committee. it was my predecessor, jonas sjöstedt, who was in charge of our case. in our opinion we call for the right of animals to a natural behaviour, the prevention of overproduction of meat, the right of all chickens to be released by 2016, the right of sows to move freely throughout their life cycle and the introduction of an eight-hour limit for animal transport. it is therefore with regret that i have to say that mr jeggles' report does not meet any of these requirements. it is therefore important that we have as much influence on this report as possible. the gue/ngl group has asked for a separate vote on the points which we consider to be relativising the value of animals in relation to trade. if mr jeggle's report is adopted, animal welfare will only be worth pursuing if it does not run counter to wto and commercial interests. that's what it says, but we can't have that! animals have values regardless of changes in competitiveness. the important thing is that we in europe take the initiative and become competitive through higher standards and better animal welfare. therefore, reject the wording of recital g and paragraphs 51, 52, 53 and 59. i also urge you to vote in favour of amendments nos 11 and 12, which are now also supported by jeggle, which is good. if you vote in favour, we can finally have an action plan that will make life much better for the hundreds of millions of animals living in the eu. the animals deserve it!
mr president, i would like to thank you. a civilized society can never compete on the lowest price with animals raised in conditions akin to torture. only with high animal welfare standards will animal husbandry in the eu survive. local production, short transport times and consumer safety with natural breeding - that's where the future lies. we have an agricultural policy that tortures broilers, forces animals on long journeys, gives more medicines to animals than to people and castrates pigs. such an agricultural policy should be castrated rather than the pigs! a maximum of eight hours of transport is a reasonable requirement. minimum standards for animal welfare - amendment no 11 - are needed. we've quadrupled our wealth in the last 40 years. if we cannot afford to sacrifice even a percentage of this wealth to protect defenceless animals, we cannot call ourselves civilized. to my spanish friends, i would like to say this: a noble fight would be an unarmed matador against an unarmed bull that is untreated, not getting its back broken, not drugged and not being tortured. it would be a noble fight! however, as things stand, this is animal cruelty, which should not be supported by financial means.
member of the commission. mr president, thank you for what has been, overall, a very interesting and supportive debate. i shall inform my colleague, mr kyprianou, of your comments. i should like to touch upon just a couple of your comments. mr holm emphasised trade issues and suggested that competition is incompatible with animal welfare; i disagree. it will be very difficult for eu producers to compete in the global market on the basis of price alone. this is clear if you look at the competitive advantage of certain third countries in terms of lower land, labour or infrastructure costs. instead, i think high animal welfare standards should be promoted as one of our competitive advantages. we should also remember that, as mr tarabella said, there needs to be real choice for consumers. recent surveys and research projects have demonstrated consumers’ active interest in the welfare of farm animals. it is clear that consumers believe that they can improve animal welfare through their purchasing power and that a large proportion would be prepared to pay a premium price for such products. ms resetarits also emphasised this point. the commission thinks that labelling will eventually make these products more competitive, to the benefit of the industry, and in particular to those who produce in a welfare-friendly manner. we believe that europe’s high animal welfare standards need to be seen as a marketing opportunity and a competitive advantage, rather than as a burden or liability. the policy of using vaccination to prevent the spread of animal diseases has been carefully considered by the commission and is now integrated into the specific community legislation on foot-and-mouth disease prevention. the same principles are today in the avian influenza measures just adopted. i have to remind mrs ayuso gonzález that the treaty does not allow the community to act in respect of the use of animals in cultural and traditional events, such as bullfighting. therefore, the action plan does not foresee any measures in this regard.
good animal welfare is a necessity in a modern society. there are wide differences in animal welfare between eu member states. it may therefore be appropriate to have some common rules, for example on the transport of animals. junilista sees the eu as a union of values, in which animal ethics is also an important part. however, we are questioning and opposed to an animal welfare authority under the auspices of the eu. the existing structures in the eu member states should be adequate. if there is community legislation, member states are obliged to apply it. in the absence of such a system, the commission and, ultimately, the court of justice will take action against the state or states which do not comply with the legislation. as is so often the case, the balance between decision-making at eu level and at national, regional and local level is very difficult. however, there are certain values that must be protected at all costs, such as human rights and a basic animal welfare. this should preferably be done at international level.
in writing. in view of the stalled process of creating a constitution for the european union, the rapporteur believes that this is the right moment for the eu to highlight the protection and promotion of fundamental human rights. in this way, the rapporteur chooses to justify the need for a new agency to enforce rights. this function has been monitored at the highest level since 1953 by the well-respected european court of human rights, which encompasses so many more countries than the eu member states, with very good results. the obvious motive of the report is to maintain an agency for monitoring fundamental human rights, since the constitution has fallen. this is highly remarkable and further demonstrates the eu's tireless drive to become a supranational power that will manage everything in all areas without regard to the existence of intergovernmental agreements and agreements and national self-determination. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competent authority' to cover all the activities of the european parliament. we have also voted that if the proposal is adopted, the rights of the child should be given a prominent place in the work of the agency. the june list rejects the report as a whole.
in writing. we voted against the whole of the report by mr jeggle, a6-0290/2006, because it is in several respects counter to the union's efforts to improve animal welfare. we cannot support the idea that the eu should delay the introduction of better animal welfare until our trading partners in the wto do the same. this will seriously impede progress in this area. we also oppose the introduction of trade barriers against third countries with lower animal welfare standards than the eu. this is likely to affect developing countries that depend on agricultural exports to the eu. however, we would like to stress that we are in favour of the commission's action plan for animal welfare and animal protection. we also welcome the parts of the report which seek to move forward in several important areas of animal welfare.
in writing. we believe that pursuing a policy within the eu aimed at protecting animals and improving their welfare is an essential responsibility of the union of values. i therefore agree with the report in its entirety. however, i do not support the idea in the report's justification of creating an eu centre or laboratory for animal welfare and animal welfare. it is the responsibility of the member states to ensure that an animal welfare policy is pursued in accordance with the general animal welfare strategy agreed at eu level.
in writing. we welcome the commission's proposal for an action plan for animal welfare 2006-2010. there is a great need to improve the conditions for animals in practice and the commission's proposal contains many important proposals. unfortunately, we have had to conclude that the jeggles report has not lived up to our high expectations, in particular by emphasizing the deterioration of competitiveness resulting from high standards of animal welfare, which in practice risks making a progressive policy in this area impossible. that is why we have decided to abstain in the final vote on the report today.
in writing. we believe that pursuing a policy within the eu aimed at protecting animals and improving their welfare is an essential responsibility of the union of values. i therefore agree with the report in its entirety. however, i do not support the idea in the report's justification of creating an eu centre or laboratory for animal welfare and animal welfare. it is the responsibility of the member states to ensure that an animal welfare policy is pursued in accordance with the general animal welfare strategy agreed at eu level. i also disagree with part of the call in paragraph 71 of the motion for a resolution to put an end to dogfighting and bullfighting at eu level by means of legislation at eu level. in our view, it is ultimately up to the citizens of a member state to decide in national elections or referendums whether they wish to ban the abovementioned activities within the territory of their state.
in writing. the junilists believe that freer trade is good for world prosperity. the idea of free trade is that the more people involved, the better the outcome for the parties involved. this own-initiative report contains many meritorious elements which could lead to increased prosperity. unfortunately, as so often happens in this congregation, irrelevant and negative writings are added. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment and the environment from harmful substances. unfortunately, the aspects just mentioned overshadow the desirable that could be achieved. i therefore voted against the report in today's vote.
in writing. free trade is the most important means of prosperity in the world, and the more countries that participate in such free trade, the more prosperity increases in the countries that participate. bringing large parts of south america into a free trade area with the eu is therefore a major step towards prosperity and poverty eradication. this own-initiative report therefore contains many worthwhile proposals which, if implemented, could lead to increased prosperity. as is so often the case in this house, it unfortunately also contains a great deal of writing, the aim of which is to promote the development of the european union into a federal superpower and to increase the power of parliament at the expense of the member states. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment and the environment from harmful substances. despite these strong objections, i consider that it is better to conclude this regional association agreement than to stop it.
in writing. we have voted against this resolution because its content seeks to restrict and in various ways make it more difficult for third countries to access the internal market and to introduce various new forms of aid to eu farmers in this sector. we believe that the market must be opened up to agricultural products in general, including berries and cherries for processing. it is indeed a scandal that the committee on agriculture has suddenly tabled a motion for a resolution in the european parliament which talks about excessive imports of berries from third countries and which seeks to introduce, among other things: qualified market access, support schemes for producer groups, special entry price safeguard clauses, financial support for the liquidation of perennial berry and cherry plantations in situations of persistent surplus supply. we in this parliament are definitely opposed to such proposals.
