for the verts/ale group. mr president thank you, mr coelho, for your work on improving this proposal. it is important that we increase data protection, which the european parliament is trying to do. it is important that people know what is being recorded about them, on good grounds. i think that schengen, and the schengen information system, have been beset by problems from the very beginning. in practice, we have replaced simple border controls with the ability for authorities to constantly monitor residents once they are in the country. what is proposed here is an expansion of the authorities that have access to data. this means that we will be making greater use of this data, and we will have to be even more careful about the data we are putting in. for that reason, i think it is inappropriate to include biometric data. when the united states discussed doing this, they let their court of auditors examine the possibilities. it was concluded that there was a great risk either that many who have the right to come into our union will not get through or if you reduce the margins of security that those who you want to stop will get through. there are many people, for example hardworking people with bad fingerprints, who will not be able to be identified reliably by fingerprints. there's a risk that the innocent will be rejected and the guilty will slip through the cracks. biometric data is also very expensive. it is not acceptable that such data should now be used for searching purposes and that this should be decided by a committee procedure. biometric data is a privacy violation, especially since it could very well affect innocent people. that is not a path we should take. some governments are now pushing last-minute measures to give the security police access to this data. the whole principle of data protection, the whole principle of the right to know what has been recorded about a person, the whole principle of legal certainty and the rule of law, falls. i think it would be just as well if the council withdrew that discussion from the agenda. the security police shall not have access to this information.
mr president, commissioner, members of the audience and members of the house! i want to thank coelho and the others for a good job. we, the countries that have been able to join sis, have seen immediate results with the search registers in our work against the borderless crime that moves between the eu countries. we look forward to a more developed sis ii system in which all 25 and soon 27 countries can participate. in the reports made available to the public, this cooperation has given us both an instrument to stop former convicts from returning and a tool in the fight against drugs. we have also been given a tool in the fight against human traffickers who not only smuggle and transport children for prostitution but also for exploitation as labour. once sis ii is built and operational, we can provide effective evidence of cooperation to citizens. however, this also requires that we continue to work both against crime and for the protection of personal privacy. dialogue is needed if we are to live up to the expectations placed on us in the general debate on peace, security and justice as the basis of all our work. but can't we do this faster?
member of the commission. mr president, i would like to thank all the speakers for their excellent speeches. before i turn to the amendments, i would like to respond to the barnier report and say that the commission has already started work on many of the issues related to this report and that, of course, the most important of all is the development of civil protection units. meetings have been held with experts from the member states to determine which types of units should be available in each case and also as regards the specific terms and conditions for each type of unit. a second area of work is that of training, and although there is no provision for the creation of a european institute - as the barnier report suggests - we nevertheless propose to develop a network of centres which will extend the existing training programme. we also agree that the monitoring and information centre should be strengthened. and we have already asked for an increase in the number of staff, the staff, who will be employed in this centre. we have also asked the member states to give us the opportunity next year to use certain experts in the mic (monitoring and information centre). the commission has two legislative proposals: the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of the environment in the event of accidents at work is a good one, but it is not enough to say that the directive is a good one. now, as regards the amendments, the european parliament has tabled a number of important amendments aimed at strengthening the possibilities of civil protection at european level. the amendments concern the need to ensure timely delivery of civil protection assistance, the importance of early warning and alert systems, the interoperability of civil protection units and the role that the mechanism can play in helping european union citizens. the commission can accept most of the proposed amendments. the commission has already made a number of proposals, and i am sure that the commission will be able to accept them. these are those relating to the provision of measures on prevention, public health and intentional marine pollution. as regards the measures relating to prevention, the commission would like to stress that the proposal for the financial instrument for political protection will cover actions in this field. the community civil protection mechanism is intended to cover actions only in the areas of preparedness and intervention. the commission nevertheless agrees with the european parliament on the importance of preventive action and is committed to promoting it in the appropriate context. as regards the inclusion of public health, the commission recognises that civil protection actions are - very often - aimed at protecting public health. however, the mechanisms set up at european union level for civil protection are not part of public health policy, which is covered by other existing community instruments such as the community action programme in the field of health. now, as regards intentional, deliberate marine pollution, the commission fully supports the proposal that the civil protection mechanism should be able to intervene in cases of widespread marine pollution caused either by accident or by intentional causes. the european commission's proposal to extend the scope to cover man-made disasters also covers this possibility. however, the term 'intentional, deliberate' marine pollution usually refers to small illegal discharges of oil from ships and the commission wishes to avoid interpreting the scope of the regulation to cover such small-scale discharges. it would be practically impossible for the commission to mobilize the mechanism - the staff are not even there, as i said earlier - but of course even this kind of small rejection should be dealt with by the means available to each country. the amendments were therefore rejected. the commission notes that the amendments relate to aspects of existing legislation. the commission supports in substance some of these amendments. however, the interinstitutional agreement on the use of the recasting technique does not allow the commission to accept them unless they are essential for the implementation of the new provisions contained in the proposal or if they directly follow from the new provisions. the commission will, however, take these issues into account in the development of our policy in this area. the commission's position differs from the european parliament's report on these main points. i would like to stress, however, that these differences of opinion do not overshadow our common objectives. we fully share the european parliament's ambition to develop a much stronger european intervention capacity in the field of civil protection, both within and outside the european union, and we thank you for your support. i am forwarding to the secretariat of parliament a full list of the commission's position on the amendments. i would like to congratulate the rapporteur, mr papademos, again on his excellent work.
the european parliament is now considering a proposal to revise the emergency services mechanism, which was set up in 2001. there is no doubt that disasters cause great stress for the parties affected. however, a coordinated european rescue service is not the solution to the problem, especially not as this proposal looks. rescue services in each member state are financed by taxation. it is up to each state to choose how tax revenues from its citizens are to be distributed. this proposal may risk some member states opting to allocate a minimum of resources to national emergency services, relying on other eu countries to help in the event of a disaster. this 'free riding' problem is very serious and unfair to the citizens of those countries that choose to have a strong rescue service. as regards international operations, these should be coordinated through ocha and financed by the member states and not by the eu. the commission's documents also mention rapid reaction forces and the fact that member states must communicate what resources are available, including military resources and military capabilities, in cases where an affected country requests that type of support. the junilists are firmly opposed to this. the military resources of a country are a national matter and it is up to each member state to decide whether or not to mobilise them.
the questioner. president. - the debate is closed. i would like to begin by thanking karin jöns for her great commitment and for tackling this extremely important issue. as karin jöns has already said, this is the most common form of cancer among women. it is also the most common cause of death among women aged 35-59 years. we also know that breast cancer is increasing among younger women, and that the chances of detecting the disease vary greatly between the different member states of the european union. the availability of mammography varies from country to country. i know from my own country, sweden, where we have had mammography for a long time for women in certain age groups, that mammography has been effective in reducing mortality among women and that it is important to detect the disease early in order to be able to initiate the right treatment. all this makes the efforts contained in the resolutions mentioned by mrs jöns extremely important. three committees are involved in this work. we in the committee on employment are not specifically responsible for health and health care, but for the link with the labour market. fortunately, we now have a labour market which is willing to involve women, and women are increasingly represented on it. in my own country, the female employment rate is as high as the male. if you get a serious disease like breast cancer, one aspect of treatment is actually, you have to give time to treatment. sometimes you can remain in your working life, but then you must also be able to discuss with your employer how to adapt your working life to function during your treatment. or you may become seriously ill for a period of time, but recover from treatment and return to work. the question then is how to reintegrate in a way that allows me to function, so that the treatment for breast cancer cannot affect my relationship with the world of work in a negative way. it is important that we raise these issues - that is one aspect of the concern of the committee on employment. the second aspect is the staff working on these issues. there's always something happening in terms of research, development and methods of treating breast cancer. it is important that health-care professionals are kept up to date with new methods that are emerging. we have instruments at european level to help develop the skills of staff within the framework of the social fund. we must also be able to use the social fund for skills development for this activity, so that staff can use the best methods. this is also something for the lisbon process. the lisbon process is about best practice, and we will look at the countries that have come the furthest. in conclusion, i would like to say something that i also discussed with karin jöns. this is a disease that affects women, but it is important that we men commit ourselves to better practices. it is not just women who should be involved in better treatment of women's diseases, but all of us - men and women. member of the commission. mr president, i welcome the very positive responses; there may be still some pressure for doing more. we all want to do more for culture, because it is important for the european mentality and european identity; for living together in one broader and broader house. i think that the best narrative of europe is diversity and unity. diversity growing means an invitation to promote and deepen unity, and unity based on values is mostly promoted via our cultural programme. i am sure that some points that were mostly to do with the budget are invitations to do more in future. if you recall the previous programme, we had seven cents per culture, per year, per citizen – mr bono mentioned that now we will have 15 cents. there were invitations to do ten times more – 70 cents per culture. i just want to remind people that the budgetary authority in the union is parliament. your support, but also further support, will help to create more space. the citizenship programme is also based on the cultural article 151 of the treaty. first of all, this is the competence and basic responsibility of member states. we are supporting member states in their cooperation, not replacing them. we will have the european year of intercultural dialogue in 2008 and the achievement in negotiating a new convention on cultural diversity protection and promotion under unesco shows the success of this union. i want to assure parliament that the implementation of the programme as soon as possible after the final decision will allow us quantitatively and qualitatively to do more for culture via culture for europe. i am sure that we will have many occasions to work together on the implementation.
in writing. i would like to reiterate the junist position of 27 september this year. i voted against this report because it rejects the council of ministers' proposal for amending budget no 3. the junilist, unlike the majority in the committee on budgets, believes that the council of ministers can very well, if it deems it appropriate, reallocate expenditure between the various eu institutions during the implementation of the budget.
in writing. the amending budget is made up of three separate parts in completely different areas. i have chosen to vote against the whole package as it has been put forward. as regards the total of eur 259 million in financial support to stimulate the economic development of the turkish cypriot community, the question arises: is there still a comprehensive solution for the peaceful and stable development of cyprus when the eu is now stepping in and contributing significant financial resources? as regards the appropriation for the european year of equal opportunities for all, the junilista has already expressed scepticism about the whole project. as regards budgetary adjustments due to the modernisation of the accounting system, it is more of a technical matter, although i am sceptical about the overall operation of the european development fund.
in writing. i would like to reiterate the negative position of the junilists at the first reading of the case in september 2005. the question we should ask ourselves is this: is the eu supposed to deal with this type of policy issue or is this the responsibility of the member states? the junilists' answer is given, this is a question for the member states. if the member states wish to devote special resources to promoting the national film industry, they are of course fully entitled to do so. the eu, however, will not pursue specific programmes to promote the european film industry. if several eu countries consider it necessary to cooperate in this policy area, they can do so without eu involvement. intergovernmental agreements or cooperation between film companies may take place outside the eu. the eu does not need any more ambitious and costly projects. the eu should instead seek limited cooperation on important and genuinely crossborder issues.
in writing. of course, cultural matters are very important. junilista believes that cultural policy should, in principle, be managed by the member states. however, in the case of european cultural heritage, it may be justified to deal with certain issues at community level. we believe, however, that this cultural programme has been given too generous a grant for an activity which should be the responsibility of the member states. i have therefore voted against the amendment in this report.
in writing. we welcome the commission's proposal to improve the emergency services mechanism introduced in 2001. europe and the rest of the world are affected by all kinds of accidents and crises, and intensive coordination to respond to these is of course desirable. unfortunately, we have had to note that the commission proposal and papadimoulis' report make clear references to the need for military resources to be made available to the rescue service. we strongly oppose any initiative for military cooperation within the european union. that is why we decided to abstain in the final vote on the papadimoulis report today and we also decided to vote against the commission's proposal.
in writing. i object to the constitutional proposal to use each other's military efforts. however, a coordination center that can help move fire-fighting helicopters to where they are needed most at the moment is wise and cost-effective. in addition, in the event of major disasters, there is a severe shortage of highly advanced fire-rescue services, and it is very good if we can help each other quickly. this is precisely the kind of cooperation the eu should be pursuing. i vote for it.
in writing. the moderates voted today in favor of the report on women's immigration. the fundamental rights of immigrants must be respected and should not be dependent on gender or a specified group of people. we support a common regulation of migration policy, which is a natural continuation of schengen cooperation. we are opposed to a common integration policy, which is best handled by the member states themselves. we also do not want any records of female genital mutilation, as this constitutes a breach of privacy which we cannot support.
in writing. the junilist has previously stated that the eu is a union of values. i have therefore chosen to support formulations which make it clear that all groups in society should have equal rights. junilista supports constructive measures aimed at achieving greater integration of vulnerable groups in society. we believe, however, that the national parliaments and the regional authorities should be the ones to tackle this urgent issue. i am critical of the general trend of the eu institutions seeking to achieve greater influence and competence in an increasing number of areas. it is important to draw a clear line between what the eu decides and what is left to the national and local levels.
mr president, i would like to thank you. firstly, the junilists believe that the eu's common agricultural policy must be scrapped. as i have pointed out so many times before in this house, agricultural policy is reprehensible because the money goes to the wrong - and often very rich - beneficiaries. in addition, the world's poorest countries are being deprived of a real opportunity to sell their agricultural products on the eu market by subsidising an agriculture that is not competitive. secondly, the junilists believe that structural policy should be re-nationalised. the eu funds that return to sweden are highly conditional and in most cases not adapted to swedish needs. next year, sweden's contribution to the eu budget will increase to just over eur 3 billion. the return is approximately eur 1 billion. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the eu's agricultural and trade policies are fair and transparent.
member of the commission. madam member, i do not think the future of the euro is in question at all. the euro, a few years after its launch in 1999 and five years after more than 300 million europeans could have it in their pockets for the first time as a means of payment in coins and notes, is now a reality that is considered a success by those who use it. so, in my view, the question is not whether the euro has a future; the question is whether countries that are not properly preparing to adopt the euro will suffer the consequences in the future. that's the real question. every time i have contact with the authorities of countries which will have to adopt the euro in the future but which do not yet meet the criteria required by the treaty, i urge them to adopt a credible and effective strategy to meet those criteria and adopt the single currency. we are living in a time in recent years when there is abundant liquidity on the financial markets, interest rates are low and memories of monetary crises and exchange rate volatility are being left behind in the european territory, but no one can guarantee that these situations that we have known in the past, before the existence of the euro, will not reappear in the future. i hope they don't come up, but they can. we have experience of the advantages which the 311 million europeans derive from sharing a common currency, and we have even experience of the advantages which the fact that twelve of them have already adopted the common currency brings to all the european member states. there is therefore ample reason to convince the member states which have not yet adopted the euro to prepare and adopt economic policy decisions aimed at meeting these criteria and at strengthening a currency which, seven years after its birth, is already the second most important currency in the world.
the swedish people have voted by a large majority to reject joining emu. posselt wants to overturn the result of a referendum. it is not compatible with a democratic approach. sweden's economy has not been negatively affected by our being outside the single currency union. i would ask the commissioner to say that he fully supports the result of the swedish referendum and that he rejects mr posselt's regrettable and undemocratic approach.
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would like to begin by thanking all the shadow rapporteurs for their very constructive and good cooperation. it made it possible to find a solution at first reading with the council, which also worked well on this. this solution improves on the commission's original proposal and i am glad that the commission is now also accepting the compromise. pfos and pfos-related substances are highly fluoridated and extremely difficult to degrade. they are also highly bioaccumulative. we have learned that organochlorine compounds are toxic and cause problems in nature and we have seen that organobromine compounds are harmful. flame retardants threaten us more than the fires they were supposed to prevent. now we're in the luck of the fluoroorganic compounds. they're extremely stable, and the bond between fluorine and carbon is the strongest of all in organic chemistry. the stability and surface activity properties are what have made pfos a sought-after chemical. for many years, as the commissioner said, it was used in the coating of chemicals and textiles. the largest manufacturer realized the risks to its workers and consumers. the workers had high levels of mercury in them. they decided to withdraw the pfos product scotchguard from the market. the commission's aim in its proposal was to stop the reintroduction of pfos, but the compromise goes further. we now have stricter concentration rules and set the limit for preparations at 0.005 percent. this is justified as its surfactant characteristics allow pfos to be used in low concentrations. if the limit were set at 0.1%, there would be a risk that several applications would slip through the net of the legislation. the definition of areas of 1 microgram per square metre also has the same objective. the compromise with the council also means that pfos in fire-fighting foam will be banned. it makes perfect sense. already today there are alternatives on the market without fluoro-organic compounds. the commission is also proposing to extend the period of storage to 54 months. everyone has a vested interest in speeding up the settlement. the great oil fire in england, which produced millions of litres of pfos-contaminated water, has shown the costs of using pfos. the only way to destroy pfos is high-temperature combustion. each of us can calculate for himself how difficult it will be to burn millions of gallons of water at high temperatures. incremental is the other big area. the proposal now limits the use of non-decorative chromium-silicate hard chromium as a process tool. this derogation will also be reviewed following an inventory of remaining essential uses to be carried out by the member states within two years. here too, there are alternatives, such as larger enclosed systems, increased ventilation and, hopefully, other processes in the future. for the other exemptions such as photolithography processes and anti-reflective and photographic film treatment, these are very small quantities. however, these exemptions should also be abolished as soon as technically and economically feasible. i also want to mention pfoa. that is, the acids and salts of the same group of substances. in germany many germans know what it is like to have polluted water in the ruhr area, where people now have to get their drinking water from tankers. a costly and unsustainable solution. we can try to stop this pollution by the amendment now in the pfoa compromise, where the commission is to review and constantly monitor the development of alternatives. where safe alternatives are available, these shall replace pfoa. i think we're way ahead of our time here. we have to address this, because the next big environmental problem could be fluoroorganic chemistry and its variants. this is a first step towards protecting people and the environment from some of these substances. i would also point out that this is a first reading agreement and that parliament's support for this compromise means that the council is supporting it and that we will be ready before reach comes into force. then this is included as an addendum to reach. if we don't get this deal done by tomorrow, the bill will fall flat, because then reach will take over.
for the gue/ngl group. mr president, commission and members of the european parliament! we shall now decide on the restriction of the dangerous and harmful substances perfluorooctane sulphonate and the closely related perfluorooctane acid pfos and pfoa. i am particularly pleased that there is such widespread support for the solid work that my compatriot carl schlyter has put in place. good work, carl! i think we should bear this in mind when we take another extremely important decision later this autumn - for both the environment and our health - namely the reach chemicals act. i hope that there will be a great deal of support from the left and the right, from the commission to the council of ministers. pfos is, as already mentioned, a harmful substance. it is bioaccumulative, which means it is stored for a long time in the environment and in our bodies. a world wildlife fund study of people in 17 countries found pfos and six other perfluorocarbons in the human body. so it's high time we got a limit. i am therefore pleased that carl schlyter and a largely unanimous environment committee have tightened up the threshold for the amount of pfos that can be used on the market. i would also have liked the committee to have put in more clear timetables for when pfos is to be phased out of the market. with that reservation, i think this is an excellent report which i and my group gue/ngl look forward to voting in favour of. i want to finish where i started. it is good that parliament is restricting substances that are harmful to health and the environment. and it is very good that there is such a broad support for these measures. so i hope we will keep that in mind when we make decisions on reach later this autumn. let us, then, have broad support for a strong chemicals legislation.

in writing. terrorism, in all its forms, is a heinous phenomenon that mostly affects innocent civilians. a true democrat must, in any event, reject and condemn such acts. parliament has today considered motions for resolutions from several political groups on the peace process in spain. unfortunately, the rejection of terrorism and the support for the peace process have been overshadowed by political power struggles which are really about spanish politics. it is a question which must ultimately be decided in the sovereign state of the kingdom of spain. it is therefore very sad to see how various political forces in the european parliament are trying to exploit this tragic chapter in spanish history in an opportunistic way. in the light of the above, i have voted today against all the motions for resolutions on the peace process in spain.
in writing. the content of the programme is open to question. the rapporteur wrote in his explanatory statement at first reading that it 'contributes significantly to active citizenship of young people in society and deepens their sense of belonging to the eu, which can bring significant european added value'. we question whether the programme really makes any substantial contribution to this objective. international youth exchanges are a positive thing, but they must be solved financially through the efforts of civil society or through the provision of funds by the member states. we do not believe that the eu should engage in this kind of activity.
in writing. i would like to reiterate the negative position of the junilists at the first reading of the case in october 2005. although the junilists are supporters of the erasmus programme, for example, there are other parts of the subprogramme proposals which need to be examined carefully to determine their validity, given that each member state is responsible under the treaty for the organisation and content of education and training systems. overall, too much money has been allocated to too many projects. we question the existence and financing of some of these projects through the eu.
in writing. i would like to reiterate the negative position of the junilists at the first reading of the case in april 2006. we are very much against several of the positions taken by the european parliament's committee on culture and education on this programme. the work on this case has been based primarily on a disagreement with the low turnout in the 2004 european parliament elections and the rejection of the constitutional treaty in referendums in france and the netherlands. the 'citizens for europe' programme has nothing to contribute to changing the political situation. the work done by local amateur sports associations in this programme breeds contempt for citizens. we also note the importance of the peoples of europe cherishing their memories of dictatorships and tragedies in their history. but it has to be done at national level. this is not something for the eurocrats in brussels to concern themselves with. the junilists therefore rejected the amendments to the agenda tabled by the committee on culture.
in writing. the establishment of sis ii is seen by eu-critical forces as another building block of a common eu police force and as part of the fabric that, on a case-by-case basis, forms an eu state. the eu is developing into the ultimate closed control society that many swedes feared when sweden joined. this is happening with the blessing of politicians on both the left and the right, who, with populist messages such as law enforcement and external border controls, are contributing to the building of a de facto orwellian society. the junilist is for cross-border police cooperation. it is necessary to deal with international crime today, but this has been successfully done for decades by the international police agency interpol. the cost of another information system that is only valid within the european union is therefore superfluous. junilista is very sceptical about the fact that the entities in the member states responsible for issuing vehicle registration certificates should have access to highly sensitive personal data. the management of such data should be a national concern. all states must be able to guarantee their citizens protection against unauthorised access to their personal data. the junilist therefore votes against all three reports on sis ii and access to the system.
in writing. are eu actions against breast cancer more effective than those of established internationally recognised organisations with broad expertise, such as the who? the junilists' answer to this question is no. the european parliament should not dictate to what extent member states should implement significant measures such as mammography examinations, breast cancer awareness campaigns, breast cancer research, the establishment of cancer registries and the establishment of breast cancer information centres. the appropriate coordination and crossborder cooperation is preferably carried out within an organisation such as the who, rather than within the eu. i have therefore voted against the resolution in question.
in writing. the junilist believes that free trade is desirable because it leads to an increase in the prosperity of all parties involved. it is worth noting that, until joining the eu, sweden was one of the most free trade-friendly countries in the world. the eu sometimes pursues a very protectionist policy, to protect sectors which are not competitive on the international market. agricultural aid is an example of this, where agricultural products are subsidised, with the result that equivalent products from other countries, often developing countries, cannot be sold on the eu market. a well-functioning world trade system is of the utmost importance if free trade is to become a reality. however, the muscardini report is protectionist, which is why i voted against it in today's vote.
for the verts/ale group. it is a tragic event that has struck the ivory coast and we all express our sympathy and solidarity with the victims of the criminal behaviour of european companies. victims must be given all the support that can be offered to them in this process. we have had a ban on the export of hazardous waste to non-oecd countries since 1997. it is time to make sure that it is properly implemented. it is unacceptable that the dutch authorities did not take their responsibility when they had the chance to stop the ship. all credit to estonia for doing what was needed. i would like to thank commissioner dimas for your rapid recognition of the seriousness of the situation. the commission must now make a rapid proposal for stricter enforcement. we need to ensure that every time someone undertakes to transport hazardous waste, there is a person with personal responsibility who signs a liability policy. the board and the executive director must also be given greater personal responsibility. otherwise, traditions with unclear and complicated responsibilities will make it difficult to approach unscrupulous carriers and shipowners. africa has suffered enough from colonialism and slavery and the exploitation of its natural resources. we must now stop this latest form of neo-colonialism where we dump our waste in poorer countries if we are to call ourselves civilized.

in writing. we want the budget to contribute to sustainable social, economic and environmental development for the eu in line with the lisbon strategy. we have therefore voted in favour of the amendments which give priority to research, development and education. however, we do not want to support the amendments which provide more money for direct agricultural aid and export refunds for agricultural products, as this aid is already oversized. this support also results in inefficient agriculture that harms the environment and, by dumping surpluses, contributes to widening the gap between the world's poor and rich. we also do not want to give money to tobacco farming, which directly contributes to public health problems. we believe that this aid should be reduced rapidly and that it should be abolished in the near future. the money thus saved can be used, among other things, for measures to reduce tobacco use and dependence.
in writing. the junilists believe that the eu budget should be limited to 1.00% of the average gnp of the member states. i have therefore voted against all the increases proposed by the european parliament, while junilista welcomed the few savings proposed by either the committee on budgets or individual members as amendments. there are other unfortunate budget lines, but junilista particularly regrets the high support for the eu's agricultural policy, the cohesion fund and the fisheries sector, as well as the budget lines which include support for various forms of information campaigns. the junilists also believe that the european parliament's constant travel between strasbourg and brussels must be corrected and that the european economic and social committee and the committee of the regions should be abolished. this week (23/10/2006) the european court of auditors found for the 13th consecutive year that it cannot be held responsible for more than a small part of the eu budget being used correctly or for its intended purpose. how can the madness continue, year after year, of allocating funds that can only to a small extent be guaranteed to go to their intended purpose?
in writing. we are opposing a continued expansion of the eu-budget, which today is already too extensive with a large part of the funds going to the wrong ends. today there seem to be a reciprocal action between new objectives for the union and demands on financing via eu. these mean new demands on payments from the member states, but also that new funds are being transferred to a system which is bureaucratic, inefficient and complex. however, there are many desirable initiatives being financed via the budget, and we support reasonable changes of existing means. but our opposition to a constantly increasing budget is firm, and therefore we have chosen to vote against the report in the final vote.
in writing. we have voted today in favour of the reports on the eu budget for 2007. we welcome the approach of giving taxpayers more value for their money. we also support the efforts made in research and development and the ambition to strengthen the eu as a global player. we reject, however, the proposals to increase the appropriations for agricultural and regional aid. as always, we are also sceptical of centrally designed campaigns and opinion formation.
in writing. the junilist believes that the eu should not interfere in the internal affairs of other countries. it is important, for reasons of principle, to follow the policy we have chosen. there is no doubt that syria should improve its relationship with israel. the junilist is a staunch opponent of all totalitarian regimes and we condemn all countries that choose to support terrorism in all its forms. it is clear that the eu's foreign policy is guided by the different interests of certain member states in regions around the world. i find this policy very insidious and destructive, as the present report is a good example. the eu is trying to influence syria's relations with its neighbours, which cannot be considered acceptable. i choose to abstain for reasons of principle.
in writing. the report stresses that the posting of workers directive is not fully applied by all member states. it is therefore necessary to take a number of measures to ensure that member states can effectively guarantee minimum standards of protection and employment conditions for workers temporarily working in another eu country. junilista supports the application of the host country's legislation to workers working in other member states. this has been established, among other things, in the debates on the services directive. i have therefore voted in favour of the report as a whole.

for the gue/ngl group. mr president, commission, members of the european parliament and members of the public! i would like to begin by thanking mr garcía-margallo y marfil for this report on the situation in the euro area. the rapporteur notes that the outlook for the euro area is better than it has been for some time. of course, this depends on the economic policy that is being pursued. the policy pursued has not been a success. large parts of europe are in a social and economic crisis. in europe's largest economy, germany, seven million people live in poverty, according to new statistics. one of the reasons for this is precisely the economic and monetary union, emu, which has the world's most inflation-conservative central bank and a stability pact that underpins economic policy. as the european trade union etuc recently stated in a report, we do not need more of this inflexible policy. the people of europe need an active fiscal policy and a reorganization of the emu. as you know, my home country sweden is not a member of the emu. a clear majority voted no in 2003. that is why i am a little concerned when i read the report. it states that the euro area will comprise 13 member states from 2007 onwards, but that i now quote the coordination of macroeconomic policies and the single market will cover all 27 member states. what does this mean? does this mean that sweden, which voted against emu, will have to join sooner or later? i would be grateful for a reply from the commission and also from the rapporteur if he is allowed to speak at the end of the debate.
mr president, firstly i should like to thank the rapporteur for his report. it underlines the need for cooperation in strengthening the european economy. it is fair to say that the euro has provided europe with stability that we have never seen before. when i listened earlier to my swedish colleague, it sounded as though it was a problem for the european economy to have low interest rates. however, the truth is that we have lower interest rates than ever before. does anyone think that the european economy would have been better if we had the interest rates of the 1970s or 1980s, or if we had the budget deficits of those years when europe was losing out in the european economy? let us stop dreaming and look at the reality. today we have a common currency that places the emphasis on and encourages competition, trade and investment and focuses on the need for structural reforms, as well as the need for fiscal discipline. that is good. that provides us with opportunities, because the problem of the european economy is not the interest rates, it is the lack of deregulation, it is the lack of more trade and of more of the internal market. we can see that we are more efficient and more successful since we opened up the markets. i believe that the euro will never be stronger than the european economy and the discipline of the countries participating in the euro. that also underlines the need for the european central bank to be independent, as underlined in the report. that is very important, because otherwise we will lose credibility. from a swedish perspective, we will soon have the euro in a number of new countries around the baltic sea. i hope we will have a debate in my country that will make it possible for us to join them and other euro countries in the years to come.
for the verts/ale group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would like to thank the rapporteurs for their exemplary work and the excellent reports they have written. they improve the commission's proposal by requiring good status to be achieved in 2017 instead of 2021. in addition, the objectives are made binding and the importance of marine reserves is emphasised. climate change, waste, unsustainable fishing, noise, over-fertilisation and the extraction of raw materials are all threats to our oceans. if the mass destruction and natural disaster that happens in the deep ocean were visible on land, the debate on marine protection would dominate everyday life. flocks of fish, birds and mammals are threatened with extinction. the marine strategy can be a small step in trying to tackle the problems, if it is as ambitious as the committee on the environment proposes. member states must take responsibility for establishing significantly more marine reserves. we have national parks in every country, but marine reserves shine with their absence where they're needed most. there is overwhelming statistical evidence that marine reserves provide protection that both increases fisheries and biodiversity in the surrounding areas. when fish are allowed to grow in shelter, the older fish spread out and produce better catches. the baltic sea in particular is a very sensitive sea with brackish waters. the baltic sea countries must always be able to impose stricter requirements on water protection if they consider it necessary, without being constantly threatened by lengthy legal proceedings to put the internal market before the protection of the sea. i would like to stress the importance of the committee's demand that no subsidies be given to farming which leaks large amounts of nutrients into the sea. my amendments 81 and 82 aim to regulate emissions from waste storage in a serious way. the original proposal is too weak on this point. i am opposed to amendment no 90 to remove the reference to radioactive substances, as this would be contrary to the original objective of 2002. finally, the commission the requirements of the maritime strategy for the exploitation of the seas must be adapted and modified to fit within the framework of the maritime strategy.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to thank lienemann and kušķis, who have done a very meritorious job with these directives. i myself live by the baltic sea and have therefore unfortunately seen firsthand what it means for individuals, but also for employment and growth, when the marine ecosystems are out of balance. i also know that people expect us in this house to take a strong stance and do something about the problems they face every day. i therefore hope that tomorrow parliament will not listen to the previous speakers but will give broad support to the amendments proposed by the committee on the environment. firstly, a much faster process, which means that measures are introduced earlier and a much more ambitious timetable for achieving the objective of good environmental status in our seas than the commission has proposed. secondly, more clearly defined objectives which, as mr lienemann said, guarantee that we actually achieve something and that it does not just end up as a snowball. thirdly, the baltic sea, which is perhaps the worst affected, should be a pilot area for this strategy, where measures should be introduced at an early stage, preferably on the basis of the helsinki convention. it's necessary to save the baltic sea.
mr president, i would like to thank you. mr commissioner, i would like to thank you for your reply. it is good to be back, and i have started my work by reading two good reports. the ability of our oceans to survive the pressures on the marine environment is a responsibility for all of us. the european union and this house have a special responsibility to find ways of working across borders with coordinated and powerful measures. time is, as many have pointed out, short, extremely short. in the part of europe where i live, the baltic sea is the most important sea and the sea that has been and still is the most threatened. that is why we are very concerned about the planned gas pipeline from russia to germany. the more detailed plans will be presented tomorrow. a gas pipeline in the sensitive baltic sea poses a major environmental threat. the pipeline could be damaged by ships, by old mines left behind and exposed to terrorist attacks. the pipeline poses a threat to the environment and fisheries, both at the time of construction and during its lifetime. the risks, in my view, far outweigh the benefits. europe certainly needs energy, but this natural gas pipeline will not be laid on the bottom of the baltic sea. if the gas pipeline is to be built, it should be on land for the sake of the marine environment and the baltic sea!
member of the commission. ladies and gentlemen, i listened to your debate with interest and i am pleased to say that it generally appreciates the overall approach adopted by the commission. on the other hand, it is clear that the question is so complex and often urgent that there are different views on many issues and that many issues still require further investigation and clarification. let me now go into some of the major changes in detail. in the implementation timetable (amendments 20, 24, 31, 32, 34, 35 and 69), the commission proposed 2021 as the deadline for member states to achieve 'good environmental status' of the marine environment. the environment committee has suggested an earlier date. while the commission would undoubtedly like to see 'good environmental status' achieved quickly, i do not think it is realistic to postpone the implementation deadline. the commission's proposal balances the need to improve our understanding of the marine environment and the threats it faces, firstly, with the need to act, secondly. to find the most appropriate measures to protect the marine environment with the least cost and the greatest efficiency, we must arm ourselves with the necessary knowledge and skills. under these conditions, moving the deadlines forward would be counterproductive. this is evident, especially when we consider the analogy with the current water framework directive, to which this directive is closely linked. the water framework directive, successfully adopted in 2000 with ms lienemann as rapporteur, requires 'good environmental status' by 2015, 15 years after the adoption of the directive. the same would apply to the draft directive on the seas. given the extent of the seas covered by the marine strategy directive and the scale of the task, it should be clear to everyone that the timetable we are already proposing is very ambitious. moreover, the deadline of 2021 proposed by the commission in this directive will coincide with the first review of river basin management plans under the water framework directive, which will ensure synergies in the further implementation of both directives. the commission also considers that it is not practical to require member states to 'achieve good environmental status by 2021' as proposed by the environment committee, in accordance with the directive's objective. that wouldn't be realistic either. the measures to be taken as part of the marine strategy will be fully operational in 2018, according to the proposed timetable. some measures taken will not produce immediate results, as it may take ecosystems some time to respond to the measures. however, there should be an overall positive trend towards good environmental status, which is why the commission proposed the concept of 'achieving good environmental status'. as regards marine protected areas (amendments 27, 39, 62 and 72), the commission can support the introduction of an article which will highlight their importance. such an article should ideally be based on article 6 of the water framework directive, which deals with protected areas. the commission may also support the idea that additional protected areas or even closed nature reserves may be necessary to achieve the objectives of the directive. however, we cannot accept the environment committee's proposal to introduce an obligation to create marine protected areas as part of the implementation of the proposed directive. marine protected areas should only be established if they can directly contribute to the achievement of 'good environmental status'. they should not therefore be created for their own sake, but should be seen as a means, not an end. as regards the key theme of the characteristics of 'good environmental status' (amendment 80), the commission agrees with the committee's wish to incorporate the characteristics directly into the directive. however, the commission has some problems with the list of characteristics as proposed by the environment committee. many of the characteristics are based on specific impacts on the marine environment and threats, rather than on the quality of the ecosystem. such an approach is dangerous because some potential risks and threats may be overlooked or, conversely, unduly emphasised. more importantly, if we were to look only at impacts, the eu would not be able to move from the current fragmented approach to marine environmental management to a more holistic approach that takes into account all impacts and their interactions on the marine environment. finally, as regards the financing issues (amendments 19 and 74), there are several eu financial instruments from which member states will be able to draw, in particular the structural funds, life+, the 7th framework programme for research, and it is therefore not necessary to create specific financial mechanisms. ladies and gentlemen, the debate has touched on the complexity of the whole problem, including the fact that the marine environment is being polluted by the atmosphere, as well as the fact that the quality of the marine environment is highly dependent on climate change management. it was also mentioned that some seas are not included, it was the black sea issue, but the approach of bulgaria and romania is fundamentally changing the situation and opening up new possibilities. as regards the arctic ocean, the geographical situation does not allow for a significant change in the european commission's position and for the development of a separate strategy for this particularly sensitive and important environment, which is not directly linked to the territory of the european union. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the list of commission positions on amendments has been forwarded to the parliament's secretariat.
the attention paid to marine issues is very welcome, as there is a great need to ensure that our seas are not polluted or destroyed even more. the amendments tabled by the committee on the environment are largely positive and will hopefully improve the state of the marine environment, not least by defining good environmental status. however, there is one article in the commission proposal which raises some concerns. article 13 on specific areas allows for partial disregard of environmental objectives where the changes in an area are the result of measures taken in the public interest. it is to be expected that this article will be quoted extensively. it is therefore of the utmost importance that the commission is very restrictive if and when this happens, because our future environment for all is also of great public interest.
for the ind/dem group. president. - the debate is closed. we all say that the court of auditors has produced a good report; it is enlightening and clearer than it usually is. what's it say? it shows that the abuses continue to the same extent, and we must ask ourselves whether we are going to continue to live with these abuses. this is, of course, a completely unreasonable approach to the problems. we must look at how and why this is happening and come up with clear proposals from the commission on what to do. it must be noted that most of the things that go wrong are related to agricultural policy. there are many other reasons for changing the agricultural policy, namely to abolish it altogether. that would eliminate a very large part of the problems we see in this context. i would nevertheless remind you that this house bears a very large part of the blame for the loss of the eu's reputation among the people of europe. this house always votes to keep everyone on their back and in practice never calls for accountability. it was pathetic to hear the leaders of the pse group and the ppe-de group stand here in the chamber and, almost in tears, say that they felt obliged to postpone the discharge to parliament itself because of the scandal with the premises here in strasbourg. although i had shown that the committee of the regions should not be discharged, it was nevertheless discharged. we must not continue like this!
for the verts/ale group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. in sweden, mercury thermometers were banned over 20 years ago. it is finally time for the eu to follow suit! mercury in measuring instruments is a technology of the past, a deadly chemical that we must end as soon as possible. any claim that mercury is harmless is absurd. worse still, mercury that leaks, ends up in incinerators or is otherwise dispersed and transformed in the environment into readily absorbable forms that are even more harmful to humans and animals. we cannot continue to manufacture barometers which are harmful to the environment for nostalgic reasons. anyone who wants to can buy a harmless electronic replica that looks exactly like an old barometer or buy a used one. the directive is about putting new barometers on the market. now barometers were given a two-year reprieve in the compromise, which is completely unnecessary technically, as the commissioner said, but unfortunately necessary politically to reach a compromise because of too much sensitivity to corporate lobbying. anyone who votes for anything other than the compromise is sabotaging the entire legislation and stopping all phasing out of all thermometers. then we'll have to start over again within the confines of reach. the commission shall, within two years, make proposals for the phasing out, where possible, of other measuring equipment, in particular blood pressure monitors. i would remind the commission that the compromise states that as soon as there are reliable alternatives. for blood pressure monitors, in many cases, there is. go home to brussels immediately and compile a report on the market restriction of blood pressure monitors when possible.
in writing. i believe that eu financial support for the fishing industry should be abolished as soon as possible and that the member states responsible for the withdrawal of fisheries should themselves replace the developing countries affected by eu fisheries agreements. this should not be financed from the eu budget. i have nevertheless voted in favour of the report because it reduces the scope for the special national interest to influence the aid.
in writing. it is unacceptable that the eu is now trying to bind all its member states to a third party in an international agreement. the whole idea of the un convention against corruption is based on voluntary action and respect for national self-determination. in his report, the rapporteur, mr catania, has chosen to invoke 26 different articles of the treaty in support of his proposal. none of these articles gives the eu the power or competence to conclude a legally binding agreement with a third party. the eu should instead focus on the widespread corruption within its own institutions. corruption within the eu is a growing problem that is eroding citizens' confidence in the european idea. the junilist considers that the eu is overstepping its powers and therefore votes against the proposal as a whole.
in writing. the junilist believes that the common agricultural policy (or rural development as it will be called from 2007) should be abolished. this week (23 october 2006) the european court of auditors found for the 13th consecutive year that it cannot be held responsible for more than a small fraction of the 105 billion euro from the eu budget being used correctly or for its intended purpose. agricultural policy aid is one of the most difficult areas to control, and in some sectors, such as olive oil production, there are major problems with control. this can't go on. the eu's funding funnel must be shut down. we have therefore voted against this report.
in writing. we shall vote against this report, but we shall abstain on the commission's proposal in this case. the report's explanatory statement contains a number of views, including criticism of the abandonment or renationalisation of the cap, which junilista considers to be a good thing, and that it is contrary to the right of the european parliament to participate, a view which junilista does not share. in addition, the explanatory memorandum states that the health check, or the mid-term review as we prefer to call it, of the long-term budget planned for 2008/2009 is to form the basis of proposals for the financing period after 2013 only. that view is certainly not shared by junilistan. we believe that the mid-term review should lead to cuts in the agricultural/rural development budget already in the current 2007-2013 budget period.
in writing. although sweden has abstained in a referendum on joining emu, we have voted against the report and wish to explain why we have done so. the report gives a good picture of how emu goes hand in hand with the creation of a european state. the report calls for coordination of macroeconomic policies among the euro area countries and for the euro area to have a voice in international financial institutions and forums. better fiscal and budgetary coordination is advocated, and national budgetary plans are to be coordinated. the objective of establishing a common consolidated tax base in europe could be achieved by means of the enhanced cooperation mechanism, if member states cannot reach a unanimous agreement. we can only note that the 'no' side in the 2003 swedish referendum on emu was quite correct in their argument that emu is a major step towards a united states of europe.
in writing. in this commentary on the marine environment strategy, the rapporteur raises several interesting and important issues. the commission also stresses that the common fisheries policy must meet the requirements of sustainable development, while allowing member states to decide on emergency measures to protect a particular stock. the report also calls for the establishment of a european union-wide network of national authorities responsible for the protection of the environment. a good idea would be to draw on resources from the structural funds, for example. we have taken the view that the positive aspects of the report outweigh the negative aspects, and that is why we voted in favour of the report in today's vote.
for the alde group. mr president, commission, members and the public! i shall focus on three items in the commission's work programme, namely global competitiveness, green growth and energy. all three are crucial components of a competitive europe. firstly, global competitiveness. the future of europe lies in competing in the front lines of research with quality and with knowledge. only then can the european model be preserved. the seventh framework programme is therefore the most important industrial policy document for many years. i regret to say that this approach is partly lacking in this work programme. space research is good and important, but where are the ambitions for the medical industry? why is it only mentioned in connection with cybercrime? if i had one wish, it would be for a more coherent strategic plan to compete in the 21st century. secondly, the environment and growth. all too often, when such documents are presented, one gets the feeling that the environment and growth are somehow at odds. i'm convinced that's not the case, as i think the work with reach proves. in the coming decades, the world will be forced to do something about climate change. it is about new technologies for saving energy, new technologies for powering cars and so on. there will be a gigantic market for environmentally friendly technology and for energy-efficient products. that is where our european companies should be. i would therefore like the commission to devote more effort in the coming year to developing environmentally-friendly growth. thirdly, energy. europe's competitiveness depends on a secure and economically viable energy supply. it is easier to fight for its principles than to live up to them, but i hope the commission will do well in the areas of global competitiveness, green growth and secure and sustainable energy supply.
for the ind/dem group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. legal certainty means that the exercise of power and the administration of justice can be predicted by the rule of law. the commission's legislative and work programme for 2007 clearly shows that the commission expects the draft constitution to be implemented. this is despite the fact that large majorities in the netherlands and france have voted against this proposal. the struggle between the political establishment and the peoples of europe is thus continuing. the european project is on a dangerous path. the court of justice will lose its legitimacy because of its arrogant attitude towards the elected governments of the member states. the court of justice is in fact a self-appointed authority in europe. it chose, even before the 1998 amsterdam summit, to appoint itself as the guardian of the principle of the separation of powers. this was done without any democratic or political anchoring. the court of justice has also made it clear to the national courts that they cannot consider themselves independent, but are the extension of the court of justice's legal activity. i therefore urge the members of this house to think about it before it is too late. the eu does not need further concentration of power without popular support. the eu needs competition, not just between companies in the market to create better products, but between countries to find good policy solutions to the problems of our time.

president of the council. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the council has taken note of the european parliament's resolution on trade and economic relations between the eu and india. the 7th eu-india summit, held in helsinki on 13 october, reviewed trade and economic relations between the two parties. the summit focused on the report of the eu-india high level working group on trade. the working group was set up at the 2005 summit to examine ways of deepening and broadening bilateral trade and investment relations. the working group recommended the development of a comprehensive trade partnership through the negotiation of a comprehensive trade and investment agreement. the working group also considered that a substantial and comprehensive agreement would bring significant benefits to both the eu and india. the working group recommended that future negotiations cover the areas identified in the report, such as trade in goods and services, investment, trade promotion, public procurement, technical regulations, intellectual property and geographical indications, competition policy and dispute settlement. the discussions at the summit on this subject were constructive and supportive of the work of the working group. the eu-india summit welcomed the work of the working group and supported the proposal for a future comprehensive trade and investment agreement. the summit considered that both parties should promote the opening of negotiations for such an agreement. both sides are currently holding internal discussions on the matter. the summit also reaffirmed the commitment and commitment of both sides to the strategic partnership and close cooperation that has helped strengthen eu-india relations.

in writing. the junilista welcomes the services directive and believes that service companies, regardless of their country of origin, should not be discriminated against in any eu country. the debate on the services directive has centred on whether the country of origin principle should be the guiding principle of the directive or whether the host country's legislation should be fully applicable. the country of origin principle concerns important but very limited areas such as construction, installation and consultancy services. we welcome competition in these areas, but believe that it should be on a level playing field for all parties. we believe that swedish rules should apply on swedish territory. it is therefore gratifying that the council in its common position rejects the principle of the country of origin. the council's position is essentially a victory for the views of the junilist. competition in the services sector will be intensified. at the same time, the national autonomy of the member states will be respected, since the principle of country of origin is not the basis of the directive. it is positive that national monopolies in services will not be affected and that national legislation on labour law, social security legislation, collective agreements and trade union rights will not be affected.
