draftsman of the opinion of the committee on women's rights and gender equality. – this report is the result of enhanced cooperation, pursuant to rule 47, between two committees, libe and femm. i am therefore partly responsible for this report, specifically the parts dealing with trafficking of women and girls for sexual exploitation. seventeen years: that is the age of the typical victim of trafficking in europe. she is a young girl ending up in a brothel or in a so-called escort service in germany, austria or the netherlands. seventeen years: that is the same length of time that this parliament has been debating and producing papers on the issue of trafficking. people say that we should stick to what we have been doing in this parliament until now. but i say we have to stop the wishful thinking and the empty talk and resolutions and start dealing with this issue in a practical way. first, we have to identify the enemy. it is organised crime. while we have been talking, they have changed their agenda from weapons and drugs to the more profitable buying and selling of human beings for the sex industry. we are talking commodities. we are talking merchandise. we are talking regular markets where naked young girls are sold to the highest bidder. we are talking about girls who are sold at the age of 17 and at the age of 20 are worthless and thrown aside. i have met them and listened to unbelievable horrors of being raped 14 times a day. do we want to end this, or do we just want to go on talking? this organised crime industry feeds on the demands of ordinary men who believe they have a right to buy the body of a young girl as cheaply as possible. some people say trafficking in women and girls for sexual exploitation is already a crime, while prostitution is merely a matter of consenting adults making a business transaction. but more than 90% of the girls in the sex industry in germany, austria and the netherlands, where prostitution is legal, are from abroad. how can you know that this girl is doing it of her own free will? how can you be sure she is not smiling only because she knows someone will threaten her family back home if she does not comply? if you see this as just another job, how come you would not want your daughter to do it? or your own wife? the demand from men to buy sex is also a driving force in the trafficking business. if you agree with me that we need to really do something about trafficking, please join me in voting against amendments 23, 24 and 25 tomorrow. commissioner frattini, i am very grateful for your work, but we are still awaiting the study on how the laws on prostitution in europe affect the number of trafficking victims. that is needed if we are to find the best practice.
for the pse group. mr president, commissioner frattini, members and audience! i would like to begin by thanking mrs bauer for a good report and the libe committee for its good cooperation and for the increased cooperation with the committee on women. i would also like to thank commissioner frattini. in the two and a half years that i have been in the european parliament, the issue of trafficking in human beings has been high on the political agenda, both here and in the member states and among our citizens. that's a good start. i am very pleased that we have a common view on condemning human trafficking, as we did at the world cup. i welcome the fact that we are now including other aspects of human trafficking and that we agree that what we are talking about is the modern day slave trade and that at least 80 percent are women and children. what concerns me is the lack of analysis of what happens to women and children once they are here in the eu. it is not helpful to be upset about crossborder trade if you do not see or want to talk about what happens next in the eu. the eu market and sex buyers buy women and children indiscriminately. when i meet women who have been trafficked, they tell me that they have been bought and sold to prostitution and to customers in many, many eu countries every day, all year round. it has been possible because we in the eu still dare not speak about the women and children who are here and now. it's time to change attitudes. the next step is to gain more knowledge. that 50 percent of children are sold into prostitution is a scandal! we should be ashamed every day that we are not doing more, because in the eu we are responsible for children's rights. i am proud to say that we in the pse group agree on how we want to proceed. we want to set goals, complete studies and get better knowledge. then maybe we can agree on what's needed in the short and long term. if you other parties want to know, i can tell you that we are opening up a debate on various solutions to trafficking in human beings in the member states, such as legalisation or the law on sex trafficking as in sweden. why is the ppe-de group afraid of university studies? i'd like to know. to be against it tonight is to say that we in the european parliament are concerned about human trafficking, but we stick our heads in the sand like the ostrich and do not take responsibility for what happens to all the women and children who are exploited here and now. many citizens say that we mostly talk here in the eu without setting concrete targets. that is why the pse group and i want to set the target of halving the number of people trafficked within the eu within 10 years. the pse group also supports amendments nos 1 to 22 and recital p. in the vote on recital o we shall abstain, since we do not take a position either for or against any particular inquiry. we shall vote differently on amendment no 25. i will vote for paragraph 1 (a) and against amendment no 25 based on my political experience of the view on sex trafficking and prostitution in sweden. the most important thing now is the halving target and the implementation of the planned eu 2007 survey. i would like to thank all citizens for this discussion. i would like to thank everyone who has taken part in the debate here in the house, as well as the president and commissioner frattini.
in writing. in today's resolution on the situation in gaza, which i voted for, i would like to call for a more balanced account of the reasons why israel, and the israeli army, are being forced to use methods which in themselves can be seen as unreasonable. the right to protect one's own citizens is fundamental and should be assessed as a whole, not as individual events.
in writing. we support the via baltica motorway project, provided that a sound environmental assessment is carried out.
in writing. the junilist welcomes the fact that the baltic sea is being raised on the political agenda. the report contains many positive elements, including that due attention should be paid to the environmental problems in the area. we also take a very positive view of paragraph 13, which states that member states should be allowed to introduce stricter environmental rules than those proposed by the eu. however, we do not believe that the eu should finance the via baltica motorway project. it is also proposed to extend cooperation with europol. we can support this if it will only improve the exchange of information. but this cannot mean that we get a european arrest warrant or that one state's police can carry out searches in another state. we have found that the positive aspects of the report outweigh the negative aspects, and in today's vote we have supported the report as a whole.
in writing. the main objective of the report is to emphasise that the baltic sea is an extra sensitive sea with brackish waters. paragraph 13, which gives member states the right to have stricter legislation to protect the baltic sea, and paragraph 11, which requires environmental impact assessments for all energy projects, are crucial to saving the baltic sea. that is why i am voting in favour of the report, despite the negative reports about increased border controls and more unsustainable infrastructure projects in the region.
in writing. the report we are voting on today is a wish list for the advocates of a militarized european state. fortunately, it is only an own-initiative report, but it is nevertheless a clear signal to the other eu institutions in which direction the majority of the european parliament wants the eu to develop. one of the most absurd ideas put forward is the establishment of a permanent mediterranean fleet. the committee also calls for the establishment of a special budget line for military operations and for an attempt to revive the failed constitutional proposal. as always when the european parliament has a say, it is intended to give more power to the members of this house. we have also taken a position on amendments calling for an eu-led coastguard. the developments described in the report are very worrying and should be a wake-up call, even for the most committed advocates of eu statecraft. given the uncertainties we face today, as a result of the many conflicts going on around the world, one should ask whether the creation of an eu army is the right way to tackle the problems. whether or not a nation state should send troops must always be decided by national parliaments, never by the united states of the european union. we have therefore voted against the report, including most of the amendments tabled to it.
in writing. the eu's common foreign and security policy must not become a competitor to nato. it is therefore gratifying that the european parliament has taken a clear stand in favour of strengthening the transatlantic link and highlighting the importance of closer cooperation with nato. today, there is a common foreign and security policy within the eu. the report is, however, a step towards deeper foreign and security policy cooperation which could mean that sweden loses the possibility of deciding its own foreign and security policy. however, the member states' own military resources should continue to form the basis of this cooperation. it is regrettable that the report expresses support for the european arrest warrant, which means that swedish citizens can be extradited to other eu countries without the possibility of judicial review in sweden. the report also contains language supporting the constitution. this is why i voted against the final vote, even though i share the report's intention to strengthen eu cooperation with nato.
in writing. we voted in favour of the report on inheritance and wills (a6-0359/2006) to clarify the rules on the choice of law. however, since the report calls for a harmonised law on inheritance and wills, we would like to emphasize that harmonisation of substantive law is not desirable, but must, in accordance with the ec treaty, be and remain a purely national competence.
in writing. the report would create legal chaos and would pit national legal norms and practices against each other if its ideas were to become reality. the junilist is a determined opponent of common civil and criminal law at eu level. the consequences of any approval of the proposal are, among other things, that swedish citizens will lose their unconditional right (taking into account the six-month rule) to divorce. an example of this is the following: spouses who are swedish citizens take up residence in malta. one of the spouses then moves back to sweden and files for divorce with a swedish court. under current swedish law, the spouse can obtain a divorce under swedish law. the proposal, however, implies that the law of malta should apply instead. this may result in no divorce being granted at all, since divorce is prohibited not only in malta but in a number of other european countries. the swedish citizen thus loses both the maintenance of his children and the right to half of the spouses' joint assets. this is totally unacceptable in the junilista's view. legislation in this area expresses national, religious and social values. these are not to be run over by the eu. as usual, we find that subsidiarity and pluralism are only in the talk, while the reality is a ruthless pursuit of equality.
in writing. the council of europe convention on action against trafficking in human beings has already been signed by 30 states. the convention has been ratified by, among others, moldova and romania, and ratification is under way in sweden. the convention applies to all forms of trafficking in human beings, whether national or international, or linked to organised crime. the convention thus complements the provisions of the united nations convention against organised crime. the junilist believes that the eu should respect the national and international agreements which its member states have already signed. we believe that the conclusion of legally binding agreements is a matter for the national parliaments of each country. we support the fight against trafficking in human beings and we congratulate the governments of romania and moldova for their courage in ratifying this hugely important convention. we therefore consider that the eu's call is not necessary. the sovereign nations of europe have already taken up the fight among themselves without supranational interference.
in writing. the eu's communication policy must not be about trying to sell a united states of europe to the citizens. the eu institutions must only provide accurate and factual business and financial reports and other information, such as exchange projects in higher education. nor is it the case that the more knowledge an individual voter has, the more he or she is in favour of the creation of a european superstate. the view of intergovernmental or supranational cooperation for the eu is based on values and not on expertise. we also do not believe that eu parties should be initiated and financed from brussels to initiate a dialogue with the electorate on eu issues. political interest in eu issues must be built up from below in political parties and organisations. the report also stresses that communication should be based on initiatives promoted through communication channels such as cultural programmes (for example, the awarding of literature or film prizes), sports events, etc. we reiterate our position that such ways of promoting the eu breathe contempt for the citizens. this is not the way the union is supposed to inform people of its existence and its activities.
mr president, i would like to thank you. iran is a nation with thousands of years of cultural history, a country with great wealth and much to offer its people and the world. but what does the current regime offer its people? repression, imprisonment, unemployment and censorship! president ahmadinejad took office on 3 august 2005, and since then the situation has worsened. the current government offers its people cruel corporal punishment. 600 children will join their mothers in prison. prisoners may have three square meters to move around, but they are lucky compared to those on death row. iran even manages to execute more than the united states. in the last 12 months, 111 death sentences have been carried out. the most flagrant violation of the convention on the rights of the child, to which iran is a signatory, is the death penalty for minors. a regime that does not allow a young person to repent and improve has no future, it will fall. iran has great potential, but if the regime pursues the persecution of academics, journalists and political activists instead of exploiting their potential, the country and its people will continue to suffer. iran has had many hands extended to it. the european parliament gives another one today: release the political prisoners, journalists and representatives of minorities mentioned in today's resolution. these are not big demands, but if they are implemented they can pave the way for a better dialogue. why is the regime so afraid of women? let them participate, be elected, work, live and act on the same terms as men. the potential for development in society is doubled. there is hope. already today, un delegations are being allowed to inspect various activities, and some political prisoners have been released. the new government must, however, seize the opportunity to choose the path that opens up the right of women to democracy and to the outside world. it's the future!
for the ind/dem group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. there is no respect for the principle of subsidiarity in this house. there is never any proposal to transfer political power back from the eu to the member states. however, there are sound economic arguments for increased eu action in research. a pure market economy entails too little research because the returns of research efforts accrue to everyone while the costs have to be borne by individual firms. that is why it pays for taxpayers to fund basic research. if more countries join together, it will be even more socioeconomically profitable because a greater share of the proceeds will then go back to the taxpayers. it is a great idea to set up the european research council and to facilitate the mobility of researchers within the eu. but we must say no to the bureaucratic and the bureaucratic. what is needed is more research resources, transparency and free movement. this is a debate on research governance, but it gives greater scope to politicians, bureaucrats and new eu institutions. finally, let me remind you of the grotesque allocation of resources. the seventh framework programme accounts for one-seventh of the cost of the eu's agricultural policy. research funding is vital for the future of europe. agricultural policies, on the other hand, waste resources, suck out europe's consumers and worsen world poverty. doubling research funding, halving agricultural subsidies and cutting the eu levy by 40% is a mercy to be quietly begged for.
mr president, i do not want to spoil the party here tonight. i think we can be proud of what we have achieved – the european research council, the search for excellence in different programmes – but i think it is necessary to state tonight that this is not enough. it is not enough if we compare it with what we should be able to do at european level, and it is important for the representatives of the council as well as for you, commissioner, to remember that and to underline that we cannot wait for another seven years to step up the efforts we need in order to keep up with other parts of the world if we are to be the world’s leading knowledge economy. as a shadow rapporteur i am proud of what we have achieved from our side. we wanted to make it easier for smes to have access to research programmes, and we have achieved that. we wanted to have better funding for smes, and we have achieved that. we wanted to ensure that universities were able to get into the projects without losing money through indirect costs, and we have achieved that. we also wanted less bureaucracy, and we have achieved that. we wanted to free up opportunities to use intellectual property rights, and we have achieved that. we are happy about what we have achieved, but that demonstrates that we need to take further steps.
the junilista believes that it is totally unacceptable that people with disabilities are faced with prejudice, negative discrimination and limited opportunities. this is not in keeping with our view of the eu as a union of values. the regulation of the european parliament and of the council of 5 july 2006 is therefore a success. it clarifies something that should be taken for granted, not least because we have a single internal market, namely that disabled people should have the same rights as other citizens when travelling by air. sweden is a pioneer in the field of disability rights. a far-reaching eu legislation, with detailed views on how disabled people should be treated, could have called into question the successful swedish reforms of the 1970s and 1980s. let us therefore avoid detailed regulation and instead clearly state that society, industry and the public in general must treat disabled people on an equal footing with other citizens. in conclusion, let us devote ourselves to self-criticism. the eu institutions are presenting reports on disability rights, but at the same time they cannot ensure that all eu buildings are fully adapted to the needs of disabled people. the european union's websites and documents are also not accessible in formats suitable for disabled people, especially the visually impaired. when will the quaestors of the european parliament and the relevant eu authorities act on these pressing and immediate issues?

in writing. junilista has repeatedly pointed out that the eu needs to be enlarged very urgently, but that this can only happen when the candidate countries are ready for it, i.e. when they meet all the accession criteria. unfortunately, we have recently seen an unfortunate development, where negotiations have started without the candidate countries being reasonably close to fulfilling the conditions for membership. both bulgaria and romania face major problems, particularly in the areas of the rule of law and corruption. the inclusion of these countries as member states at this stage is therefore contrary to eu principles. these countries would have needed to develop much more before membership became a reality, a process which could take several years. however, junilista believes that once a country becomes a member of the eu, the same rules apply as for the other member states and their citizens. the eu shall have a single labour market with free movement of workers. however, the accession treaties provide for the right of each member state to introduce transitional rules in this area for a limited period of time, and this must be respected. however, we do not believe that sweden should exercise this right. we believe that bulgaria and romania are not ready to join in 2007 and we have therefore voted against the two reports.

for the verts/ale group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. the original purpose of the reach was to protect people and the environment. the positive impact of knowledge and development of better use of chemicals would also bring economic benefits. the lancet published a report in november showing that 200 common chemicals cause brain damage, concentration problems, behavioral problems and lower intelligence. how can we have a society based on knowledge, innovation and development when we unnecessarily allow chemicals that lower intelligence and make it difficult for us to concentrate? ladies and gentlemen, we have had many long meetings, and it has been confirmed time and again that a qualified majority in this parliament takes it for granted that consumer products containing dangerous substances should always be replaced by less dangerous alternatives when they are available. we have repeatedly agreed that chemical safety reports should be introduced for all low-volume chemicals. we have repeatedly stressed the importance of openness and transparency and laughed at the absurdity of the secret board of directors, which is to take important decisions on the future of chemicals, having secret financial interests. we have taken it for granted that companies should be responsible for their products and have also taken decisions which would give small businesses a fair chance. but now, in the hour of need, now at the eleventh hour, a majority of you have chosen to ignore these goals and instead run into the precarious arms of the german chemical industry. despite the fact that the environmental committee called for major improvements to reach, the last trilateral meeting was all about deterioration. it was the ppe-de group that dealt with the chemical industry's affairs, but why did the others accept that game? mr president, i would like to thank you. the vote on wednesday marks the end of a long process with important contributions from many quarters. i think we should pay attention to commissioner margot wallström, who put forward the proposal, and her democratic innovation with the big internet consultation, which did an enormous amount to improve the reach proposal itself. we must pay attention to the members who have worked hard for many nights and with whom we have had tough discussions, langen in the committee on industry, the rapporteur nassauer and others, not least the rapporteur-general guido sacconi, and the commissioners, both günter verheugen and dimas, who have done a great deal to prepare this proposal for the vote. and yet i stand here with very mixed feelings. if i were to sum up, i could say proud but not satisfied, to use a swedish expression that has been used in recent years. i am proud to have been part of this work as rapporteur on the committee on industry. so i started with three themes. firstly, the proposal must be strengthened in relation to environmental components, in particular in relation to substitution. secondly, the proposal needs to be clarified. there was a tremendous amount of ambiguity and overlap with other legislation and we have now removed 'paper and pulp' and 'minerals and ores', which are some important areas that have no place in this proposal. we've also simplified reach, which has been a huge benefit to small businesses. the proposal for one registration per substance, 'one substance one registration', i think will mean a great deal in the future. my starting point was therefore that these three steps - reinforcing, clarifying and simplifying - were good for european industry, for european consumers and for european citizens. the starting point of having a strong policy that combines market considerations with environmental considerations is, i believe, a very important success factor. i am disappointed in three points. firstly, i think we could have used the opportunity to provide consumers with clearer information to meet increasing environmental and consumer demands. it would have been possible, because we've done the work and we've taken the cost. the compromise that now exists does not take full advantage of those advantages, and i find that very sad. i would like to ask how the commission and the council are going to solve the 0.1% requirement, i.e. whether the car is to be considered partly dangerous or the dangerous component in the car is to be considered partly dangerous. it's important to clarify. the requirement for substitution is another issue. why couldn't we have gone one step further with the substances we call cmrs, which are both carcinogenic, mutagenic and reproductive? my third question concerns the principle of responsibility, the 'duty of care'. it has been in european tort law since roman times and is not a new concept. i think it would have been good not to have it just as a statement of principle in the reasons, but to have it in the legal text. unfortunately, we have not yet reached the end of these points. i have a fourth question, namely that it is important that the developing countries now have the chance to use this information so that it does not create a barrier to trade. so to sum up, proud but not satisfied. the big job remains, and to quote an american speaker a few weeks ago: we didn't cross the rubicon to sit and fish. ladies and gentlemen, the big job is left after the vote.

in writing. the junilist believes that it is essential that consumer protection in the eu is adequate. however, the financing of the measures to be taken is primarily a national matter. we therefore consider that the budget line of eur 156.8 million for the current consumer policy programme is not justified. we are also strongly critical of the proposal to provide financial support for the development of master courses in consumer affairs within the framework of an integrated european degree. the eu institutions are not, of course, to control the type of training provided in the member states or to finance it. we should therefore have voted against this report.
in writing. the junilist is for cross-border police cooperation. it is necessary to deal with international crime today, but this has been successfully done for decades by the international police agency interpol. however, junilistan is very sceptical about the fact that law enforcement is conducted at a supranational level. we are in favour of intergovernmental cooperation and against common legislation. the commission proposes the creation of asset recovery offices in the member states, which are directly subject to ec legislation and administration. this means further interference with the legal systems of the national member states and a weakening of interpol's respected position. the junilista therefore votes against the report as a whole.
in writing. the junilists consider that transboundary animal diseases are an important area of cooperation within the union, but routine financing of the control of animal diseases is not a matter for the union. we would point out that it is ultimately the responsibility of the member states to combat animal diseases and zoonoses within their territory, both in terms of measures and in terms of finances. we therefore particularly oppose amendment no 6 by the committee on agriculture, which would allow member states to submit national programmes financed by the eu, depending on their particular circumstances. we are voting against the report as a whole, on the assumption that the european union will have to take on a major financial responsibility for the fight against animal diseases. the union's treasury is not a cornucopia of money which can finance measures in the agricultural sector all the time. sweden, for example, has a long and successful history of fighting salmonella within its borders and is proof that member states can do this themselves without the need for european union funding.
madam president, i would like to thank you. the harmful effects of alcohol cost the eu €125 billion a year. this is equivalent to 1.3% of eu gdp. alcohol is not just any product, and therefore television advertising for alcohol must be restricted. children who watch tv during the day should not be exposed to alcohol advertising. i therefore hope that parliament will support amendment no 169 tomorrow. the directive we are discussing here is a minimum directive. the idea is that countries should be able to have more restrictive advertising rules, but in practice this is not possible. in sweden, for example, we have banned advertising to children, but tv 3 and channel 5 can still circumvent these rules by broadcasting from the uk. it is unreasonable broadcasts aimed at a swedish audience should follow swedish rules in the same way that broadcasts aimed at a british audience should follow british rules. i therefore hope that parliament will vote in favour of the amendments which give the receiving country the possibility of influencing the advertising content of the broadcasts, including nos 246 and 153 tomorrow.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i recognize that we need a new regulation of the directive because of the developments that have taken place in the audiovisual field, not least technologically, over the last 20 years. however, there are negative elements both in the commission proposal and in the amendments to the directive. the directive risks, in several respects, leading to a deterioration in consumer protection in general and in the impact of television on minors in particular. just as technological advances have advanced, so the amount and variety of advertising has increased over the past two decades. advertising is everywhere today, even directed at minors and children. there must be a balance between the amount of advertising allowed and the groups of consumers to which advertising may be directed. although this is a mini-directive, many of us socialists are too strict about all advertising in children's programmes and advertising directed at children. we understand why the commission has banned advertising in religious programmes, but we question why those who watch this type of programme are considered more important to protect from commercial influence than children. children cannot distinguish advertising from other programmes and therefore cannot understand the underlying interests underlying the advertising. i, and many others, are against advertising directed at children.
we see here a conflict of objectives in a difficult question in modern technology between the interests of freedom versus the interests of protection. on the one hand, it is difficult to censor programmes broadcast from other member states, but on the other hand we believe that each member state must have its own cultural and moral autonomy in matters relating to alcohol and tobacco advertising, advertising directed at children, rules on discrimination and pornography, and so on. each member state must be able to legislate in these areas. technological developments in high-speed broadband internet and mobile telephony make possible tv-like media services where the moral code is difficult to monitor. but here too it is essential that member states, if they so wish, can legislate to regulate child pornography and copyright. the matter is undoubtedly very complex, but we believe that sweden's position in the council should have been better heard at the november council meeting. we do, however, consider that the proposal of the committee on culture is better than the council's and therefore support this report.
for the ind/dem group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. there is a constant stream of exhortations from various eu institutions to member states on the importance of reducing public spending. at the same time, this house is constantly demanding increased expenditure at eu level. this is baroque. member states' public spending is directed at schools, health care, research, infrastructure and support for vulnerable groups in society. the bulk of eu spending goes to insane agricultural policies, misguided structural funds and funding of eu institutions that should have been shut down long ago. the expenditure of the member states is subject to continuous democratic scrutiny. officials who are guilty of inefficiency, negligence, fraud or corruption are not granted immunity and are often dismissed. politicians who fail to meet the citizens' demands for honesty and efficiency are removed in democratic elections. the court of auditors is the body responsible for auditing eu expenditure, but it has not yet been able to issue a clean audit report. when olaf detects crimes, they are not prosecuted. this chamber grants a general discharge regardless of how eu budget funds are used, and politicians never lose elections in their home country because of mismanagement of eu money. in short, the member states have some democratic and effective control over the use of taxpayers' money, but the eu institutions and this house do not.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to begin by thanking the rapporteur, mrs bachelot-narquin, for her excellent work, but also the shadow rapporteurs who have come in, mr schroedter and others. i would also like to thank the council, which is not present, for listening and the commission for being constructive during the trialogue. others have spoken of the improvements made, for example in cofinancing, small labour markets and so on. there were a lot of improvements. i would like to stress what mrs bachelot-narquin said, that this does not solve all the problems, that it is a european added value, that it does not take away from the member states, the regions or the companies their responsibility to face globalisation. this is an added value. do not think that this fund will solve all the problems, but the bulk of them must be solved in the member states. but it's an important contribution. secondly, it is not a question of supporting enterprises or supporting specific regions, we have other support for that. this is about reintegrating workers who have been unduly affected by globalisation so that they can re-enter the labour market as quickly as possible. fortunately, this will come into effect as early as 1 january. i want to thank everyone for their cooperation.
for the gue/ngl group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. a big thank you to all those involved who have worked hard and dedicated and a special thank you to the greens and sartori for this institute now finally being set up and starting its important work. it is an important milestone for all of us who work for women and men in an equal society. it is also a milestone in strengthening children's rights, because the situation of children is largely affected by the rights of mothers and women. but as much as i am happy about this step towards establishing an equality institute, i am equally disappointed by the fact that in 2006 a special institute for women's self-evident rights is needed. that we have not come any further in recognizing the right of half of humanity to live their lives on equal terms with the other half of humanity. we talk a lot in the eu about democracy and human rights, but democratic and human rights must also apply to women. it cannot be considered democratic that large sections of the population believe that women can be treated as commodities in a market and that society cannot be responsible for women's right to their own bodies. it is about women's right to be free from abuse and beatings, that political power is still largely the power of men, that women earn less than men for the same work, and so on. i really hope that all of us in the plenary vote will support the establishment of the institute for gender equality as a major and important step towards the future, so that we can really talk about democracy and human rights, equally for everyone, regardless of gender.
mr. speaker, and all the members who are still awake! equality between women and men is one of the fundamental principles of the eu and one of its central tasks. in all its activities, the eu shall seek to eliminate inequalities between men and women. however, despite much progress in the area of gender equality, several evaluations show that positive developments are very slow. the eu lacks the capacity to compile analyses and to develop good solutions in the field of gender mainstreaming and gender equality. by creating a european institute for gender equality, the eu and the member states are highlighting the high priority of the issue and the political will to do more to tackle the current inequalities. today, as a social democrat mep, i am very proud that european cooperation is being used to further the fight against gender discrimination. it is now important that all the member states make use of the institute and that statistics and information are submitted. this is a very big responsibility for the member states. finally, i am pleased that a new member state, lithuania and the city of vilnius, are receiving this institute. i do not think that the principle of proximity is relevant here, since all the member states of the european union are central. i wish them well and i thank the rapporteurs lissy gröner and sartori and the commission.
on behalf of the gue/ngl group. – mr president, i ride a bicycle – that is enough for me! herr talman! tack till föredraganden groote för ett bra arbete. vi i gue/ngl och de gröna vill dock göra några grundläggande förbättringar. detta på tre punkter. vi vill att denna lagstiftning ska träda i kraft tidigare än den kompromiss som har gjorts med rådet. både miljön och de europeiska konsumenterna skulle vinna på det. vi vill också ha hårdare utsläppskrav för dieselbilar och drivmedelsneutralitet för diesel och bensin. ett tredje viktigt ändringsförslag är stadsjeeparna – vi vill inte ha några undantag för dem. avslutningsvis vill jag också plädera för johannes bloklands ändringsförslag, som går ut på att medlemsländerna ska kunna ha hårdare lagstiftning på området. det borde vara en självklarhet när vi fattar beslut, men tyvärr är det inte så. i det fallet bifall även till ind/dem.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to thank elmar brok and alexander stubb for the debate on the enlargement process and their reports. i would like to begin by commenting on mr brok's thoughts in point f, that enlargement must not undermine the political character of the project. i come from sweden, which has clearly stated in all studies that continued enlargement is one of the most important issues for the eu. my concern today is that we are not speaking clearly about further enlargement and turkey's future membership. many members of parliament are against turkey's membership, as was already clear when the ppe-de called for a closed vote on turkey's membership negotiations. it was too many people like me who thought it was unthinkable that politicians should not dare to stand up for their views in front of their electorate and the citizens of europe. on several occasions during the past year, views have been expressed which have led me to wonder whether there is any will to continue enlargement, even if we resolve the key issues high on our agenda. we need a new constitution, even if there are more members, for modern decision-making. we need a reformed agricultural policy for the new jobs of the future and a sustainable europe. we need a bigger budget for future funding. i wonder what mr brok means when he says in paragraph 11 that the union can only function if all its members share a common set of values based on a european identity. am i to take that as closing the doors to turkey? my group, the pse, has tabled amendment no 29, which we call on everyone to support. we are in full agreement that turkey must meet the demands we made at the outset on cyprus, on the kurds, on human rights and on all the others. but let us be clear and separate the different agendas.
mr president, mr commissioner, i would like to thank you for your report. the eu enlargement process is a great success, from 6 countries to 27. we have seen the shape of a new europe. to start hesitating now and talk about 'tiredness' of continuing enlargement would be going in the wrong direction. of course, the criteria of democracy, human rights and the proper functioning of the rule of law must be met. we have also seen the power with which the former eastern and central europe has changed. it is true that the eu needs new forms of decision-making, but the eu must not create new conditions for the countries that are now seeking membership. mr commissioner, i am personally concerned about the forces within the european union which are trying to make it difficult for turkey to become a member of the union in the future. europe as a whole must be our vision of the future - including turkey.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the rapporteur said that if these amendments were accepted, there would be a compromise. however, we do not know. if we approve the amendments, the council may well accept them, but we will not know until coreper has taken a decision. there is therefore no obvious link between accepting the amendments and conciliation. in writing. we choose to support the compromise proposed as it represents a clear improvement on the current chemicals legislation. furthermore, we consider that a settlement would have been risky, as we believe that it would have likely resulted in a weaker reach.
in writing. in today's vote on the second reading of reach, i abstained. i cannot support the weakening of parliament's position, which the environment committee adopted in october, as the council has forced it to do. my criticism of the settlement is based on three main points: - the substitution principle does not cover all chemicals, and many chemicals will continue to be exempted from the substitution requirement, including carcinogens, provided they are under 'adequate control'. - the right to information is weakened by the fact that fewer chemicals are covered by the information requirement, which will apply in higher concentrations than the ep environment committee would have wished. - the principle of liability is not written into the law itself but is moved to the preamble. this is particularly serious for the many chemicals with low or no information requirements. my abstention is not a failure but a sign of my dissatisfaction with the council's position. i do not wish to criticise the rapporteur, mr sacconi, but to congratulate him on his long and excellent work on a difficult subject. today's vote paves the way for reach to enter into force by the end of 2007. now, too, my work and that of many others to further improve the law begins.
in writing. junilista is committed to limiting eu cooperation to genuinely crossborder issues. these include the internal market and a number of environmental issues. we are convinced that a strong chemicals directive represents a long-term competitive advantage for swedish and european industry. we are therefore critical of the watered-down compromise proposed by the ppe-de, pse and alde groups. the compromise means that the principle of substitution is practically abolished. nor is there a legally binding principle of liability which makes clear that the burden of responsibility should lie with the undertakings rather than with the authorities of the member states. the requirement that member states should be able to have more far-reaching chemicals legislation has also not been welcomed. the compromise may be a victory for the old-fashioned part of the chemical industry, but it is a loss for modern and progressive companies, for public health and for our common environment. we have voted against the majority compromise on the basis of the positions set out above. we have chosen to support the more environmentally friendly option, i.e. the compromise package put forward by the gue/ngl and greens/efa groups.
in writing. the junilist believes that the whole idea of the european year is unnecessary and not something that the eu taxpayers should be funding. the allocation of eur 10 million for the european year of intercultural dialogue is totally unacceptable. the benefits of this are difficult, perhaps even impossible, to see. we are voting against the amendment in the report, in line with our previous position, which was to vote against the report at first reading.
in writing. we voted against the report on the proposal for a directive on audiovisual media services. common eu rules on television and other audiovisual media are needed in an increasingly borderless europe. we believe, however, that the directive must guarantee a high level of consumer protection, particularly in relation to advertising aimed at children and advertising of alcoholic beverages. in addition, the recipient country should be able to influence the content of the programmes specifically targeted to it. the result of the vote means that the rules on advertising have been liberalized too much. in addition, our swedish ban on advertising to children and alcohol is now at risk of being undermined, which is unacceptable.
in writing. the junilista rejects the commission's proposal. the council dealt with the matter in november before the european parliament's first reading in december. a compromise was agreed, but it was not supported by seven countries, including sweden. the so-called country of origin principle, which means that tv companies must follow the rules of the country from which they broadcast, regardless of the country in which the programmes are subsequently shown, is questioned by sweden (and junilistan), as it causes problems for example for tv 3 and kanal 5 to broadcast from the uk where they are subject to british legislation and not swedish. the council has also called on the commission to take action against the use of the term 'competition' in the context of the european union's competition policy. the committee on culture has proposed an amendment concerning cases where a media service provider has established itself in the member state of jurisdiction 'in order to circumvent the stricter rules in the fields' coordinated by the directive. however, we would have liked to see the case further prepared in order to have a stronger wording. sweden and junilistan consider that both advertising directed at children and alcohol advertising should be completely banned. we have tried to vote as far as possible for an improvement of the directive in accordance with the guidelines set out above.
in writing. the increased competition for businesses and the increased opportunities for new, cheaper and better products for consumers resulting from globalisation place new demands on flexibility, innovation and adaptability. it is by striving to be better, more efficient and faster that we meet the new demands and opportunities of globalisation. the effect of the globalisation fund already in place will be the opposite. it slows down innovation and delays adaptation without taking account of the opportunities offered by globalisation.
in writing. we are very critical of the thinking behind the creation of a globalisation fund. the proposal starts from the premise that globalisation as such is a problem. however, we believe that globalisation is an opportunity, not least for poor developing countries, provided that major economic players, such as the eu and the us, reform their respective protectionist trade policy positions within the wto. eu member states are able to take national measures to assist sectors which they consider to be in need of financial support. a special eu fund is created to protect against arbitrariness, inefficiency, bureaucracy and unjustified expenditure. how should the commission assess, in a relevant manner, whether a sector has been negatively affected by globalisation? we have voted against the report in question on the basis of the views expressed above.
in writing. i believe that globalisation has had a positive effect on the development of the world and has served us well. today we voted on a report on a european fund for the effects of globalisation. the report contains a lot of critical writing on these very effects. the change in world trade patterns has not had the negative effects that the report claims. my attitude towards globalisation and the increasing exchange of people, goods, services and capital is positive. i believe that a world with open borders, where democracy and human rights are the values that govern the interaction between people as well as between countries, is worth striving for. globalisation has created, and continues to create, great opportunities to free people from oppression and misery, to increase prosperity and to provide greater conditions for democracy. that is why i have decided today to vote against the report.
in writing. we would have liked a tighter timetable for the introduction of euro 5 and lower limits for nitrogen dioxide emissions in euro 6. however, we voted in favour of the compromise with the council of ministers because we believe that it is a good compromise and that an extension of the process would not lead to better rules but only to a postponement of the timetable for implementation.
in writing. despite being among the richest countries in the world, there are good reasons for supporting the international fund for ireland. member states wishing to contribute to this fund may do so by decision of their respective national parliaments. this procedure is more democratic and also means that member states' financial resources are not unnecessarily diverted through the eu budget. we have therefore voted against the report in question.
in writing. we voted against amendment no 10. we swedish social democrats in the european parliament interpret the amendment as a way of giving member states who so wish the possibility of retaining part of the production aid for bananas. as we are consistently working for the decoupling of agricultural aid, this is unacceptable. production aid makes it impossible for banana producers from non-eu countries to compete on fair terms. we are also critical of the proposal as a whole which will lead to increased expenditure. we believe that a reform of the banana market organisation should lead to budgetary savings.
in writing. we have voted against this report because it is in line with the specific interests of european banana growers. we firmly reject amendment no 10 in the report on the special treatment of aid for cotton, olive oil, raw tobacco, hops and bananas. if individual member states want to favour banana cultivation in their territory for regional policy reasons, that is up to them, taking account of international trade agreements and eu rules, of course. however, it is then up to the individual member state to finance this aid without the involvement of the european union.
in writing. the junilists are in principle in favour of enlargement. however, it is essential that the countries wishing to become members meet the requirements before joining. the countries now being considered for possible future accession have a long way to go. it would be wrong for the present member states and the candidate countries to set the date of accession to the eu at this stage. this process should therefore be allowed to mature slowly. the report states, inter alia, that the treaty of nice is not a sufficient basis for future enlargements. the implication is that this is a call for a new constitution, despite the fact that the people of france and the netherlands have voted against it in referendums. the rapporteur also talks about the eu's 'integration capacity' and believes that it must be made clear that this should in no way be confused with the public's perception of the consequences of further enlargements. we can see here a certain contempt for basic democratic principles. if a majority of citizens think that the eu should not be enlarged, this should be heard by the elected representatives. this is a clear example of the contempt for citizenship that is growing in this parliament. since future enlargements are being used as an argument for a future constitution, we have voted against the report.
in writing. the junilista does not share the rapporteur's view that a more federal eu must be created in order to continue the enlargement process. on the contrary, we believe that it is of the utmost importance that political decisions which are to apply to the whole of the union have a good basis in the council of ministers. we therefore oppose the extension of the areas in which decisions can be taken by qualified majority. we also believe that it is of the utmost importance that each member state is represented in the commission in order to give its work credibility in all the member states. nor do we see how the enlargement process would benefit from the creation of a post of foreign minister. the junist party members have therefore voted against the report as a whole.
president of the council. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, data protection issues have been at the heart of finland's presidency. on the other hand, the presidency has faced challenges that are relevant and foreseeable, such as the implementation of the third pillar data protection framework decision. on the other hand, there have been situations in which the presidency has had to respond quickly and when circumstances demanded it. examples of the latter are the negotiation of a new agreement with the united states on passenger name record (pnr) data and the issue of bank transfer data managed by swift. as the presidency, finland is aware that the european parliament has on several occasions expressed its strong support for the third pillar data protection framework decision and its rapid adoption. the proposal for a framework decision was one of the priorities of our presidency in the field of justice and home affairs. our ambitious objective was to reach a political agreement on the framework decision during the finnish presidency. this autumn, considerable time and effort has been spent on advancing the proposal for a framework decision. the first reading of the framework decision was completed in our session, and the second and third readings were held. in addition, the article 36 committee and the permanent representatives committee were invited on several occasions to examine specific issues related to this matter. despite these efforts, the timetable proved too tight. it is clear that agreement will no longer be reached during the finnish presidency, and that the examination of the framework decision will be transferred to the german presidency. the proposal for a framework decision is a very broad and complex body whose implications for police and judicial cooperation and data protection require careful consideration. the difficulty of the matter is illustrated by the fact that, despite much progress, some key issues remain open. for example, questions of scope are still unresolved. the main open question is whether the data protection principles of the framework decision should also apply to the processing of data within the member states or whether the scope should be limited to so-called cross-border data, as some member states advocate. the presidency was also tasked in the autumn with negotiating a new agreement on the transfer of passenger name records (pnr) to the us authorities. the agreement resulting from the negotiations is essentially the same as the 2004 agreement. the operating conditions of the airlines remain in place and they can continue to transfer pnr data to the united states. the united states, for its part, has committed itself to ensuring an adequate level of data protection in the future. the agreement, signed in mid-october, is temporary and runs until the end of july 2007. the aim is to complete a new, more permanent pnr transfer arrangement by then. negotiations on this subject, which are expected to be difficult, will be launched as soon as possible. a new negotiating mandate is currently being prepared. the importance of the new pnr agreement to be negotiated in 2007 is further underlined by the recent disclosure of information concerning the so-called automatic targeting system (ats) operated by the us authorities. this system combines data from a variety of sources, including pnr data of air passengers arriving in the united states. the aim is to facilitate the preparation of risk assessments for passengers. the existence of the ats has been known to the council and the commission since the first joint review of the application of the pnr agreement in september 2005. however, the u.s. department of homeland security did not release the description of the system until november 2 of this year, when it was published in the u.s. government gazette. the compatibility of the existing and future pnr contract with the ats system is currently being examined, in particular as regards the retention period, the transfer and the appropriateness of the data. the presidency and the commission have sent the us authorities an official enquiry on the matter. the results of this will be reported to the european parliament in due course.
vice-president of the commission. mr president, as you know very well, on 19 october the european union and the united states concluded an agreement for the processing and transfer of passenger name record data by air carriers to the united states department of homeland security. the government of the united states confirmed a set of undertakings which guarantee the protection and security of pnr data. against this background, concern has been expressed recently following information published last month by the department of homeland security on the so-called automated targeting system. this is a security-screening system making a risk assessment of international travellers, relying among other things on pnr data. the information published by the homeland security department reveals a significant difference between the way in which pnr data are handled within the automated targeting system and a stricter regime for european pnr data according to undertakings given by the department of homeland security. as the president-in-office has just said, we have today sent a letter to the united states government to request formal confirmation that the way eu pnr data are handled in the ats is the one described in the undertakings. the current eu-us agreement on pnr data will expire in july 2007. at the beginning of 2007 the commission will recommend the council to mandate the presidency, assisted by the commission, to negotiate a new pnr agreement with the united states. i am sure that any new agreement will provide for a high level of data protection for all pnr data transferred under the agreement, while protecting the security of citizens. i will keep the european parliament informed on the preparation of the draft mandate, which i expect to get before the end of march 2007, otherwise taking the position that travellers must be informed when their pnr data may be transferred to competent authorities of third countries. the department of homeland security undertaking expressly acknowledged this. we need an international agreement with the support of the public on both sides of the atlantic and of the democratic representatives of the peoples. i have often said that there is an important balance to be struck between measures to ensure security, on the one hand, and the protection of non-negotiable fundamental rights, on the other. the commission, assisting the presidency in the negotiation of future pnr agreements with third countries, will ensure that security issues are properly addressed through the transfer and appropriate use of pnr data, while protecting personal data as guaranteed by article 8 of the charter. finally, a high level contact group was set up at the eu-us ministerial troika meeting on 6 november 2006 to discuss information sharing and protection of personal data for law enforcement purposes. there is a clear need on both sides of the atlantic to work more closely together on these issues. i would also be in favour of close and continuous contacts between this new contact group and both the european parliament and the congress of the united states. we need a broader perspective and a long-term vision to tackle, together with the united states, the terrorist threat, while protecting the fundamental rights of individuals without putting them at risk. more generally, on a subject just mentioned by the president-in-office, i also remain firmly committed to encouraging and continuing to encourage the council to approve as soon as possible the proposal i made 15 months ago on data protection under the third pillar. i count on the support of the incoming german presidency on that. we face a common problem and threat. terrorism will continue to exist in the coming months and years. only a very solid strategy and balanced cooperation with our main international, transatlantic partner will make it possible to reduce if not eliminate this modern form of totalitarianism against democracy.
the ppe group is in favour of the report. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. we in the ppe-de group support the european parliament's recommendation by sophia in 't veld on the use of passenger data. we also prefer the canadian agreement to the american one. we also hope for improved transatlantic cooperation and believe in the will of the fratini in this respect. however, we are not taking part in the debate to comment on a newspaper article but to push for council decisions on data protection rules, even when they have consequences for the ministers of justice in the third pillar and not just when they restrict the rights of companies as now in the first pillar. fundamental rights must provide for the protection of citizens, regardless of the structure of the treaty. data protection is enshrined in the charter of human rights of the european union and in the council of europe convention for the protection of human rights and fundamental freedoms, which also establishes the right to privacy. it is also part of the principles supported by all three eu institutions and all member states. all member states have data protection legislation and a data protection authority. we need all this at eu level too. parliament and the council have been in agreement on this since september, but nothing has happened. the eu has developed its fight against crime and terrorism. in this case, data protection must be at the level of the case law of the council of europe or, preferably, at the level of the member state with the best data protection. parliament always contributes to the development of the field of justice. the vis, sis and europol are no exception. the institution that opposes data protection risks in practice becoming an inefficient law enforcement agency. it is 'both' and 'both' that apply, not 'either' or 'either'. if the council supports the proportionality and necessity criteria, why does it not say so? are you concerned that the ec court of justice will defend fundamental rights? what legislation is in trouble? when do we get the proposal on data protection in the field of law enforcement? the council has a responsibility to the citizens.
mr president, if we are describing the changes in our societies in the european union as threats, of course the citizens will listen and be afraid. i am surprised to see that my danish colleague mr rasmussen is not here, considering his comments earlier. however, if we describe the changes we are making as a way of meeting the challenges we face – the global challenge and security threats – that is another matter. i believe that one of the responsibilities of the finnish presidency and of the commission is to underline what we have achieved this autumn under the finnish presidency. we have a new seventh framework programme, we have finalised our decision-making with regard to reach and the services directive. that is a change of some magnitude and it is making europe better. we have prepared enlargement for romania and bulgaria and have appointed new commissioners. slovenia will enter the euro cooperation. it is a significant moment in the development of the european union. does that make europe worse? does that make people feel threatened? of course not. it makes europe better for its citizens. that is the situation. if we say that clearly and loudly people will also see the rationality of the changes we are making. that should be the responsibility not only of mr rasmussen but also of the european council and the summit meeting. regarding enlargement, let us say loudly and clearly that we are laying down tough criteria while making it credible that those who fulfil them can be members. the presidency should lay the groundwork for a new policy with russia – firm but open, open but firm. we need russia for energy security but we also need to apply stringent rules and a firm policy regarding democracy and respect for fundamental human rights. that is the way it should be and that is what we should hope for in this final meeting with the finnish presidency. you have done a good job and you can do even better in the coming days.
for the pse group. mr president, mr commissioner, i would like to thank you for your reply. finally, we can begin the journey towards a common european driving licence. unfortunately, we have been forced to wait a maximum of 26 years before all the member states have introduced the common driving licence. that's way too long. i think, like previous speakers, that it will go faster. we must now call on the member states to take this matter seriously and to work towards an early introduction of a common driving licence, which will also be replaced regularly. we must also call on the commission to support the member states in this work, as mr jarzembowski said. this is an important issue for citizens, since it concerns so many people. a majority of citizens in europe have a driving licence. one thing we know for sure is that citizens want to be able to drive in their own country and in other countries with ease and to be able to use their driving licence without the administrative hassle. however, citizens also want those who drive on the roads to have a good education. those who commit crimes or break rules should also be able to be punished for them, even if they come from another country. the new common driving licence will eventually meet all these requirements. of course, it has to go faster than 26 years. as has already been pointed out, this will promote road safety, but at the same time it will prevent social dumping, so that those who want to take advantage of the fact that there are fake driving licences in circulation will not be able to benefit economically from it in the future. i would also like to end by thanking all my colleagues for the cooperation we have had. we have been working on this issue for a long time, and many of us have been involved in the debate. in conclusion, i would like to add that i think it is good that motorcyclists are allowed to drive motorcycles with a phased access. i think it's good for road safety.
draftsman of the opinion of the committee on development. – madam president, with one minute of speaking time i can only make two points. first, biofuels are indeed very important, but they have to be produced in the most efficient way. biofuel production in tropical countries is much more efficient than in europe. instead of benefiting fully from that, we seem to be ready to put a levy on such imports. if we can import oil and gas from saudi arabia without levies, why are there levies on biofuels? apart from the efficiency argument, we are also depriving the low-income countries of much-needed income. secondly, emissions in china and india are expanding very rapidly. that is why, in its opinion, the committee on development has suggested that our union should establish a proactive strategy of technology cooperation with these countries, offering financial support to cover some of the extra costs for the most efficient technologies possible. amendment 5 suggests replacing ‘financial’ with ‘technical’. we must be more ambitious than that, otherwise emissions from these countries will overwhelm us in the future. therefore, i urge colleagues to vote against amendment 5.
on behalf of the alde group. – madam president, i should like to thank the rapporteur and my colleagues for their cooperation. a prosperous, competitive and sustainable future for europe will require a major shift in energy policy. today we have problems with production, distribution, consumers’ rights as well as global warming, stability and security. the challenge and urgent development of a new european energy policy is an opportunity for european citizens, european industry and the environment. therefore i welcome an ambitious energy agenda from the commission next year. i underline the importance of the message from parliament today. i therefore ask my colleagues to support the outcome of the vote in the committee on industry, research and energy, where we succeeded in combining an enforced market solution, enhancing market entry for new players, more transparency and consumers’ rights to information, with a clear political signal on long-term international and binding targets on renewable energy and co2 reduction. moreover, i should like to emphasise the importance of including the distributor and consumer aspects to the current production concentration and to stress that existing technologies such as combined heat and power, trigeneration and district heating must be used to a much larger extent, reducing and making more efficient the energy we use. energy policy should not be considered as an isolated issue. it is vital to develop fp7 as well as the reform of the cap in line with the needs of an increased indigenous european energy production. parliament will support current structures of cooperation between member states and the commission’s firm conviction to further develop them. however, we are not in favour of new agencies causing a financial, as well as a bureaucratic, burden on the union. i should like to express my strong support for commissioner kroes’ ambition to make sure competition legislation is not violated, in order to avoid abuse of market power. experiences are like stars: you see them first towards sunset. the eu has the possibility to seize the opportunity to greatly improve the energy market, solve environmental problems and create new jobs. let us take that opportunity.
for the ind/dem group. president. - the debate is closed. it is well known that if a man has only one tool, a hammer, he eventually begins to view every problem as a nail. the reason for this is obvious: a hammer can be used in a useful way only if you use it to hammer nails; hence, you have to imagine that all problems are nails. most of the problems we face in life, however, are not nails. with that attitude, most things go wrong. you can't fix venetian vases with a hammer, and you can't convince a political opponent with a hammer. the majority in this house, however, has one hammer, namely the european union. most of you want to believe, or rather want to make others believe, that the problems which happen to be current at any given moment should, or must, be solved with the help of the european union. this is rarely true. this is true when we look at nuclear safety, radiation protection, nuclear materials in the eu and its neighbourhood. seppänen is on the right track and should have our support, but morgan, who threatens to bureaucratize energy policy to such a degree that it will require a million people to implement it in the short term, is on the wrong track. adaptation to rising energy prices is best done at national level. it is in the competition between countries and companies to find effective solutions to energy problems that creativity can flourish, not by bureaucratising the lives of half a billion people living in economies with very different conditions.
in writing. we want to reiterate our principled view of the eu budget. the junilists believe that the eu budget should be limited to 1.00% of the average gnp of the member states. we have therefore voted against all the increases proposed by the european parliament, while junilista welcomed the few savings proposed by the committee on budgets or by individual members in the form of amendments. there are other unfortunate budget lines, but junilista particularly regrets the large support for the eu's agricultural policy, the cohesion fund, the fisheries sector and the budget lines which support various forms of information campaigns. the junilists also believe that the european parliament's constant travel between strasbourg and brussels must be stopped and that the european economic and social committee and the committee of the regions must be abolished. on 23 october, the european court of auditors found that for the 13th consecutive year, it could not be held responsible for more than a small proportion of the eu budget being used correctly or for its intended purpose. how can the madness continue, year after year, to allocate funds which, to a small extent, can be guaranteed to go to their intended purpose?
in writing. we cannot support this budget for a number of reasons. first of all, out of protest against the fact that a few months ago the court of auditors once again did not approve the eu´s accounts. because the eu cannot successfully combat fraud and inefficiency, it should not increase its budget. secondly, we believe this budget makes the wrong political choices, such as too large a focus on industrial agriculture and foreign policy and military projects, and not enough on environment and social projects. finally, this budget does not adequately change some policies which are simply wasteful, such as mass subsidizing of destruction of wine and continuing subsidies for growing tobacco.
in writing. these reports are permeated by a number of populist proposals which are designed to attack the right of self-determination of the criminal justice system, one of the most fundamental principles of the rule of law. among other things, it advocates a 'true european judge' and a 'true european criminal justice'. the purpose of these proposals is clear. the aim is to build a european superstate, and the proposals are a crucial step in that direction. this is completely unacceptable. the junilist defends the sovereignty of the nation state in legal matters. eu cooperation will be limited to the achievement of a functioning internal market and to dealing with transnational environmental issues. the eu will not have a harmonised legal system. we have therefore voted against these reports in today's vote.
in writing. road safety is a highly important issue of a cross-border nature. the eu driving licence directive could thus provide a tangible added value. however, normally the commission and the european parliament, in their regulatory activities, try to govern the rules of the member states in this area in detail. we believe that the subsidiarity and country of origin principles should be fully applied. this is not the case in the present report, for example with regard to the detailed proposals on driving licences for motorcycles and licences for mopeds. we believe in the principle of mutual recognition and therefore have full confidence in the ability of the member states to take well-considered and wise decisions. the harmonization of driving licence provisions can be achieved without a detailed proposal for a directive, as in the present driving licence directive. we therefore voted in favour of amendment no 6, which proposes that the common position be rejected on the grounds that it is too much regulation without improving road safety.
mr president, i wish to begin by thanking the finnish presidency. as a representative from a neighbouring country, i am proud to be a neighbour to finland these days. you have achieved a lot and have shown that europe is moving. that is important because all the things we have discussed this morning show that we are able to move europe forward. however, that also to some extent highlights the challenges we are facing when we discuss environment policy and greenhouse effects or the need for more competitiveness or the need to face globalisation or the need to do something about the developments in iran. all these discussions result from the fact that we are able to take a higher degree of responsibility than ever before. when we are discussing the new challenges from countries applying to join the union, that is a result of achievements and success, not of the failure, mr farage. if we had failed no one would want to be a member of this union and now they want to be members of this union because of our success. we have more steps to take. a credible union needs a credible common foreign and security policy. happy christmas, mr president.
